  - **A clip path that is one axis-aligned rectangle covering the page clips nothing and is dropped; every other clip stays refused.** The page of an office-suite PDF is clipped to its own size, and Poppler writes that as a `clip-path` in the SVG, which made every Draw page unwritable as DXF or metafile. The geometry reader accepts a clip whose single `<rect>` or short `<path>` child (at most 512 characters) is four axis-aligned corners that, drawn under the current transform, reach within one point (96/72 device pixels) of every side of the page. Poppler's clip for a LibreOffice page falls short of the page by 0.59 to 0.76 point (right edge 0.76, bottom 0.59, top 0.46, left none, measured on the real samples), which is 1.01 pixel in the SVG of Poppler 26 (sized in points) and 0.76 in that of Poppler 24 (sized in pixels). A smaller rectangle, a rectangle short on one side by more than the point, a rounded one, a curve, several shapes, a clip or shape with a `transform`, `style`, `class`, `clip-path` or `clipPathUnits` attribute, a clip on the root element and any reference to a missing clip path are refused as before. The shape of a clip is read once per clip id however many elements refer to it, and charged once to the vertex budget, so a page of many elements under one clip costs no more than the elements.
  - **EMF and WMF from these sources** are the in-process encoders' output for the SVG page; the PDF chain accepts them as targets only for these sources (the registry offers them for no other PDF source).
  - **The text `<svg` in the bytes of a file named `.vsd`, `.cdr`, `.emf`, `.wmf` or `.cgm` no longer makes it convertible.** The in-process engine used to cut an embedded SVG out of any file of these names and convert that; a real file of these formats does not hold its drawing as SVG, so every real file was refused while a renamed SVG converted. The signature check still refuses a renamed SVG at the door.
  - **Each of these five sources must open with the signature of its own format before it reaches the office suite**, because the suite sniffs the content and would open a Word document or a page of HTML under any of these names as what it is. EMF: header record type 1 and ` EMF` at byte 40. WMF: the Aldus placeable key, or the standard header of nine words. CGM: the binary BEGIN METAFILE command (a first 16-bit word of 0x0020 to 0x003F) or the clear-text `BEGMF` command, which the in-process parser reads. Visio: a compound file whose directory names the `VisioDocument` stream (a file that fills the whole 64 KiB header window without the name in it is taken on its container signature, since its directory may lie beyond). CorelDRAW: a RIFF file whose form type starts with `CDR` or `cdr`, or a ZIP package that holds `content/riffData.cdr` or `content/root.dat` (libcdr's own test), where a package that runs past the bytes read counts when its first entry is in a CorelDRAW folder (`content/`, `metadata/`, `color/`). Forcing the import filter by name for these sources (`--infilter`) is left to the row that does it for every office source (#761).
  - **DWF, Sketch (`sk`) and sK1 (`sk1`) stay refused, with the reason.** The worker includes no reader of DWF 6 (W2D) or DWFx files, Sketch files or sK1 files (the office suite imports none of them). The only open-source DWF reader found, an MIT-licensed W2D and DWFx parser that writes SVG, is a pre-alpha first released in 2026; it is evaluated on the real samples as a separate change before the worker relies on it. The vector editor dropped its Sketch and sK1 import in its 1.0 release, and the remaining converter of those formats is packaged for an end-of-life language runtime and has had no release since 2021. Every target answers with a typed refusal that says so, never with a drawing synthesised from a placeholder. The registry keeps the formats and their targets, so the refusal is what a caller sees. The `<svg` cut-out that `sk` and `sk1` had accepted any file through is gone.
  - **The worker image carries the Draw application** (`libreoffice-draw-nogui`); the image had Writer, Calc and Impress only.
- **Why it is right in practice**: the reference service converts Visio and CorelDRAW files with a vector editor that reads them through libvisio and libcdr, and the metafiles with the same editor, so the same libraries decide what a drawing looks like, and LibreOffice's own test data holds the files that exercise them. LibreOffice and its page chain are already in the worker, with the sandbox, the limits and the typed failures of [40](#40-formats-only-the-office-suite-reads-templates-refused-word-documents-and-xps-text); a second engine would add a process, a licence and an image layer for the same import code. One interchange keeps one rendering path: the PNG, SVG, PostScript and DXF of a drawing show what its PDF shows. Dropping a clip that clips nothing loses no geometry, which is why it is safe where dropping any other clip is not.
- **Trade-offs**: what the importers do not draw is not drawn. Text set on a path stays straight and larger than in CorelDRAW (the badge `pb-poi.cdr` keeps its words but not their curve), and the objects of the right column of `fdo65220-2.cdr` are missing from the render while CorelDRAW's own page thumbnail in the file shows them; the reference tool shows the same page, because it reads the file with the same library. A Visio file converts every page (the reference tool converts the first). The page of a Visio drawing is the page size the file declares. A metafile is a small picture: the office suite puts it on an A4 page (or on the 793.7 x 446.5 pt page of a CGM) and the reference tool crops the page to the drawing, so a converted EMF or WMF has white margins the reference output does not. SVG text, and the text of a DXF or a metafile, is glyph outlines, because it comes from the PDF. The page clip tolerance of one point would also drop a clip that cuts a sliver of the page edge that wide; an office-suite page has nothing drawn there. A conversion costs one office-suite run (the targets of each file took 1 to 3 s in total on a macOS developer machine; no Linux run was made). A `.wmf` file with a picture and a `.cdr` file with a picture convert to PDF, images, SVG and PostScript but not to DXF, EMF or WMF; the registry offers those targets only for `vsd`, where all four real files convert.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Inkscape in the worker** (the reference tool): GPL-3.0, a large GTK and Python distribution for importers that LibreOffice already carries. It runs only as a test oracle on CI and on a developer machine, as a separate program; it reads no CGM and two of the three EMF files.
  - **In-process Visio, CorelDRAW and metafile readers**: large, partly undocumented binary formats; the libraries took years to cover them, and a partial reader would draw some files wrong and call it done.
  - **Draw's own PNG and SVG export** (`draw_png_Export`, `draw_svg_Export`): the PNG covers the first page only and neither shares the PDF rendering path; the page chain handles every page and every target the same way.
  - **Clipping the geometry by the clip rectangle** for the pages the writers refuse: it would unlock the metafile pages with a clip region, but needs a polygon clipper in the geometry reader; recorded as a follow-up, not decided here.
  - **Keeping the SVG cut-out for files that hold the text `<svg`**: it answers with a drawing for a file that is not of the format.
  - **Withdrawing DWF, Sketch and sK1**: the reference service offers them, and a typed answer that names the missing reader tells the caller what is missing.
- **Verified by**: `tests/vector-sources-real-samples.test.ts` (four Visio, two CorelDRAW, three EMF, three WMF and three CGM files of the real-world corpus converted to every target the registry offers: the page count and size read by pdfinfo and pinned, the pictures of the PDF against the bitmap records that the EMF and WMF files themselves hold and the pictures that a CGM file declares by its own BEGIN PICTURE elements, phrases pinned as literals and read by pdftotext, the PNG, JPEG, SVG and PostScript pages compared with the PDF page by SSIM with ghostscript drawing the PostScript, the DXF read by ezdxf and its extents holding every inked pixel of the page, the EMF and WMF drawn again by the office suite, every letter of Inkscape's PDF of the same file in the converter's PDF and the first pages alike, and the colours of the CorelDRAW file with no text compared with the page thumbnail CorelDRAW stored in the file; a renamed SVG refused for all five sources; `sk` and `sk1` refused; one fact per sample that no phrase or second reader judges, read from the records of the file itself (the rounded rectangle of a WMF record against the ink, the background colour of a binary CGM, the pictures of a CGM that draw something, the characters of a CGM's text); a clear-text CGM still read in process; the registry targets equal the converted ones; the real DWF refused for every target with the reason), `tests/office-source-signatures.test.ts` (each signature accepted, and HTML, flat OpenDocument XML, Word and OpenDocument packages, a Word 97 compound file, an SVG, a PDF, a ZIP of unrelated files and random bytes refused under all five names), `tests/svg-page-encoders.test.ts` (the signature each writer of the SVG-page targets writes), `tests/svg-page-clip.test.ts` (a page-sized clip dropped as a path, as a rectangle and under a scale, and written as DXF; the tolerance on each side, at and beyond one point; a `style` or `class` on the clip or its shape; the clip read once for any number of references; a smaller rectangle, a rectangle short in one direction, a clip scaled below the page, a triangle, a curve, rounded corners, several shapes, a transformed clip, a root clip and a missing clip path refused), `tests/office-suite-typed-failures.test.ts` and `tests/office-suite-route.test.ts` (the application, the PDF filter and the routing of the sources), `tests/cad-metafile-registry.test.ts` (the metafile pairs the registry offers), `tests/container-ci-job.test.ts` (the Draw package in the worker image), and a run of the six Visio and CorelDRAW files on `main` before (36 pairs, all refused) and after.
- **Revisit when**: the geometry reader clips by a rectangle (unlocking DXF, EMF and WMF for the metafile pages and for CorelDRAW and Visio files with clip regions), the encoders write bitmaps, the office suite crops a metafile to its drawing or a crop is added to the chain, QA wants the raster formats of the reference list for EMF, the DWF parser passes the real samples, or a maintained Sketch or sK1 reader appears.

## 43. STEP and IGES through a B-rep kernel: exact STEP and IGES output, meshes in millimetres

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, CAD row), decided 2026-10-17 (QA request in issue #783: every format the registry accepts converts its real files, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file).
- **Decision**:
  - **STEP (`step`, `stp`) and IGES (`iges`, `igs`) are read, and STEP and IGES are written, by `cad-convert`**, a program of our own (`native/cad-convert`, about 400 lines of C++) linked dynamically to the unmodified Open CASCADE libraries of the distribution (LGPL-2.1 with the exception, see [11](#11-open-source-engines-only-separate-programs-for-copyleft-tools)). It runs as a separate process in the process sandbox (no network, 2 GiB of memory, a 256 MiB output limit, the time left of the job at most 120 s, a 1 MiB diagnostics buffer, its own scratch directory removed afterwards), like the DWG reader ([29](#29-dwg-input)). The in-process tessellator of STEP and IGES text is gone, and with it the edge (browser) route for these files: the edge has no B-rep kernel, so the tier router sends them to the server and the page converts nothing itself.
  - **The document model carries the file.** The program reads the file into the kernel's document model (shapes, assemblies with their placements, colours and names), and writes STEP and IGES from that model. STEP to IGES and IGES to STEP are therefore exact: the B-rep is rewritten, not a mesh (a STEP face becomes a trimmed surface of IGES, entity 144, over its curves and surface; an IGES surface becomes an `ADVANCED_FACE` over a `B_SPLINE_SURFACE_WITH_KNOTS`), and the colours and part names survive where the model holds them. A rewrite to the same format is the same operation.
  - **Every output is in millimetres unless the request names another unit (`outputUnit`).** The reader converts the length unit the file declares (metre, inch, millimetre, ...) to millimetres, the STEP and IGES writers are told to write millimetres, and STL and OBJ hold millimetre coordinates. STL has no unit field and slicers and viewers read its numbers as millimetres, so a file in metres or inches would arrive 1,000 or 25.4 times too small or too large without this; the STL header and the OBJ comment say millimetres.
  - **STL and OBJ are meshed from the B-rep.** The deviation of a surface from its triangles may be 0.1 % of the model's largest extent and neighbouring triangles may turn by 0.5 radian (about 29 degrees) at most; STL is binary, OBJ shares the vertices of neighbouring faces. A DXF is drawn from that mesh as `3DFACE` entities, one per triangle. A STEP or IGES file that holds curves or points and no surface has nothing to mesh and is refused. OBJ carries no materials: its colours would need a second file.
  - **Typed answers.** A file the kernel cannot read (text, a STEP file under the name of IGES and the reverse, a file cut off in its data) is HTTP 422 with `unreadable`; a file that reads but holds no shape or no surface is 422 with `no-shapes` or `no-surfaces`; a program that crashes (a damaged file can make the kernel's reader fault, the reason it is a separate process) or runs out of its memory is 422, an overrun is the typed timeout, and a missing program is the engine-unavailable answer (503, retried on another worker). There is no fallback to another reader and no placeholder output.
  - **No resource files.** The program needs none of the kernel's resource files; the image excludes the files of the `occt-misc` package (55.7 MB of sample data, 4.8 MB of resource files) with a dpkg path exclusion; the program converts all four targets of the eleven real samples with the directory removed, byte-identical for STL and OBJ, and `strace` shows it opening nothing under it and installs nothing else of the kernel than the five library packages the program links.
- **Why**:
  - Fidelity: the reference converter offers no STEP or IGES conversion, so there is no commercial output to match and the real files are the evidence. The in-process tessellator read two of the eleven real files of the corpus and drew both wrongly (the cylinder of the first was dropped, and its metres were written as millimetres). The kernel meshes all eleven, with the surface area within 0.4 % of an independent mesher of the same files and the boxes in millimetres.
  - Exactness needs a B-rep kernel. Turning a mesh into a B-rep is lossy and cannot be undone (planes, cylinders and trimmed NURBS become thousands of triangles), so STEP to IGES through a mesh, as the in-process writer did, is a different model; the kernel rewrites the surfaces and trims themselves. STEP to STEP and IGES to IGES keep the model, colours and names where the document holds them.
  - Millimetres: STL, the format of 3D printing, has no unit; mm is what slicers assume, and every CAD package exports meshes for printing in mm. The same unit in STEP and IGES output keeps a file made here from changing size in the next tool.
  - A separate process: the kernel is a large C++ parser of attacker-controlled files, and one input in the corpus (an IGES file cut off in the middle) faults its reader. The sandbox turns that into a refused job, and linking it dynamically to the distribution's libraries keeps the LGPL obligations to relinking by the package manager.
  - Cost per conversion: 0.02 to 0.16 s and 44 to 75 MB of memory per file on the eleven real files (Debian 13 with Open CASCADE 7.8, Ubuntu 24.04 with 7.6 and macOS with 7.9 agree to within 0.1 % on the area).
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **Image size.** The five runtime library packages pull 20 packages in total because the visualization library depends on the draw library, Tcl/Tk, a picture-format library and `occt-misc`: 164.7 MB installed (Debian 13, measured as the packages the worker's package list gains), and 104.0 MB after the files of `occt-misc` are excluded (sample data 55.7 MB and resources 4.8 MB, samples and tools 0.2 MB); the program is 0.17 MB. The draw library and Tcl/Tk (about 17 MB) could be removed with `dpkg --force-depends` but would leave the package database inconsistent; they stay.
  - **The same unit mistake as the file.** A file whose header declares a wrong unit is converted as declared. `testfile.igs` of the corpus has a wrong Hollerith count in its Global section (38 for the 32 characters of the file name), so the kernel reads every following parameter one place late, takes the model for inches and writes it 25.4 times too large. A repair of the Global section by the IGES specification (not by file name) is a follow-up.
  - **No colours or names in STL, OBJ and DXF.** Meshes carry geometry only; colours and names survive in STEP and IGES only.
  - **Four options of the old tessellator are gone from the API.** `uSamples`, `vSamples`, `allowOpenMesh` and `smoothingAngleDeg` described the in-process tessellator (a sample grid, a watertightness gate, a crease angle for normals) and would have changed nothing, because the kernel's mesher takes its own deflection and writes no normals; an option that is accepted and ignored is not offered. They are removed from the schema, the types and the edge option lists (the product is not launched, so the contract could change). A request that still sends one is not rejected: the options schema allows unknown keys, as it does for every other key it does not list, and they are ignored. An open shell now converts, where the old gate refused a solid that was not watertight.
  - **`outputUnit` stays and works** (`mm` default, `cm`, `m`, `in`), because exchange tools let the user pick the unit of an STL and slicers differ in what they expect. `cad-convert --unit` scales the vertices of STL, OBJ and DXF to it and writes STEP and IGES in it with the unit declared (IGES unit flag 10 for `cm`, 6 for `m`, 1 for `in`; STEP `SI_UNIT(.CENTI.,.METRE.)`, `SI_UNIT($,.METRE.)` and `CONVERSION_BASED_UNIT('INCH', ...)`), so the same model keeps its physical size when another tool reads it. It applies to the result of a STEP or IGES source only.
  - **No mesh to IGES or STEP B-rep from STL and OBJ.** STL and OBJ sources still become STEP and IGES through the in-process mesh writers (a faceted B-rep), which is lossy by nature and not part of this change.
  - **The browser does not convert STEP and IGES.** Files that the edge converted before are sent to the server.
  - A build of the program (about 3 s) is part of every worker image and CI job.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **The in-process tessellator** (parsing entity text and meshing B-spline surfaces in TypeScript): it read 2 of 11 real files and both wrongly, dropped analytic surfaces (planes, cylinders, cones, tori), could not trim or write a B-rep, and invented ribbon surfaces between two curves. About 4,500 lines of it and the mathematics only it used (surface evaluation, constrained triangulation, exact predicates) are removed.
  - **A WebAssembly build of the kernel** (in the page or the worker): it meshes but writes no STEP or IGES, so the exact rewrite is impossible; its WebAssembly file is 7.6 MB. It is kept as an independent oracle in the tests only.
  - **The kernel's own scripting shell**: Debian ships no binary of it, and the package that does pulls Tcl/Tk and a graphics stack for a console nobody needs.
  - **A mesh as the interchange between STEP and IGES**: lossy, as above.
  - **Paid CAD libraries and services**: out of scope ([11](#11-open-source-engines-only-separate-programs-for-copyleft-tools)).
- **Verified by**: `tests/cad-occt-real-samples.test.ts` (the eleven STEP and IGES files of the real-world corpus: STL and OBJ area and box in millimetres against an independent mesher of the same source file, read by trimesh; STEP to IGES with one trimmed surface (144) per face and the unit flag of millimetre; IGES to STEP with an `ADVANCED_FACE` per face and `SI_UNIT(.MILLI.,.METRE.)`; both exact outputs read again by the independent mesher to the same area and box; the red cube and blue cylinder against the area computed from the file's own cube and cylinder and the box in millimetres, with both parts' names and colours in STEP and IGES; the `outputUnit` option in `cm`, `m` and `in`: STL and OBJ box and area scaled by the unit and its square, the IGES unit flag and STEP unit of the result, and the independent reader measuring the same millimetres; refusals of text, a STEP file named IGES and the reverse, a cut-off file, a cyclic file and an empty one), `tests/cad-occt-wrapper.test.ts` (stand-in programs for every way the program can fail, the arguments it is given and the media types), `tests/cad-step-iges-encoders.test.ts` (the STEP the mesh encoder writes read back by the kernel), the worker container check of `scripts/verify-worker-container.sh` (a STEP face meshed inside the sandbox of the built image), and `tests/ci-install-apt-tools.test.ts` (the program is built after the library packages in CI). The reference converter has no STEP or IGES rows, so the parity gate has none either; the `cad` family is still run for changes to the program.
- **Revisit when**: a Global-section repair for IGES is added; the image budget matters more than the unmodified dependency set (then remove the draw and Tcl/Tk packages); a mesh quality setting is wanted for printing or rendering (the deviation and angle are constants of the program); the reference converter starts to offer STEP or IGES (then add parity rows); or the kernel's reader is found to hang or fault on a class of real files (then a lower time limit or a pre-check of the container structure).


## 44. Spreadsheet page images: a page limit per request, a one-page PDF export, and PDF export without comment annotations

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, office row), decided 2026-10-18 (QA request in issue #783: a real file converts, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file; found by the nightly real-file run 39012667333). Extended 2026-10-20 to text documents and presentations (nightly run 39316523318).
- **Decision**:
  - **A spreadsheet renders at most 500 pages to page images in one request** (`png`, `jpg`: the targets the registry offers for a workbook). The limit is `OFFICE_PAGE_IMAGE_MAX_PAGES` (`page-range.ts`) and applies to the pages the request would draw: every page when it selects none, the selected pages otherwise. A request above it is refused with HTTP 413 before any page is drawn, and the answer names the page count, the limit and the two ways out: `pages` (a range such as `1-10`) or `multiPageOutput: "first"`. 500 is the limit the other multi-page chains of the worker already use (OCR, re-encoded rasters). The limit is set by the worker over whatever the request carries (it has no request field), for every source the office suite opens: since 2026-10-20 that includes text documents and presentations (see the trade-offs). The one-page PDF export below stays a spreadsheet's.
  - **`multiPageOutput: "first"` draws page 1 only, and for a spreadsheet the office suite prints page 1 only.** The worker cuts the page list to its first page before it draws anything (it drew every page and kept the first), does not start the speculative render of pages 1 to 8 that a request without a selection gets, and passes `PageRange` 1 in the FilterData of the spreadsheet's PDF export, so the suite does not lay out and write the other pages. The picture is the page the suite prints first: the same pixels as page 1 of its full PDF drawn by `pdftoppm` (5 real workbooks compared).
  - **A workbook within the limit is unchanged**: the ZIP of every page it prints.
  - **The PDF export states `ExportNotes` false.** The worker passes FilterData to the suite's PDF export (JPEG pass-through, outline). The suite takes every property that FilterData does not name from the export's own defaults, which turn the comments of a document into PDF note annotations; its plain export (no FilterData) writes none. The export now names the property, so the PDF is what the suite's plain export writes.
- **Why**:
  - **The nightly run found workbooks that print 900 to 5,200 pages** (`govdocs1-005540.xls` 1,022 pages, `govdocs1-005551.xls` 919, `govdocs1-005556.xls` 1,937, `govdocs1-009710.xls` 1,993, `office-spreadsheet-libre_office-116306-0.xlsx` 4,552, `office-spreadsheet-no_drawing_patriarch.xlsx` 4,602 and `gd-648529.xls` 2,460, LibreOffice 24.2) because the pages of a worksheet are the cells of its used range cut into sheets of paper. The suite writes their PDF in 3 to 7 s (3 to 7 s with LibreOffice 25.2 too, which prints 1,800 to 5,200 pages and writes 70 to 170 MB), so the time is in the pages: the page chain draws and encodes about 50 ms and 0.5 MB per page (4 vCPU Linux VM), which is 54 s and 487 MB of ZIP for the 1,022-page workbook and 89 s and 195 MB for the 4,552-page one when the VM is idle. The nightly runner draws several jobs at once on a shared machine, so the same requests passed the 180 s limit. The cost grows with the used range and nothing in the file says it beforehand.
  - **Real behaviour of the office suite**: its own PNG graphic filter ignores the page range and draws the current page, and the PDF export of a large sheet scales badly with its pages (LibreOffice tracker 157425, 131626, 101310), so a bounded printed range plus a separate rasteriser is what practitioners use to get one page of a large sheet. The first-page request now measures 0.2 to 1.8 s for the PDF export (4.3 s for the 919-page workbook) instead of 3 to 7 s, and 1.0 to 4.8 s end to end instead of 54 to 89 s.
  - **A refusal beats a half answer.** A workbook of thousands of pages as a ZIP of pages is not an answer a person can use (487 MB), and returning only some pages with no signal would be a different file with the same name. The 413 names what to ask for.
  - **Comments**: a Word document with comments (`office-document-wordwithattachments.docx`, nightly run 39012667333) converted to PDF with note annotations, and its Uzbek Cyrillic comment text (`uz-Cyrl-UZ`) made the Ubuntu 24.04 LibreOffice 24.2 end with a segmentation fault in its exit handlers (`rtl_uString_release` from the language tag library's static destructors), after the PDF was written, so docx to jpg and png failed with `exit 139` while the PDF export of the same file written without FilterData exits 0 (shown with `gdb`, by replacing the language tag in the file, and by naming the property: the last two exit 0). With the property named, the suite writes no annotation, exits 0, and its PDF has the pages and the text of the plain export. LibreOffice 25.2 of the worker image does not crash on the file, but its PDF carried the same annotations. A viewer that shows the comments of a document as sticky notes was never what the plain export, or the benchmark's reference (the suite's own export), produces.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - A spreadsheet that prints more than 500 pages cannot be converted to images in one request: the caller selects up to 500 pages, or takes page 1. The 919-page workbook would have finished on an idle machine; the limit is by page count because the time is not known beforehand, and 500 pages are about 27 s and 240 MB on the measurements above.
  - The request that is refused still pays for the suite's full PDF export (3 to 7 s) and the speculative render of its first 8 pages (about 0.4 s), because the number of pages is known only once the PDF exists.
  - **A document or presentation of more than 500 pages cannot be converted to images in one request either.** A real report (`govdocs1-010383.rtf`, 8 MB) prints 4,181 pages with LibreOffice 24.2 on macOS and 4,874 on Ubuntu 24.04. As a JPEG request it drew all of them and packed 505 to 595 MB (63 s on a quiet Apple silicon machine, 82 s on the Linux testbed) and passed the 180 s limit on the shared nightly runner, while every other target of the file converts in under a second. The request is now refused after the suite's PDF is written (31 s on the testbed, most of it the layout of the document: a PDF cut to the first 501 pages takes 24 s, so no cheaper way to count pages exists) and `pdfinfo` has counted the pages. The stages of the chain (the suite's PDF, the page count, the drawing) run for up to 120 s each instead of the default 45 s, held to what the job has left: on the testbed the suite's layout of that report took 31 s idle, 41 s next to 4 busy loops and more than 45 s next to 8, where the first version ended with `CpuTaskTimeoutError` (422, "exceeded its 45000 ms time limit") before the page count was known (CI run 39332840900); with 120 s the 413 arrives after 75 s next to 8 loops and 108 s next to 12. A host slower than that still answers with the typed time-limit error. The page count of the file's own statistics (`\nofpages4169` in this RTF, against 4,181 and 4,874 pages printed) was not used: the suite lays out more or fewer pages than the writer counted, a refusal from it would be wrong in both directions, and each format keeps it in another place.
  - **PDFs no longer carry comments as sticky notes.** A caller who wanted the comments in the PDF has no option for it (none existed; the notes came from the default of the export).
  - `multiPageOutput: "first"` for every PDF-based page chain now draws one page: the output is the same file, produced faster.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **First page by default for spreadsheets.** It matches what a reference service returns, but the benchmark's own contract for the two-sheet workbook is the ZIP of both pages scored by word recall against the words of both sheets (`workbook.xlsx` to PNG, recall 0.97), so a default of page 1 would cost the parity row half its words and change the output type of every multi-sheet workbook.
  - **A time or output-size limit instead of a page limit.** The page count is known before any page is drawn, so the refusal is immediate and reproducible; a time limit fails after the minutes have been spent, which is what the nightly run showed.
  - **Drawing the first N pages and returning them** with a note: a different file with the same name; the caller can ask for the range.
  - **A cap of 100 pages**: it is below what a few-sheet workbook prints and is derived from the slow rate of `pdftoppm -png` (0.2 to 0.4 s a page), which the worker does not use (it draws PPM pages and compresses them in process).
  - **Draw's own PNG export** for the first page: it ignores the page range and is a different renderer than the page chain (see [42](#42-visio-coreldraw-and-metafile-sources-through-the-office-suite-dxf-page-clips-and-the-formats-with-no-reader)).
  - **Letting the sandbox report exit 139 as success when a PDF exists**: it would excuse any crash after output and would keep the annotations nobody asked for.
  - **Rewriting the language tag of the document** before export: it changes the user's file.
- **Verified by**: `tests/document-page-images.test.ts` (the bounds and the stage limit for every office application; a 40,000-line text and 520 slides refused with a 413 that names the page count the suite's own PDF has, the limit and `multiPageOutput`; the first page and pages 2 to 3 of the long text equal to the suite's pages), `tests/document-page-images.perf.test.ts` (nightly: `govdocs1-010383.rtf` refused to JPEG by name and its first page equal to page 1 of the suite's PDF), `tests/spreadsheet-page-images.test.ts` (the filter data of the first-page export and of the default export; a two-sheet workbook still a ZIP of both pages, each page equal in pixels to `pdftoppm` of the suite's own PDF; a 40,000-row sheet beyond the limit refused with a 413 that names the count, the limit and `multiPageOutput`, its first page and its pages 2 to 3 equal to the suite's pages, none blank; `govdocs1-005540.xls` and `office-spreadsheet-no_drawing_patriarch.xlsx` refused by name and their first pages equal to page 1 of the suite's PDF), `tests/office-comment-annotations.test.ts` (the filter data of the three applications; a Word document with an Uzbek Cyrillic comment, and the real attachments document, converted to PDF with no note annotation read by pdf-lib, and to PNG and JPEG pages the image library decodes, as many as the PDF has pages), `tests/libreoffice-pool-manager.test.ts` and `tests/pdf-export-options.test.ts` (the filter strings), and a run on Ubuntu 24.04 with LibreOffice 24.2 under `ORACLE_STRICT_MODE=1` (the same tests red on `main`: `exit 139` for the real document, a text annotation for the derived one).
- **Revisit when**: a caller needs more than 500 pages of a document in one request (then a streamed ZIP of pages rather than a bigger limit); the page chain gets page-parallel drawing (the rate behind 500 pages improves); a caller needs the comments in the PDF (then an option, default off); or the worker image moves to a LibreOffice whose PDF export defaults match its plain export.


## 45. PostScript Type 1 fonts through FontForge: an OpenType intermediate, a checked container and a glyph count

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, font row), decided 2026-10-18 (QA request in issue #783: every format the registry accepts converts its real files, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file).
- **Decision**:
  - **PFA and PFB sources are read by FontForge** (`fontforge`, the Debian package `fontforge-nox`, GPL-3.0-or-later, see [11](#11-open-source-engines-only-separate-programs-for-copyleft-tools)), unmodified, as a separate process in the process sandbox (no network, 1 GiB of address space, a 128 MiB output limit, 60 s or the time the job has left, a 1 MiB diagnostics buffer, its own scratch directory as its home, removed afterwards). The script is the fixed text `Open($1); Generate($2)`; the font and the result are its two file arguments under fixed names (`input.pfb`, `output.otf`), so no byte of the uploaded file or of its name is ever part of a command.
  - **The intermediate is an OpenType font with CFF outlines.** A Type 1 charstring and a CFF charstring describe the same cubic outlines, so FontForge's result holds every outline, advance width and glyph name of the source without approximation (rounded to the integer em coordinates both formats use). Every target (`otf`, `ttf`, `woff`, `woff2`, `eot`, `svg`) is then written by the SFNT pipeline that already exists; `ttf` uses the existing cubic-to-quadratic conversion.
  - **The container is checked before FontForge sees it.** `readType1Font` (`src/lib/conversions/font-type1.ts`, written from the Type 1 font format and PFB specifications) decides by content, not by extension: a PFA starts with `%!PS-AdobeFont` or `%!FontType1`, a PFB with the segment marker 0x80. A file that is neither, a PFB with a segment of unknown type, one longer than the file or without its end marker, and a PFA with no `eexec` section are HTTP 400 (`CorruptStreamError`). It decrypts the `eexec` section, finds the `CharStrings` dictionary and counts its glyphs; a font with no private dictionary, no glyphs or a charstring that runs past the end is HTTP 422 (`UnprocessableFontInputError`) with the reason.
  - **The upload check accepts the PostScript signature under `.pfa` and `.pfb`.** A Type 1 font written as `%!PS-AdobeFont-1.0: ...` (the first line of most fonts in the wild) opens with the bytes of any PostScript program, and the spoof check refused it as `application/postscript` under a font name before any conversion ran. `.pfa` and `.pfb` now join `.ps` and `.eps` in accepting it; a PostScript page under a font name passes the check and is refused by the reader as HTTP 400, and an executable under a font name is still refused.
  - **The file FontForge reads is rewritten**: a PFB with LF line endings in the text and one binary `eexec` segment (the charstrings themselves are copied byte for byte). FontForge reads a private dictionary written with CR LF line endings as a font without glyphs and exits with success (version 20230101 of Ubuntu 24.04, the upstream release Debian 13 packages, and 20251009 on macOS): the real files `TestT1-Regular.pfa` and `.pfb` of the real-world corpus, and every generated font in the same layout, turned into a font of one empty glyph. Lone CR and LF endings read in full.
  - **The reader is bounded, because it runs in the main process.** A Type 1 source over 16 MiB (`MAX_TYPE1_FILE_BYTES`) is refused before it is read, with HTTP 413 (`PayloadLimitError`) naming the limit; real fonts are far smaller (Latin faces 30 to 150 KB, the URW set at most 134 KB, a Type 1 face of 20,000 CJK glyphs under 10 MB as PFA hex), so the limit is a margin and not a squeeze. Inside it, no more than 131,072 charstring entries (65,536 glyphs and as many subroutines, the most a font holds) and 4,096 PFB segments (a real PFB has three; Adobe's long runs split at 64 KB, 256 segments in 16 MiB) are read, also 413, because an entry of no bytes costs five bytes of file and a heap object, and an empty segment six bytes. The hexadecimal run of a PFA is decoded into one buffer of half its size. Measured on crafted files: a private dictionary of 3.3 million empty `0 RD ` entries (15.7 MB) took 1.75 s and 1.0 GB of heap before (a 100 MB upload would reach about 6 GB), and is now refused in 124 ms and 47 MB of heap; 2.5 million empty PFB segments went from 330 MB to 1 MB and 1 ms; a 16 MiB hexadecimal run went from 265 MB of heap to 25 MB (about 0.4 s, the decryption).
  - **A result with fewer glyphs than the source is refused.** The OpenType font FontForge wrote must start with `OTTO` and hold at least as many glyphs as the `CharStrings` dictionary (FontForge may add a `.notdef`, never lose a glyph); otherwise HTTP 422 names both counts. Any other loss FontForge does not announce is caught the same way.
  - **Typed answers.** A font FontForge exits on with an error, a crash, running out of memory and an output over the limit are 422 with the program's last line (the scratch path replaced); an overrun is the typed timeout; a missing program, or one the shell cannot start, is the engine-unavailable answer (503, retried on another worker). There is no fallback to another reader and no placeholder output.
- **Why**:
  - The font engine reads SFNT containers only, so every Type 1 job failed with `not a valid SFNT` (all three real files of the corpus, to `ttf` and `otf`). A Type 1 font is a PostScript program with encrypted hinted charstrings, and FontForge is the open-source reader that font tools and converters use for it (`fontforge -lang=ff -c 'Open($1); Generate($2)'` is the documented way to convert a font from the command line).
  - A separate process: FontForge is a large C parser of attacker-controlled files with an embedded Python runtime; the fixed script is written in FontForge's own scripting language and the home is the scratch directory, so no user script or plugin directory is read. The sandbox turns a crash into a refused job, and running it unmodified as its own program keeps its GPL obligations with the program.
  - OpenType first, then the existing writers: the outlines stay exact until the one place that must approximate them. On the 560 glyphs of Nimbus Sans L (a 68,590-byte Type 1 font) the existing conversion writes TrueType outlines within 0.26 % of the area of every glyph and 0.11 em units (of 1,000) of each bounding-box edge, in 560 glyphs; FontForge's own TrueType output is within 0.80 % and 0.05 units, and holds 562 glyphs (it adds `.null` and `nonmarkingreturn`). One route also means one set of tables for every target, and woff, woff2, eot and svg need no second one.
  - Cost per conversion: 0.03 s and 18 MB for the Nimbus Sans L font on macOS (FontForge alone), and about 0.14 s per job including the sandbox start, the check and the writers in the tests.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **Image size.** `fontforge-nox` without recommended packages adds ten packages to the worker image: 37.7 MB installed and 8.8 MB to download (Debian 13, an apt simulation against the worker's package list from an empty system), 23.4 MB of it the Python 3.13 runtime library FontForge links, 8.4 MB its data files (`fontforge-common`) and 5.3 MB `libfontforge4`.
  - **GPL in the image.** FontForge is GPL-3.0-or-later. It is used as an unmodified separate program, like the DWG reader and the PostScript interpreter ([29](#29-dwg-input)); `docs/licensing.md` lists it and the image carries its notices.
  - **Hints and kerning.** CFF hints of the Type 1 font stay as CFF hints. The kerning and ligatures of a Type 1 font live in a separate AFM or PFM file that a single upload does not carry, so they are not in the result; the result has the advance widths of the font only.
  - **Not byte-reproducible.** FontForge writes its own unique-identifier name string (with the date) and an `FFTM` table with a timestamp, so two conversions of one font differ in those bytes only.
  - **TrueType size.** The existing quadratic approximation writes about twice the glyf bytes of FontForge's own TrueType output (99.6 KB against 53.9 KB for Nimbus Sans L) for the smaller error above, and names no glyph that the cmap does not map (`post` format 3); it is the same conversion every OpenType CFF font gets.
  - **A reader of our own for the container.** About 250 lines of TypeScript parse the `eexec` section and the private dictionary, so the glyph count and the rewrite do not depend on the tool that may misread the font.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **A clean-room Type 1 to CFF converter in TypeScript.** Hint replacement, flex, `seac` accented characters, `callothersubr` and `div` have to be re-implemented exactly, and the hinted fonts of the corpus exercise them: a large new parser of untrusted charstrings in the main process, where FontForge's is already written, widely used on real fonts and isolated.
  - **fontTools alone** (Python, MIT): it reads Type 1 outlines through pens, which drops the hints and the encoding, and it needs a Python environment with the package in the image and a script around it to build the OpenType tables; one program that reads and writes the font is less to maintain.
  - **Letting FontForge write the TrueType file**: more bytes of approximation error, glyphs added, and a second route for woff, woff2, eot and svg that no one tests.
  - **Passing the file to FontForge unchanged and trusting its exit status**: it exits with success on a font it read as empty (CR LF private dictionary), so a conversion would return a font of one empty glyph.
  - **Checking FontForge's messages for a read failure**: they are localised, differ between versions and are not a contract; the glyph count is.
- **Verified by**: `tests/font-type1-conversion.test.ts` (the three real files of the corpus, `TestT1-Regular.pfa`, `TestT1-ellipsis-hinted.pfa` and `TestT1-Regular.pfb`, to `otf` and `ttf`: fontTools reads the Type 1 source and each result, and the glyph count (6), the family name, and each glyph's advance, outline area and bounding box must match; generated fonts with curves in every spelling, including the CR LF layout that FontForge reads as empty, to `otf`, `ttf`, `woff` and `woff2`; a hostile file name that never reaches the program; and the raw CR LF file handed to the wrapper, which must be refused with both glyph counts), `tests/font-type1-reader.test.ts` (crafted files of each kind refused fast with a typed 413, and the limits themselves read while one more is refused; the reader on seven spellings of one font read back by fontTools: names, advances and outlines unchanged, and charstrings that hold CR, LF and CR LF bytes untouched; every malformed container a typed 400, and the empty, noisy and cut-off private dictionaries a typed 422), `tests/font-type1-wrapper.test.ts` (stand-in programs for every way the program can fail, and the exact arguments it is given), `tests/font-type1-routes.test.ts` (the upload check on both headers, and the status of the three convert routes and of a failed job: 400, 422 and 503), `tests/font-type1-image.test.ts` and the worker container check of `scripts/verify-worker-container.sh` (a Type 1 font written and read again inside the sandbox of the built image). The font parity family runs for changes to these files; the reference converters offer no Type 1 rows, so it has none.
- **Revisit when**: FontForge reads CR LF private dictionaries (then the rewrite can go, and the glyph count stays); a Type 1 font with an AFM or PFM file is accepted as a pair (then kerning can be kept); the image budget matters more than the Python runtime FontForge links (then a build without Python, if the distribution ships one); or a real font is found that FontForge reads with fewer glyphs than the reader counts (then the reader's count or the font is wrong, and the answer is the 422 it already gives).

## 46. MIDI sources, bitmap subtitles in video containers, and streams the installed FFmpeg cannot decode

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, media rows), decided 2026-10-18 (QA request in issue #783: a real file converts, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file; found by the nightly real-file run 39012667333 on Ubuntu 24.04 with FFmpeg 6.1.1, checked on the worker's Debian 13 with FFmpeg 7.1.5); the undecodable audio stream of a video file decided 2026-10-19 on the same issue).
- **Decision**:
  - **A Standard MIDI file (`mid`, `midi`) is rendered to sound by FluidSynth, then encoded by the audio pipeline.** The registry offered WAV and MP3 for MIDI, but FFmpeg has no MIDI reader (its format list holds the unrelated Sample Dump Standard only), so all six pairs of the three corpus files were refused with `ffprobe could not inspect the input media`; the ZIP pairs worked. `midi-render.ts` writes the file into the job's scratch directory, runs `fluidsynth -ni -q -r 44100 -T wav -F out.wav <SoundFont> in.mid` as a separate sandboxed process (no network, 2 GiB address space, 256 MiB output, 120 s unless the job has less left) scales the 16-bit stereo 44.1 kHz WAV by one linear gain so that its loudest sample is at -1 dBFS (29,204 of 32,768; a render already at or above that level, a silent one and any other WAV layout are left as they are), and hands it to the same path a WAV source takes, so `wav`, `mp3` and the audio options behave as for any audio. FluidSynth is LGPL-2.1 and runs unmodified; the SoundFont is read as data.
  - **The SoundFont is the Debian alternatives link `default-GM.sf2`**, which `timgm6mb-soundfont` (a 6 MB General MIDI bank, GPL-2 data) provides; `MIDI_SOUNDFONT_PATH` names another bank, `FLUIDSYNTH_PATH` another program. A missing program or bank is `EngineUnavailableError` (503, retried on another worker). Bytes that do not start with `MThd` (or an RMID wrapper) are refused as not a MIDI file; a file the renderer rejects, one that plays past 256 MiB of sound (about 25 minutes) and a render that outruns its time are typed refusals; a render that wrote no sound is an error, never an empty file.
  - **A bitmap subtitle stream a container cannot carry as it is is left out and reported, never sent to a text subtitle encoder.** PGS (`hdmv_pgs_subtitle`), VobSub (`dvd_subtitle`) and DVB (`dvb_subtitle`) streams are copied into Matroska, which has codec ids for them; MP4, MOV and WebM carry none, and XSUB (`xsub`, from DivX files) is carried nowhere. A stream left out is listed in the result as `droppedStreams` (`kind: subtitle`, `reason: container_unsupported`, with its index, codec, language and title) and in the `X-Dropped-Streams` header. This replaces the earlier typed option error for a PGS, VobSub or DVB stream in MP4, MOV and WebM, which refused the whole file for an optional track, and the unhandled XSUB, which reached the text subtitle encoder and failed with `Subtitle encoding currently only possible from text to text or bitmap to bitmap`. Text subtitles are unchanged (`mov_text` for MP4 and MOV, WebVTT for WebM, copy for Matroska); `subtitles.mode: "burn"` still renders a bitmap stream into the picture.
  - **A stream the installed FFmpeg cannot decode is refused by name, not converted to a part of itself.** (1) FFmpeg 6.1 stops ffprobe at the first xHE-AAC (USAC) stream with `Audio object type 42 is not implemented` at the warning level, below the level the probes run at; the probe is asked again with a bounded log when it fails and the answer is `UnsupportedMediaCodecError` naming the codec. (2) FFmpeg 7.1 identifies xHE-AAC and decodes only the packets whose coding tools it implements, logs `Error submitting packet to decoder: Not yet implemented in FFmpeg, patches welcome` for the others and exits with status 0: `fate-xhe_target_level.m4a` (1.000 s) became a FLAC of 0.256 s. A transcode whose log shows that line is refused with the decoder and the FFmpeg version named. (3) A stream of which nearly every packet is invalid (`Decode error rate 1 exceeds maximum 0.666667`; `ffs-01200.m2ts` carries a 0.36 s "AAC" stream that no packet of decodes) is `UnprocessableMediaInputError` naming the stream index and codec, except in the case of the next bullet. A stream with a few damaged packets is still converted with the damage skipped, as before. FFmpeg 9.0.2 decodes the xHE-AAC sample whole, so a newer build converts it and the same tests then judge the whole decode.
  - **An audio stream no packet of which decodes is left out of a video conversion and listed.** `ffs-01200.m2ts` (the Blu-ray capture of (3)) converts to MP4, MOV, Matroska, WebM and AVI as its video alone, and the result lists `{ index: 1, kind: "audio", codec: "aac", reason: "undecodable" }` in `droppedStreams` and in the `X-Dropped-Streams` header (`audio#1:undecodable`); `kind: audio` and `reason: undecodable` are new values of the public contract and nothing else in it changes. A stream is undecodable when ffprobe states no sample rate for it (it reads the rate from the first frame it decodes while it analyses the start of the file) **and** a second ffprobe, started only for such a stream, decodes no frame from its first 32 packets. The answer is part of the one stream probe a conversion makes (remembered for the file), so a file whose audio decodes costs nothing, and the probe's output never reaches the result: the entry holds the index, the codec name, and the language and title tags, bounded as for every dropped stream. The stream is left out only when the file has a picture to convert and the caller did not choose it with `audio.track`; an audio target (MP3, WMA, FLAC and the rest, where the audio is the content), a file with no video track, and a request for that very track are still refused with the typed error of (3). Another audio track of the file that decodes is kept. FLV and WMV, which are not mapped stream by stream, are still refused.
- **Why**:
  - **MIDI holds notes, not sound.** Audio output for MIDI needs a software synthesizer and a General MIDI sound bank; FluidSynth and TiMidity++ are the established open-source synthesizers. FluidSynth is maintained, fast (the 132 s corpus piece converts to WAV and MP3 in 3.2 s on Ubuntu 24.04), renders to a file without an audio device, and renders the same notes the same way every time, which makes it testable against the file's own timing and pitch.
  - **Matroska is the container that holds picture subtitles.** Its codec specification has `S_HDMV/PGS`, `S_VOBSUB` and `S_DVBSUB` and none for XSUB; MP4's `mov_text` and WebM's WebVTT are text formats and FFmpeg refuses a picture for them. Copying keeps the subtitle exactly; the alternatives either lose it or change the picture. Disc rips and broadcast captures carry these streams as an extra track that most players let the viewer switch off, so the video is the file the user wants.
  - **A truncated output is worse than a refusal.** The 7.1 result looked like a success (status 0, a valid FLAC) and was a quarter of the audio. The refusal tells the caller which codec and which build, and the pair converts on a build that decodes it.
  - **A damaged audio track is not a reason to refuse the picture.** A stream that is damaged or is not what it declares (here 0.36 s of "AAC" beside a 0.38 s picture) is not something a player plays, and it shows the video and plays nothing for the stream. The reason given for bitmap subtitles applies: the file holds one thing the target can use, and refusing all of it for an optional track loses that thing. Saying so in `droppedStreams` keeps the loss visible, which is what makes dropping acceptable; the audio target stays a refusal because there the dropped stream would be the whole output.
  - **Why two signals.** A missing sample rate alone also describes a stream whose first frame comes late in the file, and a failed decode of the first packets alone cannot tell damage at the start from damage throughout; together they describe a stream that FFmpeg itself could not open (its encoder then has no rate to be given: the same file converted to WMA stopped on the missing rate). 32 packets are enough to see whether a stream decodes at all, at one process of 45 ms on this file, and a probe that fails or times out leaves the stream in the conversion, which then refuses it as before.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **Sound quality is that of a small bank.** TimGM6mb is a 6 MB General MIDI bank; FluidR3 sounds fuller and is 141 MB. FluidSynth's default gain (0.2) is chosen to avoid clipping with many voices and leaves the corpus pieces 16 to 23 dB below full scale (peaks -23 to -16 dBFS, RMS -41 to -32 dBFS); the gain stays and the loudness is fixed afterwards (peak normalisation, above). The bank is not changed; an operator who wants another sets `MIDI_SOUNDFONT_PATH`.
  - **Peak normalisation changes the level, not the balance.** One linear gain keeps the dynamics and the mix of the piece, but a piece with one short loud chord is raised less than a steady one, and two pieces of equal peak differ in loudness. The gain is applied to the 16-bit samples with rounding, which adds noise at the level of one least significant bit.
  - **Image size.** `fluidsynth` and `timgm6mb-soundfont` add 9 packages and 16.5 MB installed to an image that has FFmpeg (apt simulation on Debian 13, `Installed-Size` of the packages the worker's package list gains: FluidSynth and its library 0.8 MB, the bank 6.1 MB, PipeWire and its plug-in library 6.2 MB, four other libraries 3.4 MB); CI installs the same two packages. FluidR3 would have added 155.6 MB.
  - **A render is bounded at about 25 minutes of sound** (`RENDER_MAX_BYTES`), and its WAV is held in memory twice while it is encoded.
  - **A file with a bitmap subtitle track loses it in MP4, MOV and WebM**, with the loss reported. A caller who needs the words in the picture burns them; a caller who needs the stream converts to Matroska.
  - **xHE-AAC files are refused on the builds that cannot decode them completely**, including files a 7.1 build would have decoded whole; the refusal does not try to tell them apart, because the log of a decoder that skipped a packet is the only evidence. The nightly run on Ubuntu 24.04 still refuses `fate-xhe_target_level.m4a` (typed now); a newer FFmpeg in CI or the image is the fix and is not part of this change.
  - **Only a stream with no sample rate is dropped.** A stream that states a rate but whose packets nearly all fail is still refused (3), and so is a stream that a build cannot decode for a missing coding tool (xHE-AAC): both name the cause. An audio stream whose codec the build lacks and that states no rate (an MPEG-TS stream of a codec without a decoder) is dropped and listed as undecodable, which is true of the build and not of the file.
  - **A capture with undecodable audio converts to a silent file** for the callers who did not read `droppedStreams`; the list and the header are the only report, as for the subtitles dropped before. FLV, WMV and the thumbnail routes are not planned stream by stream and keep refusing it.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **TiMidity++ with Freepats**: the same job at 35.8 MB installed with the Freepats patch set (apt simulation), against 16.5 MB for the chosen pair; its output was not compared with the chosen bank's, so the smaller image decided.
  - **FluidR3_GM as the default bank**: the common reference bank; +139 MB of image for a format that is a small part of the traffic.
  - **A synthesizer in TypeScript or WebAssembly**: a second engine to maintain, and no General MIDI bank that is free to ship at a useful size.
  - **OCR of bitmap subtitles to text** (to fit MP4): slow, language-dependent and lossy; it turns a stream copy into a transcription the user did not ask for.
  - **Burning bitmap subtitles in by default**: changes the picture for everyone to keep a track that most viewers switch off.
  - **Retrying without the stream when the run reports the error rate:** FFmpeg checks the rate when the input ends, so a long video would be encoded to the end and then again.
  - **A decode probe of every audio stream of every video conversion:** a process (45 ms here) for the files that need nothing.
  - **A separate warning field instead of `droppedStreams`:** the list already says which stream and why on every surface (JSON, job view, webhook, header); a second field would say the same twice.
  - **Raising FluidSynth's gain (`-g`)**: the synthesizer clips a dense file before it can be brought back, and the right gain differs per piece; the peak is known only after the render.
  - **EBU R128 loudness normalisation (`loudnorm`)**: it targets perceived loudness, changes the dynamics (limiter and, in dynamic mode, gain riding) and needs two passes over the audio; a level that only needs to be audible and unclipped does not justify either.
  - **Running every ffmpeg transcode with `-xerror`**: ends a conversion at the first damaged packet of any stream, which real files of every kind contain.
  - **A version table of FFmpeg builds that decode xHE-AAC**: wrong in both directions (7.1 decodes some such files whole and Debian's 7.1 does not decode this one); the log of the run is the evidence.
- **Verified by**: `tests/media-midi-render.test.ts` (notes at 220, 440, 880 and 330 Hz rendered to WAV and read back by a separate ffmpeg with the pitch measured by autocorrelation against the equal-temperament formula, the length checked against the sum of the file's own delta times at its tempo, the peak of the delivered WAV within 0.1 dB of -1 dBFS with no sample at full scale for the built notes and the three corpus files, a tempo of 60 against 120 beats a minute doubling the length, the `.midi` extension, MP3 decoded without error at the same pitch, the three corpus files as long as their delta times say plus at most six seconds of ring-out and not silent in WAV and MP3, bytes that are not MIDI refused, a missing program answering 503) with `tests/helpers/midi-craft.ts` (a Standard MIDI File writer and reader that share nothing with the renderer), `tests/media-bitmap-subtitles-real-samples.test.ts` (a Blu-ray capture, a Blu-ray remux and a DivX file with XSUB converted to MP4, MOV and Matroska: ffprobe lists the subtitle streams of the output, which are exactly the PGS, VobSub and DVB streams of the source for Matroska and none otherwise, and the reported dropped streams equal the source streams the output lacks, with index, codec, language and title), `tests/media-stream-mapping.test.ts` (the plan of a PGS, mixed text and bitmap and XSUB input for every container; a PGS file in Matroska to MP4, MOV and WebM), `tests/media-undecodable-streams.test.ts` (the xHE-AAC sample to four audio targets: converted whole, 1.0 s within 0.1 s and not silent, when a separate ffmpeg decodes it whole, and refused with the codec named otherwise; the Blu-ray capture converted to MP4, MOV, Matroska, WebM and AVI as its video alone, after a separate ffmpeg and ffprobe confirm that no packet of its audio decodes while its video does, with the dropped list equal to the one audio stream and holding only the index, kind, codec and reason, every frame of the source video in the output and a decode of the output without error; the worker engine's result the same; a file with a decodable and an undecodable audio track keeps the first and lists the second; the audio targets, the request for the track itself and a file with no picture refused with the stream named; a stream with 24 damaged bytes still converted), `tests/media-stream-mapping.test.ts` (the plan for a stream marked undecodable: left out and listed in all five containers, kept when chosen by index or when there is no picture) and `tests/media-dropped-streams-api.test.ts` (the v1 convert JSON, the raw header `audio#1:undecodable`, the OpenAPI lists), `tests/midi-render-normalize.test.ts` (the normalisation: exact samples for a known gain, the peak of either channel, a file at or above the target, a silent file and a layout it does not read unchanged to the byte, a peak of 1), `tests/midi-render-image.test.ts` (the image, CI and container-check wiring), and runs on Ubuntu 24.04 (FFmpeg 6.1.1, FluidSynth 2.3.4) and Debian 13 (FFmpeg 7.1.5, FluidSynth 2.4.4) under `ORACLE_STRICT_MODE=1`, red on `main` before the change.
- **Revisit when**: a fuller General MIDI bank is wanted (set the bank, or add `fluid-soundfont-gm` for +139 MB); QA wants another target level, or perceived loudness instead of the peak; the worker image or CI moves to an FFmpeg that decodes xHE-AAC whole (the tests then judge the whole decode, and the refusal stays for older builds); a picture-subtitle codec gains a codec id in MP4 that players read; a source with more than 25 minutes of MIDI is asked for; QA wants an audio stream that states a rate and decodes nothing dropped as well, or FLV and WMV planned stream by stream.
## 47. Merging without the up-to-date requirement

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #797, epic #763), decided 2026-10-18 (QA decision). Applied to the `main` ruleset on 2026-10-18.
- **Decision**: the `main` ruleset no longer requires a pull request to be up to date with `main` before it merges. The required checks stay `verify` and `SonarCloud Code Analysis`, on the pull request head. Speed parity still runs on the head once the `automerge` label is added, and `verify` still requires it for a changed conversion family.
- **Why it is right in practice**:
  - **The measured cost of the rule.** When one pull request merged, every other open one turned behind and had to merge `main` and run the whole pipeline again: about 19 minutes, quality parity 14 and speed parity 12. On 2026-10-18 #794 passed every check at 01:28, turned behind when #796 merged, and ran again; with three or four open pull requests, merges waited in series for about 20 minutes each. The runners were not the limit.
  - **The usual answer, a merge queue, is not available here.** GitHub offers merge queues only to repositories owned by an organization. The ruleset update with a merge queue rule was refused (`422 Invalid rule 'merge_queue'`), and the queue workflow of #798 was reverted by #799.
  - **Off is the platform default**, and most projects without a merge queue merge this way. They rely on the checks of the head and on the run after the merge.
  - **This repository's pull requests rarely share files.** They are area-sized changes to separate conversion families, and a conflict in the text still blocks the merge.
- **Benefits and trade-offs**:
  - Merges no longer wait in series, and a merge does not cost every other pull request a full run.
  - The cost: two pull requests that each pass alone can break `main` together, through a change that does not conflict in the text (a renamed export, a shared baseline count, a policy entry number). The push to `main` runs the tests, the conformance and integration suites, the container check and quality parity for the changed families, and the nightly run measures speed. A red `main` is then fixed first, before the next merge.
  - Speed parity measures the head against its own base, not against the `main` it merges into. A speed regression that appears only in combination shows in the nightly run.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Keeping the rule**: about 20 minutes of waiting per merge in series, for a risk that the run after the merge catches.
  - **Moving the repository to an organization to get the merge queue**: the repository URL, the SonarCloud binding, secrets and deploy hooks all change, for a saving the rule change already gives.
  - **Running speed parity on the push to `main` too**: 12 more minutes per merge for a number the nightly run measures.
- **Verified by**: the ruleset read back after the update (`strict_required_status_checks_policy: false`, `verify` and `SonarCloud Code Analysis` required), and the next merges, which no longer turn the open pull requests behind.
- **Revisit when**: `main` breaks after a merge because of two pull requests that passed alone (then turn the rule back on for the change sets that touch shared files, or move to an organization for the merge queue); or the repository moves to an organization.

## 48. One-shot office conversions without a UNO listener

- **Status**: Implemented (flake rows of issue #692, epic #763), decided 2026-10-18 (QA request).
- **Decision**:
  - **Every pooled office-suite run is a one-shot `--convert-to` process and starts without a UNO listener.** The pre-warm run, the readiness probe and each conversion no longer pass `--accept`. The pool keeps one profile directory (`-env:UserInstallation`) and one work directory per worker, the 45 s stage limit, the 120 s ceiling and the sandbox options.
  - The `daemonMode` option and statistic and the per-worker `unoPipeName` and `unoAccept` fields are removed, because only the listener used them.
- **Why**:
  - **The listener served no client.** The pool starts a new `soffice --convert-to` process for each job and reads the file it writes; no UNO client ever connects to the pipe. It was a leftover of the resident UNO socket pool design (#203). The same class of hang had already been fixed once for a TCP listener (#428), which cannot bind where loopback is down.
  - **Measured on Linux (LibreOffice 24.2.7, 4 vCPU):** hung runs had complete output on disk, the main thread waiting on a futex and two threads waiting in `accept(2)` on the pipe listeners, so the listener shutdown races at exit. With the listener, CorelDRAW to PDF hung 9 times in 600 and 4 times in 600, and Word to PDF 4 times in 800. Without it: 0 in 600, 0 in 600 and 0 in 800.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **A future UNO client needs its own design**: a separate long-lived process that owns the listener and its lifecycle, not a listener on the one-shot runs.
  - Nothing is lost today: the pool never used the connection, so conversion output and latency are unchanged.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Repeating the conversion on a fresh worker after the timeout**: keeps the 45 s penalty on every hang and hides the cause.
  - **Capping the number of listening processes**: with one daemon per test process the hang rate did not change (3 in 600).
  - **Raising the stage limit or the test timeouts**: hides a stall and does not remove it.
- **Verified by**: `tests/office-table-and-daemon-pool.test.ts` and `tests/libreoffice-pool-loopback-less-namespace.test.ts` (the pre-warm, the readiness probe and the conversion pass no `--accept`, `socket,` or `pipe,` argument, each worker keeps its own profile), and the Linux runs recorded in the pull request: the two CI test files repeated under four-way parallel load, and CorelDRAW to PDF under a four-way stress, both without a hang.
- **Revisit when**: a feature needs a UNO client (then add a separate long-lived process that owns the listener), or the worker image moves to a LibreOffice whose listener exits cleanly and a measurement shows a use for it.

## 49. PDF text from fonts without a Unicode map, and PDFs with an empty open password

- **Status**: Implemented (PDF text rows of issue #783, epic #760), decided 2026-10-19 (QA request).
- **Decision**:
  - **A glyph of the text layer is read in this order, and a glyph that no step maps is left out.** First the font's ToUnicode map and the names of its encoding that pdfjs reads. Then the name the font's encoding gives the character code (the base encoding and its `/Differences`), through the rules of the Adobe Glyph List specification: a listed name, a ligature name split at its underscores (`s_s`, `e_t_t`, `f_f_i` are the letters they join), `uniXXXX` and `uXXXX` names, and anything after a period dropped. The Zapf Dingbats list applies to a Zapf Dingbats font only. For the Symbol, Wingdings and Webdings families the symbol tables of entry 7 read the private-use character the font keeps its glyph at. A glyph with no text after that (a control code, U+FFFD or a private-use character) is left out, never replaced by a character.
  - **What was left out is reported.** The result carries one warning line, `Left out N glyphs that no font encoding maps to Unicode (pages ...)`, for the glyphs of pages that read.
  - **A page where the glyphs without text outnumber the others is left out as a whole**, and a warning names it; with OCR on, such a page is recognized instead. **A document is refused with `PdfTextUnmappedError` (400) only when no page of it reads**, that is, when every page that has text is such a page.
  - **A PDF whose open password is empty opens without asking**, whatever its owner restricts (copying, editing, printing), on every route that reads the file in the process: text, Markdown, HTML, hOCR, ALTO, the Office targets and the OCR pages. The file is decrypted by qpdf into a copy that only the readers see; a result that is the PDF itself still returns the original bytes. A file whose owner password is empty opens too (qpdf and Poppler open it; only pdfjs asked for a password). A file that needs a password the request lacks, or got wrong, is a typed 422 (`PdfPasswordRequiredError`); the request's `password` now also reaches the in-process routes.
  - **A PDF that Poppler cannot read is tried by the in-process reader before it is refused.** `pdftotext` ends with status 1 or 99 and a syntax message for such a file; the worker then falls back to the in-process reader, which reads what it can (a page that Poppler's repair does not find) or refuses with a typed 400. The untyped failure of the process is gone.
  - **A glyph name is bounded like the text it makes.** A name longer than 127 characters (the limit of ISO 32000-2 Annex C) maps to nothing; the text of a code is read once per font; and the characters of a page are counted again after the glyph names are read, because a ligature name makes one glyph many characters, so a page that grows past the page limit is refused with the same typed 400 as one that starts past it.
  - **OCR to PDF is an edit of the document, not a read.** The searchable PDF is written from the decrypted copy, so an encrypted input is opened as unlock opens it: with the owner password or `confirmEditRights: true`, otherwise the 422 of entry 4. A PDF that is not encrypted is unchanged. A damaged file that Poppler refuses is read by the in-process reader also when the request carries a password (it decrypts with it), so the outcome is its result or a typed 400, never a 503.
  - **A PDF with pictures and no text is written to HTML and Markdown with its pictures** when OCR recognized nothing, not as an empty page. Two pages of the real-world corpus that hold neither text nor a picture are marked `emptyInput` there, with the reason read from the file.
- **Why**:
  - **Readers open a file with an empty open password.** The owner restrictions of such a file are flags a viewer chooses to honour; they do not encrypt anything the reader cannot decrypt itself, and refusing the file blocked routine conversions of files that every viewer shows.
  - **Extractors do not refuse a document for a few glyphs.** They read the font's own mapping, then the glyph names through the Adobe Glyph List, and skip what has no text. Measured on the 1,261 PDFs of the real-world corpus (1,199 government files and 62 test files), the old rule refused 110 documents for 59,343 glyphs of 60.6 million characters (0.1%); the new rule converts 109 of them and refuses the one document in which no glyph maps (`pdf-pdfjs-issue17333.pdf`, one glyph). Of the 599 pages the old rule refused, 396 had under 1% of their glyphs without text, 94 had 1 to 5%, 45 had 5 to 20%, 59 had 20 to 50% and 4 had more, one of them all of its glyphs. The line at one half therefore leaves out 5 of those 599 pages and keeps 594 with gaps.
  - **The ligature names of a layout program are the one gap in pdfjs's own mapping.** pdfjs maps `fi` and `uni20AC`, not `s_s` or `e_t_t`: "Massachusetts" in a magazine set in a script typeface (`govdocs1-000163.pdf`) was refused for two glyphs, and Poppler reads it.
  - **A page left out with a warning is honest; a page of control codes is not.** A character in the text that is no text pollutes every program that reads the output.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **Text loses the glyphs that no mapping reaches, and the reader of the output learns it from the warning only.** In the sample 69% of them are in bitmap (Type 3) fonts that name their glyphs `a16`, `a17` and so on, 11% in composite fonts with no ToUnicode map (symbols, math and Arial subsets), and 0.5% in simple TrueType fonts (312 glyphs).
  - **A glyph that pdfjs gives a plausible but wrong character is not detected.** A font whose built-in encoding differs from its declared one reads as ASCII with shifted letters (`CG.Times095`, `govdocs1-001389.pdf`; Poppler prints the same). Only OCR reads such a page.
  - **A page of which just over half the glyphs map is left out, and one with just under half is kept with gaps.** The line is a measured one (above), not a quality score.
  - **Reading an encrypted file costs one qpdf run, and needs qpdf.** The worker image has it; a machine without it answers 503 for an encrypted file where pdfjs alone used to read an empty-password file.
  - **Pictures of a document with no text are written into Markdown as data URIs**, as for any other source with pictures: the file can be large.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Refusing the whole document for any glyph without text**: the rule that refused 110 of 1,261 files for 0.1% of their glyphs.
  - **Writing such a glyph as U+FFFD or as the private-use character**: the replacement mark of entry 7 is for a writer that must draw something; the text of a reader goes into search, indexes and other converters, where a mark is garbage.
  - **OCR for every page with a glyph without text**: about one second a page, wrong for a page that reads, and a failure where no OCR engine is installed.
  - **Treating owner restrictions as a reason to refuse a conversion, or to ask for `confirmEditRights`**: entry 4's confirmation is for edits of the file; a conversion reads what any viewer shows (OCR to PDF is the exception: its output is a rebuilt copy of the file, so it is an edit).
  - **A reader for the embedded font's own tables (`cmap`, `post`)**: pdfjs already reads them for TrueType fonts before a glyph reaches this step, and what remains in simple TrueType fonts is 0.5% of the dropped glyphs. The composite fonts (11%) need the font program, so a font-dictionary reader and a font table parser; that is a change of its own.
- **Verified by**: `tests/pdf-glyph-names.test.ts` (the worked examples of the Adobe Glyph List specification, and the vendored lists against the glyph tables of pdfjs); `tests/pdf-glyph-recovery.test.ts`; `tests/pdf-glyph-recovery-limits.test.ts` (the name parser is called once per code, and a recovered text past the limit is refused); `tests/pdf-text-unmapped-glyphs.test.ts` (hand-written PDFs: ligature names, left-out glyphs and their count, a page left out, a document that is still refused, a page that outgrows the limit through a ligature name); `tests/pdf-ocr-encrypted-edit-gate.test.ts` (OCR to PDF of owner-restricted files made by the qpdf command line); `tests/pdf-damaged-with-password.test.ts`; `tests/pdf-empty-password-open.test.ts` (files encrypted by the qpdf command line in four encryption variants, against the same files unencrypted); and `tests/pdf-text-real-samples.test.ts` on the real files, with the text, images and glyph counts that `pdftotext` and `pdfimages` report. The real-world gate no longer lists `qpdf-issue-101.pdf` to text, and `pdfjs-empty_protected.pdf` and `qpdf-encrypted-with-images.pdf` to Markdown, as known failures.
- **Revisit when**: a sample shows simple or composite TrueType glyphs that the font's own tables would map in numbers that matter, a QA decision changes the share at which a page is left out, or pdfjs exposes the embedded font program (then reading its tables is a small change).

## 50. tar to tar.bz2 by lbzip2 for small tarballs, and the measured floor of the xz, 7z and tar to 7z rows

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #685, epic #759), decided 2026-10-19 (QA request).
- **Decision**:
  - **A tar written to `tar.bz2`, `tbz2` or `tbz` is read and vetted in process and written by `lbzip2` (Debian and Ubuntu package `lbzip2`, GPL-3.0-or-later, an unmodified separate sandboxed process) when the tar is at most two bzip2 blocks long.** The route is the one tar to ZIP and tar to gzip take (entry 30): the tar is read with the in-process reader, the extraction policy runs on its listing (names, links under `skipLinks`, entry and size caps, collisions), and the canonical tar written again from the vetted entries is piped to `lbzip2 -c -N -n T` as segments, never joined into one buffer first. The stream comes back on stdout. The bound is `2 x 100 000 x N` bytes, 1.8 MB at the default level; a larger tar goes to the 7-Zip route exactly as before.
  - **Levels**: the product's `compressionLevel` maps to the block size multiplier `N` as 7-Zip's bzip2 coder mapped it, so the block sizes do not change: 0 and 1 are `-1`, 2 is `-3`, 3 is `-5`, 4 is `-7`, 5 to 9 are `-9`. `T` is `os.availableParallelism()`. 7-Zip's extra passes at levels 7 to 9 are not reproduced (lbzip2 has one pass); its single pass at `-9` is no larger than 7-Zip's seven (273 708 against 273 895 bytes on the benchmark tar).
  - **`lbzip2` missing, failing, outrunning its limit or writing something that is not a bzip2 stream of the block size asked for is a fail-over, not a result**: the route returns nothing and the 7-Zip route serves the request. An abort stops the process group and is rethrown. The command is found at `/usr/bin`, `/usr/local/bin`, `/opt/homebrew/bin` or on `PATH` (`LBZIP2_PATH` overrides; an override that names no file counts as not installed). The worker image and the CI image install `lbzip2` (146 KiB installed, depends on libc6 only), `scripts/verify-worker-container.sh` checks that it round-trips inside the sandbox, and the oracle preflight lists it, so a missing package fails CI by name instead of silently taking the slow route.
