  - **Up to 48 MB more resident memory per thread**: peak resident memory of a process reading 300 documents on one thread at a time was 310 MB with the threshold and 262 MB without (333 MB at 256 MB). The flag is a threshold, not a limit. Measured through the host with the bundle (700 documents, the first 100 left out, 4 runs each, on a VM under a load of 25 from other jobs, so the figures are high): mean 7.57 to 7.68 ms per document without the threshold and 5.93 to 6.31 with it (median 5.85 to 6.12 against 4.52 to 4.89); peak resident memory of the process 323 to 338 MB without and 382 to 399 MB with it (about 60 MB for the two threads that overlap at a hand-over).
  - **Up to two threads per pool thread exist for about 20 documents of each 100**, one of them idle, each with the reader library and its heap loaded.
  - **A replacement that sits unused for 15 seconds is stopped**, and started again by the next document past 80, so a pool with under one document per second starts a thread every 15 seconds between its 80th and 100th document.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **A longer warm-up** (one to 80 reads of the fixed page; two or six rounds of the benchmark's own documents): no change in the median call (35.6 to 38.9 ms against 35.3 to 36.9 ms for one read); the benchmark documents lowered the first 20 documents from 8.0 to 5.7 ms and lengthened the start by as much.
  - **Why the bundle was slower than the source.** The same reader file ran 4.55 ms per document in the bundle and 4.15 ms from the source (three workers of 160 documents; the profile is 15 percent slower in every function of the reader, with no hot spot). The bundle's threads did 13 full collections in 300 documents and the source's 6, and the threshold above brings the bundle to 3.87 ms. Heap limit of the thread against ms per document (bundle): 256 MB 4.97, 1 GB 4.70, 4 GB 3.97; young generation limit of 16 to 64 MB: no change. Other causes checked and ruled out:
    - **A different build of the reader.** A trace of the files opened shows both modes read `legacy/build/pdf.mjs` and `legacy/build/pdf.worker.mjs` of the same package (the source path opens `pdf.mjs` twice, once more for the loader).
    - **The bundler lowering private members.** The bundle is built with `--packages=external`, so the reader is not in it: it holds only this repository's code (209 KB), which has no `#private` member, and a search for the lowering helpers (`__privateGet`, `__privateAdd`) finds none with `node24`, `esnext`, `es2017` or `es2015` as the target; its one `WeakMap` is a table of glyph names. The lowering of private members to weak collections, which costs garbage-collector time when many instances exist, therefore does not apply.
    - **The target and the syntax form.** On a quiet VM `node24`, `es2017` and `es2022` gave 4.4 to 4.6 ms per document, and a minified bundle 4.56; the same set (with `esnext` and `es2015`) measured again while another job loaded the VM (load 6 to 8) gave 5.2 to 8.1 ms in a spread wider than any difference between the targets, so those runs rank none of them.
    - **Also checked:** a transformed copy of the reader, `require` instead of `import()`, a zero stack trace limit, an eval or a file entry.
  - **A heap limit of 4 GB for the thread:** the same speed-up, but the limit is what stops a hostile document from taking the process's memory.
  - **The flag in `NODE_OPTIONS`:** V8 refuses it there.
  - **Not loading the native canvas module in a new thread** (the first version counted 78 ms of a 125 ms start for it): the reader requires it when its Node utilities are evaluated, before any option is read, so it would take a hook on the module loader of every thread (`module.registerHooks`, whose hooks a thread does not inherit). Measured in the follow-up with the hook in place: the module takes 1.3 ms in a new thread and a start is 115.9 ms with the hook against 114.7 ms without, so the 78 ms were not the module. Not done.
  - **Reader and engine settings:** the baseline compiler for every function, no lazy feedback allocation, a 16 MB minimum young generation, no lazy compilation, a single GC thread: 35.3 to 36.3 ms against 35.3 ms, and 38.5 ms for the single GC thread.
  - **The on-disk compile cache of Node** (`NODE_COMPILE_CACHE`, or `module.enableCompileCache(directory)` in the thread; measured in the first version and again in the follow-up): the first document of a new thread 100 ms instead of 130 ms, and a replacement's start 60 to 74 ms instead of 86 (with pdf-lib lazy), 25 ms of CPU, nothing for the following documents. It puts a directory whose content is executed code, written by the worker, among the files the service has to protect (the engine checks the cache by the source's hash and its own version, not by a signature). That trade is not worth 25 ms of a start that does not move the row. Not shipped.
  - **A startup snapshot of the thread, or a code cache of the bundle by `vm.Script`** (follow-up): Node's documentation describes a snapshot as the state a whole process is started from (`--snapshot-blob`, with an entry built into it), and nothing in the options of a worker thread names one, so a thread cannot start from it. A `cachedData` for the bundle covers only this repository's 209 KB of code; the reader is loaded by the module loader, which the compile cache covers for every module without code of ours.
  - **Reader options** (follow-up): `isOffscreenCanvasSupported` (false in Node by default), `disableFontFace` (true in Node) and `useWorkerFetch` (false) are documented defaults that the thread already has, `standardFontDataUrl` is set, and a canvas factory is only used for drawing. None of them stops the module the reader requires when it is evaluated.
  - **Warming a replacement with the benchmark's own documents** (follow-up): coefficient of variation 0.23 to 0.24 against 0.28 to 0.35 (see above), but those files are the documents the row times and the fixtures of the tests, and shipping them in the service to warm its threads would tune the service to the benchmark. Not shipped.
  - **A generated warm-up document with an embedded font program** (follow-up; a TrueType writer of about 165 lines and a three-page document read eight times, the replacement started after 60 documents instead of 80): the coefficient of variation of the calls fell by about 9 percent (0.313 to 0.286 over six alternating runs, lower in five), the median call by 3 percent, and the row against the base did not change (1.019, 0.989, 1.014). That is within the noise of the VM, and a font generator and an earlier, longer-lived replacement (idle restarts in a pool that takes under 2.7 documents per second) are not worth it. Not shipped.
  - **Reusing the thread and reloading the reader's modules** to drop their state: the heap, the realm and every global the reader changed would stay shared, which is less than a new thread gives.
  - **More documents per thread, or no retirement by count.** Lifetimes of 300 and 1,000 documents measured 0.86 to 0.88 (coefficient of variation 0.13 to 0.15) and 0.91 to 0.94 (0.08 to 0.09) against the reference when timed from the source. QA rejected it: the lifetime bounds what a defect of the reader can accumulate across documents of different users.
  - **Timing the bundle with the loader of the benchmark process inherited by the thread:** the row reads 0.70 to 0.72 with a coefficient of variation of 0.17 to 0.20, because every thread start costs 450 ms; the service never runs that way.
  - **Starting the replacement when the pool is idle:** the pool has no way to know when the next document comes, and the benchmark's reference runs are the idle time.
- **Sources** (fetched on 2026-10-20 unless marked):
  - The bundler's documentation of content types, "Private member performance": the transform of `#name` members uses weak collections, engines may not perform well with large ones, and many instances of such classes may cost the garbage collector. It applies to code the bundler transforms below the syntax level; the reader is not bundled here.
  - The engine's post on faster class features: initialising class fields, private ones included, was much slower than ordinary assignment until v9.7 made it go through inline caches. Node 24 has a newer engine.
  - The reader's project discussion of its modern and legacy builds (discussion 17989): the legacy build is the one for runtimes that lack newer language features, and the one the package documents for Node. The code imports the legacy build in both modes.
  - The documentation of worker threads: `resourceLimits` bound the engine's heap only, not buffers; V8 options are not accepted in `execArgv`, and an explicit `execArgv`, an empty one included, replaces the inheritance from the parent.
  - The documentation of the module compile cache: the cache is written when the process is about to exit, from the compiled code, into a directory.
  - The post on the engine's baseline compiler: it lets the engine tier up much more eagerly for short-lived code (the flag that compiles every function with it is not named there).
  - Barrett, Bolz-Tereick, Killick, Mount and Tratt, "Virtual Machine Warmup Blows Hot and Cold", OOPSLA 2017 (arXiv 1602.00602): benchmarks on common JIT-compiled VMs often fail to reach a steady peak, so warm-up cannot be assumed to end.
  - The documentation of Node's modules (fetched 2026-10-20): `module.enableCompileCache(directory)` persists the engine's code cache of CommonJS and ES modules in a directory, affects the current instance only (a worker thread calls it itself or inherits `NODE_COMPILE_CACHE`), and writes entries when the instance is about to exit; caches are not reusable across Node versions. `module.registerHooks` runs synchronous hooks in the thread that loads the modules, and they are not inherited by worker threads unless registered through a preloaded file.
  - The documentation of Node's command line and of its `v8` module (fetched 2026-10-20): `--build-snapshot` and `--snapshot-blob` build and start a process from a snapshot of an entry script, with a single entry point and a subset of built-in modules that is known to serialise; nothing is said of worker threads. `v8.setFlagsFromString` after the engine has started "may result in unpredictable behavior".
  - The reader's API documentation (fetched 2026-10-20), document parameters: `isOffscreenCanvasSupported` (false in Node), `disableFontFace` (true in Node; `useSystemFonts` is forced off with it), `useWorkerFetch` (false in Node), `standardFontDataUrl`, and the canvas factory. The reader's own source for Node (the package's `legacy/build/pdf.mjs`, read on 2026-10-20): the block `if (isNodeJS)` requires the optional canvas package when the module is evaluated, before it tests `globalThis.DOMMatrix` and `Path2D`, so no document option can skip it. The reader's issue 21720 (closed 2026-08-06) records that the import fails when that optional package is absent.
  - Not verified: that the reference tool extracts text without a rendering device (its source could not be fetched).
  - Not verified: the practice of restarting a pooled worker after a number of requests, with a random spread so that the workers do not restart together, which process managers for web services document; the page could not be fetched.
- **Verified by**: `tests/pdf-text-host.test.ts` (the replacement starts after 80 documents with the warm-up request, takes no document while it starts, is given the next document after the old thread is retired, the old thread is retired at 200 when the replacement never reports, at 100 when none could be started, and a starting replacement is stopped with the pool), `tests/pdf-text-host-replacement.test.ts` (on real threads: 200 documents read the same text across the hand-over, two threads were started, the first one has ended; the warm-up page is read by `pdftotext` and by the reader line by line, the same as its source), `tests/pdf-text-thread-start.test.ts` (on real threads: pdf-lib is not loaded after a text-only document and is loaded by the first page with images; a replacement told to expect images has it loaded when it reports ready and one that is not told does not), `tests/pdf-text-host.test.ts` (the replacement is told whether the old thread has read a document that asks for images), `tests/ci-parity-workflow.test.ts` (the bundle is built for the checkout and for the base before the speed rows of a pull request and of the nightly jobs, and `build:worker` builds it for the image), `tests/pdf-text-host.test.ts` (the bundle starts with an empty `execArgv`, the source keeps the loader it registers; the old-generation threshold is set before each thread is made, given back when the thread is online, fails or ends, kept while another is still starting, and the heap limit stays 1 GB), `tests/pdf-text-host-threshold.test.ts` (on real threads: a probe thread builds 160 MB of live objects and reports how far in its first full collection came; with the default that is at 5 to 9 percent of the work, with the flag at 70 to 83 percent, and the test compares probes with each other because counts of collections depend on the host. A probe made while a text thread starts has the threshold, and probes made after the text thread is online are nearer the default than the flagged one), the quality rows of `document/pdf-text->txt` and `document/pdf-structure->docx` (unchanged: character error rate 0, word F1 1), and the throughput row `document/pdf-text->txt/throughput` of the parity bench.
- **Revisit when**: QA changes the isolation decision for the text thread, the reader stops requiring its canvas module at import or `image-input-limits.ts` is split so that sharp loads on demand (11 ms more off a start), a content-general warm-up beats the noise of the row (read documents with font programs bring the coefficient of variation of the calls from 0.28 to 0.35 down to 0.23 to 0.24 at best, which shows what is left, but a generated one measured within noise), QA accepts a code cache of the reader (25 ms of a start), the cause of the slower reader in the bundle is found, the reader loads its document and font tables in a way that a new thread can take from an immutable snapshot, or the pool takes documents so slowly that the idle replacement is stopped before it is used.

## 63. Flaky unit tests of the shard run: event-loop budgets, scaling ratios, temporary directories, big fixtures and the shard plan

- **Status**: Implemented (flake rows of issue #692, epic #763), decided 2026-10-20 (QA request); limits for programs, liveness checks and the numbering guard added 2026-10-23 (QA request).
- **Decision**:
  - **The event-loop budget cases measure CPU time of the loop thread, not the lateness of a timer.** A timer fires every 5 ms and the case records how much CPU time the thread used since the previous tick (`process.threadCpuUsage()`); the largest value is the longest stretch of work the loop did without turning. The bound is unchanged (`EVENT_LOOP_BLOCK_BUDGET_MS` plus one tick), the synchronous controls still have to exceed it.
  - **A scaling comparison takes extra passes whenever its ratio is over the bound** (up to `MAX_SCALING_PASSES` in all, inside the same time budget). Before, a ratio above 1.5 times the bound was refused after the first three passes as clearly super-linear.
  - **A test that lists a temporary directory before and after a conversion runs the conversion with a `TMPDIR` of its own.** The archive-copy check of the 7z route and the pass-directory checks of the two-pass media tests did not; the RAW decode test already did.
  - **The over-cap encrypted ZIP is written in one pass by a script that implements the traditional ZIP cipher**, not staged as 50,001 files for 7-Zip to pack. 7-Zip lists every entry and decrypts one, so the fixture is still read by an independent tool.
  - **The shard plan records durations from real runs.** Every `tests` shard writes a JSON report and uploads it as `test-report-<shard>`; `scripts/ci-test-shards.mjs --update` merges the reports, drops the entries of files that are no longer sharded, and a test fails when more than 5% of the sharded files have no entry.
  - **A test file that declares an oracle case has a default limit of 60 s** (`tests/helpers/cli-test-timeout.ts`, applied by `tests/helpers/oracle-test.ts` while the file's imports load). It covers the oracle cases and the plain cases around them, which reach the same programs through helpers. A limit the file sets itself afterwards, or a case names, replaces it. The limit stays finite, so a program that hangs still fails the case.
  - **Liveness of a process is asked of `isProcessRunning` / `waitUntilStopped`, never of a signal of 0.** A signal of 0 succeeds on a zombie. `tests/process-liveness-checks.test.ts` fails on `process.kill(pid, 0)` and `kill -0` in the test sources; 13 call sites in 10 files moved to the helper.
  - **A fixture does not outlive a timer the test races.** The process-state zombie fixture ends when its stdin closes, not after 5 s. The case of the job pool that needs a child too slow for a 1 ms deadline puts that job first, on a child that has converted nothing.
  - **The numbering of this document is checked on every pull request** by `scripts/guard-policy-decisions.mjs` (`npm run guard:policy-decisions`, a step of the `changes` job, which documentation-only pull requests also run): no two headings with one number, every heading has a row in the contents and every row a heading, no number twice in the contents, a row's title and link equal its heading's, rows in numeric order within a group. The committed document had 11 faults when the check was written (five entries without a row, a row for 29 that named entry 30, a stale title for 26, rows 49 and 46 out of order, a number twice).
- **Why**:
  - **Timer lateness measures the host.** On a 4-vCPU Linux machine with the thread pinned to two cores that 24 busy loops also used, the level-19 zstd case of the budget file failed 3 times in 8 (118.1, 107.7 and 130.9 ms against 105; the failure on the runner read 112.3), and 3 of 3 under 40 busy loops on one core (259.7, 156.4 and 251.8 ms). The same case through the CPU-time measure passed 30 of 30 under the first load, and 3 of 3 under the second. On the laptop the loop thread held the loop for 0.3 to 6.4 ms in every encode case; the synchronous encoders held it for 430 to 496 ms.
  - **A ratio of 13.09 (0.5 ms -> 7.0 ms) was final.** Work that grows linearly read 13x on a loaded runner; the helper refused anything above 12 (1.5 times 8) at once, so the best of three passes could not recover. The reader's work on 100 KB is 0.4 ms. The failure did not reproduce here: the old test passed 80 of 80 runs (8 busy loops; the 30 heaviest files running beside it), and about 100 measurements of the CPU-time ratio never passed 7.3. The cause of the inflated passes is therefore not known; the fix does not depend on it. Quadratic work stays over the bound on every pass (a reader made quadratic on purpose read 20.3x after the 12 passes), and the replay of the 13.2x case on a virtual clock fails without the change.
  - **Listings of a shared directory race.** A noise process that creates and removes `easyconvert-*.7z` or `easyconvert_pass_*` entries in the shared directory made the old checks fail 5 times in 5; with a private `TMPDIR` the same checks passed 30 of 30.
  - **The fixture's cost grew with its entry count.** Staging 50,001 files, packing them, rejecting the archive and removing the files took 8.6 s on an Apple-silicon laptop (staging 3.3, packing 2.0, extraction refusal 0.3, the `afterEach` removal 2.9) and 2 s on the test VM; on the CI runner the test took 24 s and its hook passed 10 s. The one-pass fixture takes 0.5 s on the VM and the hook removes one file.
  - **A flat second per unrecorded file unbalanced the shards.** 191 of 849 files (22%) had no entry; the six jobs of the last main run took 4:48 to 8:26. Weighed by their time on a machine of the runner's size, the heaviest shard is 1.19 times the mean (1.31 with the old table).
  - **The zombie fixture and the child-survival case were not reproduced.** 200 runs of the old fixture (100 idle, 100 beside 48 busy processes) and 92 runs of the child-survival check (12 as the test, 80 straight from a shell, 40 of them beside 48 busy processes) all passed. What is shown is the clock: with a 6 s stall of the test process, the old fixture's parent and zombie were both gone and the new one's both remained. The child-survival check is changed because its method (`kill -0`) cannot tell a zombie from a survivor, the failure main CI showed for the same method in #809.
  - **The peak-memory row is not changed.** The same 2000x2000 x 6 frame animation to GIF added 493.5 to 548.4 MiB over 10 runs on the test VM (mean 516; one run beside 16 busy processes included), against the 576 MiB bound; the runner measured 578.25 once. The spread of 55 MiB on one input is the allocator's, so the bound's margin of 64 MiB over the 512 MiB budget is thin, but nothing measured on a runner shows where it should be.
  - **Cases that start programs are slower than an idle measurement says by the factor of the host's load.** The zstd case of levels 1 to 19 (76 compressions) takes 1.2 s on the 4-vCPU test VM, 4.0 and 5.8 s beside 16 busy processes, and 16.7 s (twice) beside 48; it timed out at 5.35 to 5.41 s on the runner three times. The first EPUB case runs 13 xmllint processes and takes 66 ms on the VM (62 to 66 in 3 runs), 326 to 370 ms beside 16 busy processes, 865 to 974 ms beside 48 and 3.49 and 3.57 s beside 160; the runner's 5.5 s is 83 times the idle figure, which is a runner far busier than the 4 vCPUs of the test VM. Of 2,506 oracle cases run once, 1,579 named a limit and 927 did not; re-timed alone (12-core laptop, 2 workers, load 4.3), the slowest without one took 3.3 s (a presentation to PNG through the office suite), then 2.4, 2.3 and 2.2 s, so a third to two thirds of the default on an idle machine.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **CPU time of the loop thread does not see a block that is not CPU time**: a thread that blocks on a lock or on a synchronous read for 200 ms and uses no CPU is not caught. The routed encoders compute, and none of the cases waits on a lock.
  - **A failing scaling comparison takes up to 12 passes**, a few seconds more for a real regression, bounded by the existing 20 s budget.
  - **The 177 new duration entries are estimates**: one run on a 4-vCPU Ubuntu 24.04 machine, scaled by the ratio between that machine and the recorded CI numbers of the 658 files that have both (3.4, 2.1 and 2.0 by size; the ratio ranges from 1.0 to 4.5 for 80% of the 189 files compared that last 1 s or more on the runner). The reports of the next main run replace them. 14 files that were skipped or failed on that machine for want of tools keep the median.
  - **The duration of a file is the span of its tests** as the JSON report gives it; the time to import a file is not in it (116 s of import against 948 s of tests in a shard of the last run), so the table understates the files that import a lot.
  - The ZIP script covers one cipher and one entry shape; the AES case of 1,500 entries is still packed by 7-Zip.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Raising the 105 ms budget, the 8x bound or the hook timeout**: it hides a run that is slow for another reason and does not tell a blocked loop from a busy host.
  - **Retrying a failed test**: the failure is the information; a retry of a wall-clock comparison passes whenever the host is quieter than the first time.
  - **Running the timing files alone in a second pass of each shard**: 46 files use the timing helpers or the loop monitor (about 350 s of recorded time), a second pass would add about a minute to every shard, and the files that failed did so on a ratio or a delay that the changes above no longer read from the host. Spreading them one per shard was weighed too: the plan already puts the files of 20 s or more in different shards (2 to 6 per shard before, 2 to 4 now), and what a timing file competes with is the other test processes of its own shard, which a plan across shards cannot change.
  - **Time-stamping the tests in the log to refresh the table**: the default reporter prints no per-file time for a passing file; only the JSON report has it.
  - **A default limit for every test file** (`testTimeout` in the project configuration): 44 helpers start programs, so the default would be right for them, but it would also loosen 5 s to 60 s for the pure-computation files whose cases have no measured reason for it. Files that import an oracle helper without `oracleTest` (51 of them) are not covered; they are the next place to look if a plain case of such a file times out.
  - **Fewer xmllint runs in the EPUB case** (one query for all labels): it removes 10 of 13 runs, but the next case that queries a document in a loop would time out the same way; the limit covers the class.
  - **Waiting for the zombie's parent in the process-state fixture**: the parent is `sh` replaced by `cat`; no wait is needed once nothing on a timer decides whether the fixture exists.
- **Verified by**: `tests/oracle-test-timeout.test.ts` (an oracle case and a plain case in an oracle file get a limit of 30 to 120 s, and a limit named on a case is kept), `tests/process-liveness-checks.test.ts`, `tests/process-state.test.ts` (including `waitUntilStopped`), `tests/policy-decisions-numbering.test.ts` (each fault on a copy of the document, the command line, the CI step), `tests/event-loop-budget.test.ts` (13 cases; the old and the new measure side by side under load), `tests/timing-helpers.test.ts` (the 13.2x replay and the quadratic case on a virtual clock), `tests/archive-seven-zip-hostile-members.test.ts` and `tests/media-two-pass-options.perf.test.ts` (run with a noise process in the shared directory, 30 of 30), `tests/encrypted-zip-extraction-containment.test.ts` (30 of 30 on the laptop; 7-Zip lists 50,001 entries and decrypts the first) and `tests/ci-test-shards.test.ts` (the share of unrecorded files, the update that drops entries, the report step of `ci.yml`).
- **Revisit when**: a plain case of a file without `oracleTest` times out at 5 s while it starts programs (then a project-wide default is due), the allocator's spread on the runner is measured and the margin of the peak-memory case is set from it, a run of the `tests` job fails on the loop-thread measure (then the work is on the loop), the reports of a main run are available (replace the estimates with `--update`), the share of unrecorded files passes 5% again, or a test file needs a wall-clock bound that a CPU-time measure cannot express.

## 64. The bzip2 block sort: induced sorting with the suffix type in the array entries, and the bound the nightly run holds

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #676, epic #759), decided 2026-10-20 (QA approval). The trigger was the nightly run on Node.js 24 (entry 35): the guard "sorts a 900 kB block of repository source at least 2x faster than prefix doubling" read 0.65 to 0.66 on the CI runner (63 to 66 ms against 97 to 100 ms) because Node.js 24 made the prefix-doubling reference 2.1 times faster and the sorter 1.4 times. The bound stays at 0.5; the sorter got faster.
- **Decision**:
  - **The suffix array of the encoder is built by `bzip2-suffix-sort.ts`**, a linear-time induced sort (SA-IS) arranged the way the fastest implementations arrange it. The end of the text is a virtual sentinel, so a byte block stays a byte block and no symbol is spent on it. Every entry of the suffix array carries one more bit, the type of the suffix before it, which is known when the entry is written (from two neighbouring symbols that share a cache line); the induction passes read the text once per entry and need no array of types. The positions of the LMS suffixes are listed once, by the scan that finds them, so the stage that seeds the sorted LMS suffixes never rescans the text. The names of the LMS substrings are compared by length first.
  - **The transform is unchanged**: rotations are sorted as the suffixes of the block followed by its first 131 072 bytes, the order is checked (every two neighbouring rotations differ within their first 131 073 bytes), and a block that fails the check is sorted over its doubled text. A block that is a word repeated keeps the order of equal rotations of the earlier sort. The stream is byte-identical to the one before.
  - **Smaller changes around the sort**: the cyclic period of a block is found by comparing the block with itself shifted by n / q for each prime factor q of n (the border table is built only for a block that has a period); the neighbour check rolls a 16-bit window hash and keeps its tables in the workspace; the rotation list is built in place in the suffix array.
  - **The bzip2 block checksum** (CRC-32, polynomial 0x04C11DB7, most significant bit first, which `zlib.crc32` does not compute) folds eight bytes per step through eight tables (`bzip2-crc.ts`), 1.91 ms to 0.57 ms for a 900 kB block.
- **Why**:
  - **Where the time went** (Linux test VM, 4 vCPU, Node.js 24.22, one 899 981-byte block of repository source, per-step timers on; 55.3 ms before, 36.2 ms after): the induced sort was 47 ms of the block's 55. Its first level took 31 ms (types 2.7, LMS placement 3.6, the two induction passes of the first stage 7.6, gathering 1.4, naming 4.1, the second stage's placement and rescans 4.2, its two induction passes 7.6), the level below it 10.3 ms (the first reduced text has 319 thousand symbols and 25 thousand names) and the deeper levels 5.9 ms. Around the sort: the period 2.0 ms, the neighbour check 3.2, the rotation list 1.4, the last column 1.1. After the change the first level takes 19.1 ms (counts 0.7, types and seeding 3.0, first stage 5.9, gathering 0.6, naming 2.3, seeding 0.9, second stage 5.3), the second level 7.1, the deeper ones 4.7, and the rest 5.2 (neighbour check 2.8, rotation list 1.3, last column 0.9, text 0.1, period none). The whole encoder for that block (`compressBzip2`, best of 40) takes 51.8 ms after against 73.2 before; about 20 ms of that is not the sort (the first run-length scan, the checksum, and the move-to-front, run-length and Huffman coding of `encodeBlock`), which this change leaves as it was.
  - **What was measured for each technique** (the same block on an Apple-silicon laptop with Node.js 24.19, standalone, best of 40 to 100 interleaved runs; 54 ms before):
    | Change | Time after |
    |---|---|
    | Virtual sentinel, type bit in the entries, LMS list, no type array, no rescans (all levels) | 35 to 36 ms |
    | Period by prime factors, rotation list in place, window hash read from the sort's text, no copies | 31.5 ms |
    | 16-bit rolling window hash | 31.1 ms |
    | Imported constant read once per call instead of once per entry, in the test environment only (the module wrapper of the test runner reads an import through a getter on every use) | 35.7 to 31.1 ms in the test run |
    The headline, on the Linux test VM in the test run (ten runs each): the previous sorter took 0.79 to 0.85 of the reference's time, this one 0.45 to 0.49 (30.9 to 34.0 ms against 66.7 to 72.1 ms). The differential run of 3 823 inputs (164 MB) took the encoder 10.3 s against 15.1 s.
  - **Research** (sources read for this change; no code was copied):

    | Source | What it says | Use here |
    |---|---|---|
    | Nong, Zhang and Chan, "Two Efficient Algorithms for Linear Time Suffix Array Construction", IEEE Trans. Computers 60(10), 2011 | SA-IS: linear time, small workspace, by induced sorting from the LMS suffixes | The algorithm and its recursion on the names of LMS substrings |
    | libsais (Apache-2.0), README and benchmarks | The same algorithm made branch-free and cache-friendly; ranks marked in a sign bit; forward BWT in the suffix array's memory; its benchmark page reports 3.0 to 3.9 times the single-thread speed of divsufsort 2.0.2 on text | The type bit in the entries and the absence of a type array; the virtual sentinel. Prefetching and OpenMP do not carry over to a script |
    | Fischer and Kurpicz, "Dismantling DivSufSort", Prague Stringology Conference 2017 (arXiv:1710.01896) | A description of divsufsort, which they call the fastest known suffix sorter in main memory; no comparison with SA-IS in the abstract | Read for its treatment of the first characters; not adopted (a direct sort of the substrings needs a second algorithm for repeats) |
    | Olbrich, Ohlebusch and Büchler, "On the Optimisation of the GSACA Suffix Array Construction Algorithm" (arXiv:2206.12222) | A non-recursive linear-time sorter that the authors report faster than divsufsort | Not adopted: the benchmark is against divsufsort, and the induced sort already beats it in the libsais measurements |
    | Kounavis and Berry, "A Systematic Approach to Building High Performance, Software-based, CRC Generators", Intel, 2005 | Slicing: one table per byte of the stride | The checksum |
    | The bzip2 format (block sort of the rotations of the block) | The transform sorts rotations, not suffixes | Why the sort runs over the block and a copy of its start, and is checked |
  - **The sort is not the speed of the C libraries**: the libsais benchmark page lists 0.085 s for 10.2 MB of text (120 MB/s) and 0.106 s for the 4 MB Bible text with divsufsort 2.0.2 (38 MB/s), on another machine; this sorter needs 31 to 36 ms for 1.03 million symbols (the block and the 131 072 bytes that make it a rotation sort), about 30 MB/s. Prefetching, unchecked array access and 8-bit text are what a script lacks.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **Memory**: three 4-byte arrays of the sorted text's length (text, suffix array, LMS lists), 12.4 MB for the fast path of a 900 kB block and 21.6 MB for the fallback, plus 2 bytes per block byte for the window hashes. The earlier sort's two arrays held 8 bytes per symbol (8.2 MB and 14.4 MB), besides its type array, its rotation list and a 4-byte hash per block byte. The workspace is allocated once and reused for every block.
  - **A block with a repeat longer than 131 072 bytes** is still sorted twice (the check fails, then the doubled text): 70 ms for repository text with a 200 kB repeat (36 ms without it) and 51 ms for a Fibonacci block, against 128 and 118 ms before. Blocks of 899 981 bytes, VM, best of 3 to 8: repository text 56.1 to 35.7 ms, random bytes 62.8 to 44.6, two letters 68.3 to 43.8, a 1 000-byte unit repeated with one byte changed 90.7 to 45.5. A block of one repeated byte costs 4.0 ms (2.8 before) and period two 3.4 (2.7): the period test compares the block with itself shifted by n / q for each prime factor q until one matches, then builds the border table as before.
  - **The 16-bit window hash** matches two different prefixes once in 65 536 pairs; each wrong match costs one byte comparison that stops at the first difference. The budget of 24 compared bytes per block byte that bounds crafted collisions is unchanged.
  - **A hot loop must not read an import** in this repository's tests: the test runner's module wrapper reads an imported binding through a getter on each use (13 % on this sort). `rotationStarts` copies `POSITION_MASK` to a local first.
  - One more module (`bzip2-suffix-sort.ts`, 200 lines) and a second table set for the checksum.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **A shorter wrap length** (the first bytes appended to the block): 65 536 gains 1.6 %, 32 768 gains 8.7 %. In the 249 blocks of the 12 Silesia files and the ooffice tar (899 981 bytes each) the longest repeat is 31 101 bytes, so 32 768 would hold on that corpus, but every block with a longer repeat (a copied file in a tarball) would be sorted twice, for a gain of a few percent.
  - **A byte-sized first level** (an 8-bit text array for the first pass, 1 MB instead of 4 MB): 36.7 against 36.1 ms on the test machine, whose caches hold both. It would need the whole sorter twice (8-bit and 32-bit text, V8 compiles one body per element type). Revisit if a runner shows the induction passes bound by the cache.
  - **A WebAssembly sorter**: the repository assembles its modules by hand (`wasm-assembler.ts`, no C toolchain in the build), so a port of libsais or divsufsort would be hundreds of hand-assembled instructions, and the script version would stay as the fallback where WebAssembly is off, as the kernels of `wasm/xxh64.ts` have. The guard is met without it.
  - **divsufsort, GSACA, prefix doubling with a larger first radix**: a second algorithm for repeats, no gain over the induced sort in the published measurements, and the quadratic cases the induced sort does not have.
  - **The suffix order of the block with a sentinel as the transform**: not the rotation order, so the stream would differ from the earlier one and from the reference tool's.
  - **Lowering the bound** (0.5 on the nightly run): the guard measures a regression; the sorter was made faster.
  - Tried and dropped, no gain or slower: a loop bound of `sa.length` (32.4 against 31.8 ms), a branch-free compaction of the rotation list (+1.3 ms), a branch-free type scan (0.2 ms of 3).
- **Verified by**: `tests/bzip2-suffix-sort.test.ts` (every string up to 9 symbols over 3 symbols and up to 13 over 2, random texts over alphabets of 1 to 256 symbols, all-equal, periodic, ascending, descending, Fibonacci and Thue-Morse texts up to 2500 symbols with scratch arrays of exactly the documented size, the type bit of every entry, a 300 000-symbol text, scratch reuse; each against the definition of the order), `tests/bzip2-bwt-sizes.test.ts` (every size 2 to 64 and sizes from 65 bytes to a full block over seven shapes against the earlier sort and, where the rotations differ, the definition; full blocks of text, two letters and text with a repeat past the wrap length; `bzip2 -t` and `bzip2 -dc` on the streams), `tests/bzip2-bwt.perf.test.ts` (the bound, the 2x all-equal against random, linearity), `tests/bzip2-crc.test.ts` (the published check value 0xFC891918, every length up to 80, 1 MB blocks against the bit-by-bit definition), and the existing bzip2 interop and pin suites. A differential run of 3 823 inputs (every size 1 to 400 over nine shapes, 160 random sizes up to 1.9 MB, the block-size edges, the 12 Silesia files to 4 MB) gave the same SHA-256 for the earlier and the new encoder on every one, and `bzip2 -t` accepted every stream. The parity rows of the family on the VM (no `lbzip2` there, so `tar->tar.bz2` takes the in-process encoder), head against base in interleaved pairs: `mixed.tar->tar.bz2` 1.019, `silesia-xml.tar->tar.bz2` 1.005, `silesia-ooffice.tar->tar.bz2` 1.030, `mixed.bz2->tar` 1.007, `silesia-xml.bz2->tar` 1.014, `silesia-ooffice.bz2->tar` 1.024 (24 pairs each, base time over head time; the sort is a small part of a row that also reads, tars and starts workers), and the 290 quality rows of the family pass unchanged.
- **Revisit when**: a runner shows the induction passes bound by the cache (then an 8-bit first level), the sort needs more than one block at once on one core (then the workspace is the thing to share), a WebAssembly toolchain enters the build, or the nightly bound is re-measured on a runner where the reference is faster again.

## 65. Pictures on PDF, PostScript and EPS pages: the page follows the stored print density

- **Status**: Implemented (row "Found by #824" of issue #783, epic #760), decided 2026-10-20 (QA request in issue #783: a 400 dpi scan was a 3,307 x 4,677 point page where established tools write A4).
- **Decision**:
  - **A picture written as a PDF, PostScript (`ps`) or EPS page is as large as the picture says it is: pixels x 72 / dpi points on each axis.** The pixels are never resampled; only the page and the placement change, and the picture covers the whole page. Each page of a multi-page source (TIFF) follows its own resolution.
  - **The resolution is read from the file's own header, on each axis, as the format defines it**: TIFF `XResolution` and `YResolution` with `ResolutionUnit` (2 inch, which is also the default when absent; 3 centimetre), from the directory of the page; PNG `pHYs` with unit 1 (metre); JFIF density with units 1 (dots per inch) or 2 (dots per centimetre); the Exif `XResolution`/`YResolution` of a JPEG when its JFIF states none (the JFIF density wins when both state one); and the Exif block of a WebP, HEIF or AVIF as the image library hands it over. A source decoded by a command-line decoder carries its density in the PNG or TIFF that decoder writes, and is read from that (a PSD at 300 dpi is a 192 x 96 point page for 800 x 400 pixels).
  - **The density is kept to a tenth of a dpi** (a density in pixels per metre is a rounded figure: 2,835 is 72.009 dpi and gives a 72 dpi page).
  - **No stated resolution is one pixel to the point (72 dpi), as before**: no tag, a TIFF unit of "none" (1), PNG `pHYs` or JFIF units of 0 (an aspect ratio, not a length), a value missing for one axis, zero, negative or not finite, a classic-TIFF chain that cannot be read (BigTIFF and the sources below are not read), and exactly one pixel per millimetre (25.4 dpi), which the image library writes into a PNG or TIFF whose maker set none. A file without a density converts to the same bytes as before (PostScript and EPS, byte for byte; PDF, the same page size).
  - **A resized picture is its new pixel count at the stored density** (a request for half the width gives half the page), and the Exif orientation exchanges the two axes with the pixels of a picture turned a quarter turn.
  - **Bound**: a page longer than 14,400 points (ISO 32000-1 Annex C) is scaled evenly to 14,400 on its longer side, with the pixels untouched, in PDF, PostScript and EPS alike: one function (`withinPageLimit`) for the three writers and for the JPEG passthrough, which already had the rule. A PostScript interpreter has no such limit, but a TIFF stating 0.1 dpi would make a 2,000-pixel picture a page of 1,440,000 points that Ghostscript's bounding-box device cannot measure, and a stated density should never grow a page past what the PDF writer allows. A page under 3 points follows the density as the picture says it.
  - **The PDF of a JPEG that goes in unchanged reads the density the same way** (JFIF first, Exif when JFIF states none); it read the Exif resolution first.
  - **The image library's own `density` is not used.** Measured on this library: it holds one axis, rounds to a whole number, reports 76 for a PNG of unit 0 with a 3:1 ratio, and 7,620 for a TIFF of unit "none" at 300.
  - **Camera RAW states no print size, on every route.** A page written from a RAW file is one pixel to the point whether it came through the native decoder, the embedded preview or an in-process sensor decoder, and whether the target is PDF, PostScript or EPS. Two resolutions surround a RAW file and neither is read: the camera's own Exif figure in the container (144, 180, 72, 600 and 350 dpi in five of the fifteen samples), and the 300 dpi that the decoder writes into its TIFF (a constant: LibRaw's `dcraw_emu`, darktable and RawTherapee all write 300 whatever the camera says). The TIFF of the native decoder is made to state no absolute unit (`ResolutionUnit` 1, TIFF 6.0 section 8) before it is converted, the preview and the PDF route of a RAW file ignore the density they would read, and the sensor decoders have none to read. A camera's JPEG is not RAW: its Exif or JFIF resolution is read like any picture's.
  - **Not read, so 72 dpi**: BMP (the decoder in the repository keeps no density), BigTIFF, GIF (has none), and any container whose Exif block states none. The OCR searchable PDF keeps its own rule (the density a PNG or JPEG states, 300 dpi otherwise).
- **Why**: a PDF page and a PostScript page have a size, a picture has a print size, and the converters people compare with put the second on the first: the page of a 200 x 100 dpi picture is 288 x 288 points in all of them. Measured on this machine (pixels 800 x 400, page in points):

  | Stored density | Page here | ImageMagick | tiff2ps | img2pdf |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | TIFF 200 x 100 dpi | 288 x 288 | 288 x 288 | 288 x 288 | 288 x 288 |
  | TIFF 100 x 50 dots/cm | 226.77 x 226.77 | 226.77 x 226.77 | 226.77 x 226.77 | 226.77 x 226.77 |
  | PNG 300 dpi | 192 x 96 | 192 x 96 | - | 192 x 96 |
  | JPEG 100 x 50 dots/cm | 226.77 x 226.77 | 226.77 x 226.77 | - | 226.77 x 226.77 |
  | no density | 800 x 400 | 800 x 400 | 800 x 400 | 600 x 300 |
  | TIFF unit "none", 300 | 800 x 400 | 192 x 96 | 192 x 96 | 600 x 300 |
  | PNG unit 0, ratio 3:1 | 800 x 400 | 19,200 x 28,800 | - | 200 x 300 |
  | JPEG units 0, ratio 2:1 | 800 x 400 | 28,800 x 28,800 | - | 600 x 300 |

  A 400 dpi scan of A4 (the 3,307 x 4,677 pixels of the corpus file) is 595.26 x 841.86 points here, the A4 page it was scanned from. The last three rows are where this decision leaves the tools: a number that is not a length is not turned into one.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **Pages that were large are small, and the other way round**: a 600 dpi picture is a page 8.3 times smaller on each side than the one it was (a 2,400-pixel scan is 288 points). A viewer that opened it zoomed to the window now shows a small page at its own size; a printer prints it at its size, which is what the scan was for. A picture stated at 25 to 71 dpi (screen figures) gets a larger page than before.
  - **A resized picture shrinks the page.** A request for half the width of a 300 dpi picture gives half the page, as the reference tool does. A resize that kept the print size would raise the density by the same factor; no request has asked for it.
  - **A page can be under 3 points** (a 16-pixel icon at 1,200 dpi is 1.2 points); the pixels are there and the size is the picture's own. A picture with a density of 25.4 dpi is not honoured (one pixel per millimetre is the library's placeholder, so a genuine 25.4 dpi picture is a pixel to the point), and the aspect ratio of an aspect-only density is not applied: its picture is shown with square pixels.
  - **The same picture can have two density tags** (JFIF and Exif): the JFIF one wins. A JPEG whose Exif says 300 and JFIF 72 now gives a 72 dpi page; before, the PDF of the JPEG passthrough gave 300.
  - **A TIFF whose unit is "none" is 72 dpi**, as its definition says (no absolute unit), where ImageMagick and tiff2ps read the number as dots per inch.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Reading the density of a RAW file's preview or decoder TIFF**: the figure is the camera's or the decoder's, not the size of a print (the samples read 72 to 600 dpi, and every decoder's TIFF reads 300), and it would give one RAW file two page sizes depending on the route it takes (a 2,256-pixel ORF was 542 points through the native decoder and 2,256 through the sensor decoder). ImageMagick, which converts a RAW file to PDF or PostScript through darktable, writes the pixels at 72 dpi (2,256 x 1,684 points for the ORF); darktable's own PDF export lays the picture on an A4 page whatever the picture's size.
  - **96 dpi when nothing is stated** (img2pdf's default, a screen figure): a file without a density would change page size (800 pixels would be 600 points); 72 keeps the pixel-to-point reading this service had, ImageMagick's default.
  - **Resampling the pixels to a standard page**, or **always A4**: it changes the picture, costs time and quality, and makes a photograph or a receipt the size of a sheet it never was. The page follows the picture.
  - **The image library's `density`**: see the decision.
  - **Rounding the density to a whole dpi** (the figure img2pdf keeps): a density stored in pixels per metre is a rounded figure (2,835 is 72.009 dpi, 11,811 is 299.9994), so it is kept to a tenth of a dpi, which gives 72 and 300; a whole dpi would make 150.5 dpi a page 0.3 percent small.
  - **A user-unit scale for pages over 14,400 points** (img2pdf's option): readers that do not read `UserUnit` show the page small, and the PDF already scales the picture down for JPEG sources.
  - **Reading BMP pixels per metre**: the repository's decoder does not expose it, and the value is 2,835, 3,780 or 0 in practice depending on the program that wrote the file.
- **Sources** (fetched on 2026-10-20):
  - The img2pdf manual page: the size of an image on the page is controlled by the dpi of the input image, or the default of 96 dpi if unset or missing. https://manpages.ubuntu.com/manpages/focal/man1/img2pdf.1.html
  - The img2pdf source (`pil_get_dpi`, `get_imgmetadata`, `find_scale`): the dpi is rounded to an integer ("the 72.009 dpi problem"), a PNG of unit 0 is an aspect ratio, a TIFF of dpi 1 falls back to the tag, a page over 14,400 units is an error unless it is scaled by a user unit, and one under 3 is a warning. Pillow's JPEG plugin (`JpegImagePlugin`): JFIF units 1 and 2 give the dpi, the Exif resolution only when there is none from JFIF; its TIFF plugin: unit 2 and 3 give the dpi, a missing unit is 2, "none" is no dpi.
  - ImageMagick's density behaviour: a PDF has no resolution of its own, the page is the pixels at the density (72 by default); the project's forum thread https://jqmagick.imagemagick.org/discourse-server/viewtopic.php?p=52764 did not answer a fetch on that day, so the behaviour is the one measured above.
  - Measured on the RAW samples of `tests/fixtures/raw` (Ubuntu 24.04 testbed with darktable 4.6.1, RawTherapee 5.10 and ImageMagick 6.9.12; LibRaw's `dcraw_emu` on macOS): the TIFF each decoder writes states 300 x 300 pixels per inch: `dcraw_emu` for all six samples tried (ORF, RW2, CR2, NEF, ARW, DNG, whose cameras state 144, 180, 72, 600, 350 and nothing), darktable for the four it decoded (ORF, RW2, CR2, ARW) and RawTherapee for the two tried (ORF, RW2); `darktable-cli` to PDF is 595 x 842 points (A4) for both pictures tried; ImageMagick to PDF and to PostScript is 2,256 x 1,684 and 1,384 x 1,384 points for the ORF and RW2 (the pixel sizes). A JPEG whose only resolution is an Exif 300 dpi is 192 x 96 points for 800 x 400 pixels in ImageMagick and img2pdf alike.
  - TIFF Revision 6.0, section 8 (XResolution, YResolution, ResolutionUnit: 1 no absolute unit, 2 inch, 3 centimetre, 2 the default); JFIF 1.02 (units 0 an aspect ratio, 1 dots per inch, 2 dots per centimetre); PNG (ISO/IEC 15948) `pHYs` (unit 0 unknown, 1 metre); Exif 2.32 section 4.6.4; ISO 32000-1 Annex C.2 (14,400 units, the Acrobat limit for the largest page).
- **Verified by**: `tests/image-print-density.test.ts` (the stored tags of each fixture read back by exiftool; the page measured by Poppler for PDF and by Ghostscript for PostScript and EPS, as the page device and as the box of the ink: TIFF at 200 dpi, 200 x 100 dpi, in dots per centimetre, with unit "none", without tags and with a zero resolution; PNG at 300 dpi and unit 0; JPEG with JFIF in dpi and dots per centimetre, units 0, Exif only, units 0 with Exif, and JFIF with Exif; WebP and AVIF with Exif; the library's placeholder; a two-page TIFF with two densities, whole and by page selection; orientation 6 with two densities; a resize; the 14,400 limit in PDF, and in PostScript and EPS for a TIFF stating 0.1 dpi (page read by Ghostscript, aspect kept); the three pages of the 400 dpi corpus scan as A4 in PDF, PS and EPS; the PDF image keeps every pixel of the TIFF and the PostScript page drawn at the picture's density gives every sample back; a picture without a density keeps its bounding box and page device), `tests/image-print-density-reader.test.ts` (the reader on headers laid out by hand, every damaged or truncated header reading as "no resolution"; the TIFF rewritten to state no absolute unit), `tests/raw-print-density.test.ts` (five RAW samples whose cameras state 72 to 600 dpi to PDF, PS and EPS through the native decoder, three through the embedded preview, and the in-process sensor samples: the page equals the picture's pixel size read by LibRaw's identify tool or by the image library from the PNG of the same conversion), the real camera RAW cases of `tests/registry-engine-conformance.test.ts` (an EPS or PS bounding box equals the decoded size), `tests/image-multipage-postscript.test.ts` (the corpus scan drawn at 400 dpi equals libtiff's decode), and the existing JPEG passthrough tests.
- **Revisit when**: QA wants BMP, BigTIFF or the aspect ratio of an aspect-only density honoured, a request for a resize that keeps the print size comes in, the OCR searchable PDF should take the 72 dpi rule for a picture without a density, or the reference tool stops reading the density the way it does now.

## 66. Opus from a source at full scale: the rate change ffmpeg inserts, no gain or limiter

- **Status**: Implemented (nightly row `audio/edge-clipped-castanets.wav->opus/true_peak_shift` of issue #738), decided 2026-10-20 (QA request).
- **Decision**:
  - **A libopus encode that names no resampler and is not normalised gets no resampling filter of ours.** The rate change to 48 kHz is the one ffmpeg inserts when the rate is named (`-ar 48000`): swresample's default filter, the same call the reference command makes. The other lossy encoders (AAC, MP3, Vorbis, AC-3, WMA, AMR) keep soxr at 16 bits (entry 3); lossless and PCM outputs, normalised files and a caller who names soxr keep 28 bits.
  - **No gain, limiter or soft clip is applied to a source that goes over full scale.** A conversion does not change the level of what it is given; a true-peak ceiling is the caller's choice through `audio.loudness` (EBU R 128 targets, -1 dBTP by default).
- **Why**:
  - **The reference does not limit.** Its command is `ffmpeg -i <wav> -c:a libopus -b:a <rate>`. libopus takes 16-bit or float samples, so ffmpeg inserts an int16-to-int16 resample to 48 kHz, which saturates at full scale; no gain is applied and libopus 1.4 adds no headroom: its CELT front end clips only at +6 dBFS (65536 on the 16-bit scale), `opus_pcm_soft_clip` is a decoder-side helper, and `opusenc` passes float input through as it is.
  - **Stage by stage on the clipped castanets** (12 s, 16-bit, 44.1 kHz, the public track boosted by 18 dB; ffmpeg 6.1.1, libopus 1.4, libsoxr 0.1.3 as on the runner; true peak by the BS.1770 meter of `ebur128`, 4x oversampling): source sample peak 0.0 dBFS and true peak +5.2 dBTP; after the 48 kHz int16 resample the sample peak is 0.0 dBFS and the true peak +4.3 dBTP for swresample, soxr at 16 bits and soxr at 28 bits alike (5420, 5428 and 5436 full-scale samples); the same resample kept in float reads 5.2, 5.4 and 5.4 dBTP; after libopus at 96 kbit/s the decoded true peak is 4.5 dBTP through the reference chain (-0.7 against the source), 5.0 through soxr-16 (-0.2) and 5.1 through soxr-28.
  - **The 0.5 dB is the encoder's response to the exact samples, not the resampler's peak.** The three resampled files have the same true peak; their difference (swresample against soxr-16: 0.0031 rms on a signal of 0.170 rms; soxr-28 against soxr-16: 0.0004) moves the decoded peak by 0.5 dB. Over 32 draws of the same file (8 start offsets by 4 rates, 64 to 192 kbit/s) soxr-16 minus swresample averages -0.01 dB (standard deviation 0.25, range -0.8 to +0.5); on 27 draws of synthetic clipped bursts it ranges from -0.9 to +0.6. At 32 kHz the AAC, Vorbis and MP3 encoders show the same spread (up to 1.0, 0.8 and 0.2 dB). No resampler is better for the peak; a row that allows 0.3 dB over one fixed draw fails on the draws where the two inputs part.
  - **Feeding libopus what the reference feeds it is the only deterministic match.** With the same samples in, the decoded samples are identical (MD5 of the float decode equal, on the castanets and on four synthetic cases).
  - **A gain or limiter breaks the other rows.** At 96 kbit/s on the castanets, against the reference's true-peak shift / loudness shift / SNR of -0.70 dB / 0.20 LU / -3.22 dB: soxr-16 (before) -0.20 / 0.20 / -3.22; a 1.0 limiter in float before the int16 stage -2.80 / 1.70 / -3.94; gain -1 dB -0.20 / 1.00 / -3.66; gain -2 dB -1.20 / 2.00 / -4.24; gain -5 dB -4.30 / 5.00 / -6.29. The rows allow 0.3 LU and 0.5 dB, so none passes all three.
  - **No converter lowers the level of a file by itself.** EBU R 128 s1 and Tech 3343 put the ceiling at -1 dBTP in production and -2 dBTP before a data-reduction codec, as a true-peak limiter in the production chain; Tech 3343 names lossy coding as a cause of inter-sample peaks. LAME scales only on request (`--scale`) and only reports clipping (`--clipdetect`); `opusenc` and ffmpeg's libopus wrapper have no limiter; ffmpeg's AAC encoder scales a window only when its input exceeds 0.95 of full scale.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **Every Opus conversion that changes the rate moves, not only clipped sources.** On the audio family (Ubuntu 24.04 VM, 397 rows) 69 rows move, all of them Opus rows, to the reference's value: SNR in 26 rows by at most 0.20 dB, true peak in 9 by at most 0.5 dB, loudness in 4 by 0.1 LU, bit rate in 26 by at most 0.44 kbit/s (container overhead). `music.wav->opus` loses the margin soxr gave it (SNR 28.04 dB against 27.84, BD-rate -1.37%) and ties the reference. Rows that were above the reference by a draw are at it; the verdict is 0 failing in 397 where one failed.
  - **Opus is resampled with the 64 dB filter again.** The coding noise of Opus (25 to 40 dB below the signal) hides it; the 16-bit soxr filter was already a trade of precision for speed.
  - **The other lossy encoders keep the same exposure**: a clipped source can part from the reference by the spread above. No row measures it (the AAC rows keep the source rate), so they are left as they are.
  - **The baseline row `audio/sqam-49-speech-en-f.flac->opus/true_peak_shift` was re-recorded at the reference's value (QA-approved)**: ours -0.3 and delta -0.4 became ours 0.1 and delta 0 (the reference value of the CI runner). The earlier -0.4 was the resampler's scatter (standard deviation 0.25 dB); the encoder now gets the samples the reference gets, so the row equals the reference by construction. No other Opus row of the baseline moves beyond its tolerance.
  - **`resampler` in the result metadata still names the build's choice**, not the filter an Opus encode used.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **A limiter, soft clip or gain before the encoder**: fails the loudness and SNR rows (above) and changes the level of a file the caller did not ask to change.
  - **Detect sources that go over full scale and use swresample only for them**: needs the peak of the whole input before the encode. A separate `astats` pass takes 55 ms against 118 ms for the Opus encode of the 12 s file (46% more), and a sample peak at full scale also holds for every loud, unclipped master.
  - **swresample for every lossy encoder**: removes the exposure of the other encoders too, but changes outputs that nothing measures and gives up the 16-bit filter of entry 3 without a failing row to justify it.
  - **Loosening the row, or tracking it as a gap**: the row is right to ask for the reference's peak; what was wrong was feeding the encoder other samples.
- **Verified by**: `tests/media-opus-over-full-scale-peak.test.ts` (castanets-like clipped bursts at three levels and rates, and a normal level: the decoded true peak within the allowance of the reference command and the decoded samples equal; the source true peak measured by `ebur128`), `tests/media-encoder-defaults.test.ts` (Opus and WebM carry no filter of ours; AAC, MP3 and Vorbis keep soxr-16; lossless, an explicit soxr and a normalised file keep 28 bits) and `tests/media-audio-encode-overhead.test.ts` (Opus from 44.1 kHz decodes to the samples of the plain reference command); the audio parity rows (`--parity --family audio`: 0 failing in 397, from 1).
- **Revisit when**: a parity row for another lossy encoder fails by the same spread (move it into `REFERENCE_CONVERSION_ENCODERS`), the reference command resamples or limits differently, a perceptual metric (#738) shows the 16-bit soxr filter audibly better at the rates users pick, or a caller needs a true-peak ceiling without asking for loudness normalisation (then it is an option, not a default).

## 67. Page images of a document: batches of pages drawn by up to four Poppler processes at once

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, office row found by #830; epic #759), decided 2026-10-20 (QA request: a presentation or document to `png` or `jpg` is at least as fast per page as the reference's way of doing it, with the same pictures, and no 85-slide deck is near a time limit). It adds to entry 44 (the page limit and the stage limit of the same chain); both limits are unchanged.
- **Decision**:
  - **The pages of a PDF that becomes `png`, `jpg` or `tiff` are drawn by several `pdftoppm` processes at once.** The pages are cut into batches (7 pages at 150 dpi for PNG, the batch size of the raw-page bound in `ppmBatchPages`; 8 pages for JPEG and TIFF, which Poppler writes itself), and at most `pageRenderProcesses()` batches run together: the cores this process may use (`os.availableParallelism()`, which follows the container's CPU quota), at most 4 (`PAGE_RENDER_MAX_PROCESSES`). A batch that finishes lets the next one start, so slow pages do not leave cores idle. The batch that starts before the page count is known is one of the places.
  - **A failed batch ends the render the way the serial loop ended it**: no batch starts after it, the batches in flight are awaited (nothing runs over a job directory that is about to be removed), and the first failure is reported with its type.
  - **A batch reads and removes only the raw pages of its own pages.** Until now it listed every raw page file in the job directory, which was right while one batch ran at a time and would encode another batch's half-written file now.
  - **Nothing else changes**: the same `pdftoppm` command lines per batch, the same sandbox options for each process, the PPM-to-PNG encoder and its pixel density chunk, the file names, the page order of the ZIP, the page limit of 500 and the stage limit of 120 s. A document of one batch (7 pages or fewer at 150 dpi) runs as before.
- **Why**:
  - **Where the time of `govdocs1-000728.ppt` (85 slides) went** (Linux test VM, 4 vCPU, arm64, LibreOffice 24.2.7, Poppler 24.02, one run with a stage log around each tool):

    | Stage | Before | After |
    |---|---|---|
    | Office suite, PDF export (warm profile) | 0.53 s | 0.53 s |
    | Page count (`pdfinfo`) | 5 ms | 5 ms |
    | Rasterising 85 pages (`pdftoppm`) | 12 serial runs of 7 pages at 5.7 s, one of 1 page at 0.84 s: 69.3 s | 3 waves of 4 runs at 6.0 to 6.3 s, one page at 0.9 s: 19.7 s |
    | Encode to PNG, ZIP (16 MB) | about 1.9 s, mostly overlapped | 0.7 s after the last run |
    | Total | 71.7 s (three runs: 71.9, 71.7, 72.2) | 21.4 s (three runs: 20.5, 21.0, 21.4) |

    The slides are not heavy because of pictures (there are none) but because the suite writes the background gradient as 128 stacked full-page rectangles: every page costs 0.8 to 1.0 s, in proportion to the pixel area (0.05 s at 36 dpi, 0.19 at 72, 0.81 at 150, 3.27 at 300). The CI runner took 141.9 s for the same deck. One `pdftoppm` run uses one core.
  - **How many processes** (same VM, the 85 pages in ranges of 7, `xargs -P`): 1 process 69.5 s, 2 35.6 s, 3 24.1 s, 4 18.3 s, 6 19.1 s. The gain is linear to the core count and stops there, which is why the count is the cores, at most 4.
  - **What established pipelines do** (read for this change; no code copied):

    | Source | What it says | Use here |
    |---|---|---|
    | The Python binding to Poppler that most converters of PDF to pictures use (`pdf2image`, `convert_from_path`, https://github.com/Belval/pdf2image/blob/master/pdf2image/pdf2image.py) | `thread_count` (default 1, never above the page count) splits the pages into contiguous ranges and starts one `pdftoppm` or `pdftocairo` per range with `-f` and `-l`, all at once | The technique: independent processes over page ranges. Here the ranges are bounded batches pulled by the free processes, because the cost of a page differs and the raw pages of a batch are bounded |
    | Poppler 24.02 `pdftoppm -h` | No option for threads or parallel pages: Splash draws a page on one thread | Parallelism has to come from outside the program |
    | MuPDF `mutool draw` (https://mupdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/tools/mutool-draw.html) | `-T` threads work in banded mode (`-B`) within one page | Parallel inside a page; its output differs from Poppler's, and it is AGPL (a separate process would be allowed, entry 11), so it would change the pictures. Not installed on the test VM, so not timed |
    | Ghostscript (https://ghostscript.readthedocs.io/en/latest/Use.html, "Improving performance") | `-dNumRenderingThreads` renders bands of a page in threads; for PDF the interpreter may start at `-dFirstPage` | The same two shapes (bands in a page, ranges of pages); the page pictures are not Poppler's either |
    | LibreOffice, community answers (https://ask.libreoffice.org/t/batch-conversion-from-odp-to-png/21556, https://ask.libreoffice.org/t/how-to-convert-a-specific-ppt-page-to-jpg/39969) | `--convert-to png` writes the first slide only; every slide needs a macro run per document, or the PDF export followed by a PDF rasteriser | The chain stays office suite to PDF to `pdftoppm`; the suite's own PDF is the only output that holds all pages from one layout. The suite is not the cost: 0.5 s here, with a warm profile of the pool |
  - **The reference's way is one `pdftoppm` run over the PDF the suite wrote** (the benchmark's reference for `pptx->png` and `xlsx->png`). That run takes 83 to 84 s for this deck (with Poppler's PNG writer) against 21 s now; the pictures are the same pixels.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **CPU**: the work is the same, spread over up to 4 cores for the length of a render. Under load (`WORKER_CONCURRENCY` jobs at once on the container's quota) the processes share the cores, so a job is as slow as before, not slower; one more process start per batch costs 5 to 10 ms of the 50 to 100 ms a batch of text pages takes.
  - **Memory**: up to 4 batches in flight: at most 4 x 48 MiB of raw pages on the job's scratch disk (tmpfs, counted in the container's memory) and the same in the encoder, where one batch held 48 MiB; 3 more `pdftoppm` processes of about 32 MB each. The largest process (the worker, 355 MB before and 360 MB after for the 85 slides) did not change measurably. The bound does not depend on the page count.
  - **A document of 7 pages or fewer is drawn by one process**, because the first batch starts before the page count is known: a deck of 7 gradient slides still takes 6 s. The benchmark's 3-slide deck and the workbook are such documents and did not move (see below).
  - **Text documents gain less**: a 128-page text document goes from 5.5 s to 4.6 s and a 208-page one from 5.3 s to 4.2 s, because the drawing of their pages is 10 ms a page and the rest is the ZIP: Deflate over 36 MB of PNG pages takes 1.9 s (PNG pages of text still shrink 9 to 16 percent in it). Storing them would save 1.8 s and make the ZIP 10 percent larger; that is a size and speed choice of its own and is left as it was.
  - The order in which two batches write their files is not the order of the pages, and neither is the order the processes start in; the ZIP is built from the page numbers in the file names, as before.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **`pdftocairo -png` instead of `pdftoppm`**: 17.1 s serial for the 85 slides, 4 times faster per page on this deck, but a different rasteriser: PSNR against the Splash pages of 22 to 26 dB (anti-aliasing, gradients), so every picture would change. Quality equal to the reference's `pdftoppm` is the bar.
  - **Banded rendering inside a page** (the `mutool` and Ghostscript shape): Poppler has none.
  - **A pool of long-lived rasteriser processes**: the process start is 5 to 10 ms of 800 ms for these pages and 50 to 100 ms a batch for text; a pool would hold documents from different users in one process, against per-job isolation (QA decision of 2026-10-19).
  - **More than 4 processes, or a count from the load**: 6 processes were no faster than 4 on 4 cores; the container quota is already read by `os.availableParallelism()`.
  - **Changing the PDF the suite writes** (fewer gradient steps): the suite's page is the reference for the picture; the stacked rectangles are what it draws.
  - **Starting a batch per page of a short document after the count**: it would fix a 7-slide gradient deck and cost a process start per text page; not done without a document that needs it.
- **Verified by**:
  - **Tests** (`tests/poppler-ppm-png.test.ts`, red before the change): 40 PNG pages and 40 JPEG pages are drawn by `min(pageRenderProcesses(), batches)` processes at once (the stand-in for `pdftoppm` counts the runs alive at each start: 1 before, 4 after), every PNG page equals the reference page pixel for pixel in its place and every JPEG page equals the bytes `pdftoppm -jpeg` writes; the limit, its failure rule (nothing starts after a failure, the batches in flight finish, the first failure is reported) and the place held by the early batch; a batch leaves the raw pages of another batch alone. `tests/document-page-images.perf.test.ts` (nightly): the 85 slides equal the pages `pdftoppm` draws from the suite's PDF, in order and pixel for pixel, in less than 0.6 of the time that one run takes (before: 72.3 s against 84.0 s, red; after: green).
  - **Output equal to before**: the ZIP entries of five real files are byte-identical before and after (85-slide deck as PNG and JPEG, a 64-slide deck as PNG and JPEG, a 34-slide pptx, a 128-page doc, a 208-page rtf), and the pages of the 85-slide deck, the 128-page doc and the 208-page rtf have 0 differing pixels against `pdftoppm -png` of the suite's PDF (ImageMagick `compare -metric AE`). Two pages (one of the 64-slide deck, one of the 34-slide pptx) differ from that oracle in both versions because the worker's PDF export passes the JPEGs of the page through and the oracle's default export does not.
  - **Speed against the base on the VM** (interleaved, whole conversion, PNG unless noted): 85-slide deck 71.9 s to 20.5 s (3 runs each; 0.29), as JPEG 70.9 s to 21.6 s; 64-slide deck 13.1 s to 5.4 s (as JPEG 12.6 s to 4.2 s); 34-slide pptx 4.9 s to 3.7 s; 128-page doc 5.5 s to 4.6 s; 208-page rtf 5.3 s to 4.2 s.
  - **Parity run, family `document`, on the VM** (without epubcheck, which the VM lacks): quality 268 rows evaluated, 0 failing; `drawingml-shapes-presentation.pptx->png` word recall 0.82979 and `workbook.xlsx->png` 0.97041, both equal to the reference. Speed against the base, 13 rows, 0 failing: `drawingml-shapes-presentation.pptx->png` head against base 1.0136 (18 pairs, upper bound 1.0888), `workbook.xlsx->png` 0.99768 (18 pairs, upper bound 1.0323); both are one batch, so they do not move by design.
- **Revisit when**: a document of 7 pages or fewer turns out slow (then the first batch is cut once the count is known), the worker's CPU quota or `WORKER_CONCURRENCY` changes so that 4 processes per job oversubscribe the cores, the ZIP pass is judged for page images (store or a parallel Deflate), or a rasteriser with threads inside a page and the same pictures as Poppler's is available.

## 68. ZIP packages: the type comes from the container, never from bytes inside an entry

- **Status**: Implemented (this change). Replaces the substring scan of the first 4096 bytes in the signature check.
- **Decision**: the signature check (`sniffMimeTypeFromMagicBytes`, which the spoof check of every upload and the output check of the real-file run use) tells a ZIP file's package type from its local file headers (ZIP APPNOTE 4.3.7: 30 bytes, name length at offset 26, extra length at 28), and from nothing else:
  - **A first entry named `mimetype`** whose stored data is a known media type decides: `application/epub+zip`, `application/vnd.oasis.opendocument.text`, `.spreadsheet`, `.presentation` (the template, master and web variants, `text-template` and the like, are their family's), and `application/hwp+zip`. The text is read as ASCII and trimmed. A different media type, a compressed `mimetype`, one longer than 256 bytes or one cut off is no decision; the check reads on.
  - **Otherwise the first entry under a main-part folder**: `word/`, `xl/` or `ppt/`, in the order of the file. The folders are those the three applications keep their main part in; the main part itself is named by the `officeDocument` relationship in `_rels/.rels`, which sits in a compressed entry and is not read here.
  - **Otherwise** an entry under `Contents/section` is HWPX, and anything else is `application/zip`, which every ZIP name accepts.
  - **Bounded work**: the headers are read from the start of the first 64 KiB (`HEADER_WINDOW_BYTES`, what the server reads of an upload) and at most 64 entries, so a whole file and its first 64 KiB give the same type and a ZIP of ten thousand entries costs the same as one of ten. An entry whose size is in a data descriptor (general purpose bit 3, APPNOTE 4.4.4) is skipped by finding the descriptor with its signature whose compressed size is the distance from the start of the data (APPNOTE 4.3.9); bytes of the data that spell the signature do not match unless the sizes agree, and a descriptor that is not found ends the walk. Nothing is read past the window, and a cut, oversized or hostile header ends the walk with the entries read so far.
  - **The CorelDRAW rule reads further than the type check.** `.cdr` uploads are checked by their own rule (`ownSignatureVerdict`), which looks for the entry `content/riffData.cdr` or `content/root.dat` (libcdr `CDRDocument::isSupported`) with the same header walk but its own bounds: the first 1024 entries inside the first 16 MiB, where the type check takes 64 entries inside 64 KiB. A CorelDRAW package keeps one data entry per page before `content/root.dat` (in the two corpus samples, `office-pb-poi.cdr` has 14 entries and the marking entry 6th, 20 KB into the file, and `lo-fdo65220-2.cdr` has 8 entries and the marking entry first), so a document of a few hundred pages needs more than 64 entries; the type check's bounds refused a package of 100 entries whose marking entry was the 101st and first entry was not in a CorelDRAW folder. A walk that ends at either bound is read as the walk that ran out of bytes always was: a first entry in `content/`, `metadata/` or `color/` passes, any other first entry does not.
  - **An extra field in the `mimetype` header is accepted.** The container specifications forbid one (EPUB OCF 3.3 section 4.3.3, OpenDocument 1.3 part 2 section 3.3); a package written by a tool that adds a timestamp field is still the package it says.
- **Why**: bytes in the first 4096 of a ZIP file are mostly entry data, deflated or stored, and any three characters appear in them. The scan found `xl/`, `word/` or `ppt/` in the compressed chapter of an EPUB written by this service (the nightly real-file run, `govdocs1-000555.html` to EPUB: "output bytes do not match epub"), and it ranked those folder names above the explicit media type, so the same mistake could refuse a real EPUB, ODT, ODS or ODP upload under its own name. Names in the headers are what the formats define: the container specifications put the media type in the first entry, and Office Open XML puts its parts in folders (ECMA-376 part 2 / ISO/IEC 29500-2 sections 7.3.3 to 7.3.7; the entry `[Content_Types].xml` is the media types stream). The established detectors read the same things: libmagic matches the `mimetype` entry at its place in the first header and the folder at the start of the name of the first entry and of the entries that follow, and Apache Tika reads the entry named `mimetype` and the entry names. The old rule also treated an EPUB with an extra field in the `mimetype` header as a plain ZIP, and an Office file whose folder lies after the first 4096 bytes as one too.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - A package whose marking entry is not within the first 64 entries or 64 KiB, or whose `mimetype` is compressed, sniffs as plain ZIP. Plain ZIP passes under every ZIP name, so such a file is accepted as before, but a spoof of it under another package name is no longer caught by the type (an Office file with a 1 MB picture as its first entry, declared as `.epub`).
  - A data descriptor is found only with its optional signature; an entry sized by one without it ends the walk there and the file is plain ZIP. One corpus file, a fuzzer case (`office-spreadsheet-deep-data.xlsx`, first entry cut short), moved from spreadsheet to plain ZIP for this reason; its verdict under `.xlsx` did not change.
  - An archive of ordinary files with a top-level folder named `word`, `xl` or `ppt` still sniffs as that Office type, as it did and as libmagic does; it passes under `.zip` because every ZIP name accepts every ZIP package type that is not another package's.
  - A CorelDRAW package with more than 1024 entries before its marking entry, or with the marking entry past 16 MiB, passes only when its first entry is in a CorelDRAW folder (every package the corpus holds starts in `content/`); one that starts elsewhere is refused under `.cdr`. A ZIP of more than 1024 entries with no marking entry whose first entry is in a CorelDRAW folder is passed, because the rest of it was not read; with 100 entries it is refused, as before the walk was bounded. The byte bound exists for the one scan that costs by the byte (the data descriptor of an entry sized by one): 141 ms for 64 MiB of the byte `P`, 44 ms at the bound.
  - The walk costs about 1 microsecond on a document (0.4 to 1.3 before), 6 microseconds on a ZIP of ten thousand entries (3 before) and, in the worst case that can be built (a 64 KiB entry of the byte `P` sized by a descriptor that is not there), 70 microseconds.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Keeping the substring scan and reordering it** (media type first): `mimetypeapplication/epub+zip` still fails for a header with an extra field, and an ODT or ODS whose first bytes are not the `mimetype` entry is read from its data. Any scan of bytes can be made to match by what the document says.
  - **Parsing the central directory**: it lies at the end of the file, and the server reads the first 64 KiB of an upload; reading it would need the whole file or a second read for every upload, and a central directory is the part a damaged file loses.
  - **Reading `_rels/.rels` and `[Content_Types].xml` to find the main part** (the full OPC rule, which Apache Tika follows by opening the package): inflating entries to answer a question of a name check, with the size limits that needs, for a gain only on packages with an unusual layout.
  - **Requiring `[Content_Types].xml` for the Office types**: the entry may lie after the entries read, and a file without it is a damaged package that the readers report better than the signature check does.
