- **Verified by**: `tests/zip-package-sniffing.test.ts`, red before the change (27 cases) against packages written by Python `zipfile` and by Info-ZIP `zip` (`tests/fixtures/zip-packages/generate.py`; libmagic's `file --mime-type` 5.41 names the same type for the EPUB, OpenDocument text, spreadsheet and presentation and Office Open XML packages; it calls the EPUB with extra fields a ZIP, HWPX a ZIP, and names the OpenDocument template and drawing by their own types, which this check keeps as the text family and plain ZIP): an EPUB, ODT, ODS, ODP and ODT template whose data spells the other folders, a package whose first entry is not `[Content_Types].xml`, packages with data descriptors, an Info-ZIP EPUB with and without extra fields, a plain ZIP, the packages of other tools in `tests/fixtures` (docx, xlsx, pptx, ods, epub, hwpx, odg), every cut of every package, noise after the signature, ten thousand entries, hostile sizes and a whole file against its first 64 KiB; and `tests/office-source-signatures.test.ts` for the CorelDRAW rule that shares the header walk with its own bounds (red before the bounds: a package written by Python `zipfile` with the marking entry 101st was refused, and one of 100 entries in a CorelDRAW folder with no marking entry was passed; also the two corpus samples, 20 000 tiny entries and a marking entry after a large entry). The real-file corpus: of the 658 ZIP files in the cached corpus none changed verdict under its own name (10 changed type: 9 now named by their folder where the first 4096 bytes had none, 1 damaged file now plain ZIP), and 284 conversions to EPUB, ODT, ODS, ODP, DOCX, XLSX, PPTX, ZIP and HWPX judged the same except `office-slideshow-embeddedaudio.pptx` to ODP, which the old check refused as "output bytes do not match odp" and now passes.
- **Revisit when**: a writer in the corpus puts the marking entry past the 64 entries or the first 64 KiB (past 1024 entries or 16 MiB for `.cdr`), or writes descriptors without the signature (then the walk reads on by the central directory of a longer window, or the descriptor without signature is matched by its crc and sizes); the upload path reads more than 64 KiB of a file; or a package type is added whose main part is not under `word/`, `xl/`, `ppt/` or a `mimetype` entry.

## 69. HTML tables the office suite draws past the page: nowrap before a nested table, widths beyond the page edge and sections around tables

- **Status**: Implemented (HTML to PDF row of issue #783, found by #811 on `govdocs1-001742`; epic #760), decided 2026-10-23 (QA request). It extends the staging of entry 51 and the anchors of entry 55.
- **Decision**: three changes in the HTML the office suite is handed (`src/lib/conversions/html-native-staging.ts`); each is made only where the page has the layout that loses words, and no word, link target or style is removed.
  - **A `nowrap` cell followed by a cell that starts with a table is written without `nowrap`.** The cell that starts with a table is the next cell of the row, whether the table is its first element or comes after white space, a left-out element or an unwrapped element such as `<noindex>`. A `nowrap` cell before a cell that starts with text, a paragraph or anything else keeps it, and so does the last cell of a row.
  - **A table with a fixed `width` above 718 px is written with `width="718"`.** 718 px is the width from the left margin to the right edge of the page the suite sets an HTML page on (A4, 794 px at 96 dpi, less the 2 cm left margin of 76 px). A table of 718 px or less, a percentage, an empty value, `0`, `auto` and anything that is not a plain number of pixels (`1e3`) are written as they are.
  - **A floating table (`align="left"` or `"right"`) is bounded by the width of the page, 794 px, not by 718 px.** The suite moves a floating table left, past the left margin if it must, until it ends on the page, and does not split it across pages. A floating table of 794 px or less keeps its width; a wider one is written at 794 px.
  - **The `id` of a `div` or `center` that is in a table cell, or that has a table inside it, is not written, unless a style sheet of the page names it (`#menu { ... }`, matched as a whole identifier).** The named anchor that entry 55 gives such an element stays, so a link to it still lands on its text. A `div` or `center` that is outside tables and holds none, and every other element, keeps its `id`.
- **Why**:
  - **Where the words of `govdocs1-001742` went** (Linux test VM, LibreOffice 24.2.7; the page is a record list: a 754 px table, a `width="100%"` table of rows `<tr valign="top">` with the cells `#` (`nowrap`), date (`nowrap`), title (`width="35%"`, a table inside), author (`width="30%"`) and format, and divisions with an `id` beside it). The suite sets the table in the title cell by its widest line when a `nowrap` cell comes before it, so the cell is as wide as the longest title (about 700 px), and the author and format cells after it are pushed past the right edge of the page, where nothing is drawn. The 754 px table is wider than the page edge, and a division with an `id` makes a section of the cell, which is cut off at the end of the page it starts on (the text of rows 4 to 10 was on no page). Poppler found 217 words in the 2-page PDF of a staged page that parse5 reads as 457 words; it finds 466 on 3 pages now, with none outside the page box (the 21 words not matched one by one are punctuation tokens such as `|` and `(61)` that the PDF text splits, and every letter and digit of the staged page is in the PDF text, 2 tokens aside). Each change is needed: with any one of the three left out, the page still loses words (nowrap left in: 160 tokens missing and 3 words outside the page; the width left alone: 16 tokens missing and 4 words outside; the `id` left in: 238 tokens missing).
  - **What the standards and the suite's own import say** (read for this change; no code copied):

    | Source | What it says | Use here |
    |---|---|---|
    | CSS 2.1, section 17.5.2.2, automatic table layout (https://www.w3.org/TR/CSS21/tables.html#auto-table-layout) | A table with a width is as wide as the greater of that width and the minimum width its columns need; nothing in the section narrows it to its containing block; the content of a cell may not overflow the cell, so the minimum width of a cell is the widest unbreakable part of its content | Why a fixed width or a long unbreakable line widens the table instead of wrapping; the page has to bound it |
    | HTML Standard, rendering, tables (https://html.spec.whatwg.org/multipage/rendering.html#tables-2) | The `width` attribute of `table`, `td`, `th` and `col` maps to the CSS `width`; `td[nowrap]` and `th[nowrap]` map to `white-space: nowrap` | `nowrap` is a no-wrap rule for the cell, so its minimum width is its longest line (the suite's layout of it is the measurement below) |
    | CSS Text Level 3, `overflow-wrap` and `word-break` (https://www.w3.org/TR/css-text-3/#overflow-wrap-property) | A string with no break opportunity may be broken at any point only when `overflow-wrap: anywhere` or `break-word` (or `word-break: break-all`) is set, and only where wrapping is allowed | The standard way to let a long string break, but the suite does not apply it: with `td { overflow-wrap: anywhere }` or `td { word-break: break-all }` a 160-character string in a 300 px table ends at 598.2 pt, past the 595.3 pt page, as without the style (24.2.7) |
    | The office suite's HTML import, `sw/source/filter/html/htmltab.cxx` (24.2 branch of its public source tree) | The `width` of a table is read as a number, and only a percentage above 100 is limited (to 100); a pixel width is not limited; `nowrap` of a cell is passed to the layout of the table; `col` and `colgroup` widths are read as numbers and are not limited either | A pixel width beyond the page is kept by the suite, which is why the page has to be written with a width that ends on the page |

  - **Measured on the suite** (24.2.7, synthetic pages and the real pages below): a row whose cell holds a table and whose earlier cell has `nowrap` is as wide as the longest line of that table, so the cells after it lose their words (a 30-word title line, and the same row with two `nowrap` cells and percentage widths); a table at `width="800"` or `750` loses the words that lie past the page edge, which is where a table of more than 718 px ends; a `div` or `center` with an `id` in a table cell is drawn as a section, and a row that holds a section is not split across pages (it is cut off at the page end); the `id` of a `div` that a style sheet names is a style hook, so that one stays. A table that is 718 px wide or less runs over the 17 cm text area (642 px) but ends on the page, which is why the width is cut to the page edge and not to the text area: clamping to the text area (or to `100%`) fixed more pages but changed 21 more pages that rendered correctly, and squeezed some to a narrow column (`govdocs1-000451` went from 6 to 13 pages).
  - **Why a floating table has its own bound** (24.2.7): a floating table of 765, 794, 800 and 900 px with a right-aligned 30-word line is moved left to end on the page; up to 794 px every word is on the page, and at 900 px four words are not. `govdocs1-001401` holds two floating tables of 765 px. Written at 718 px, the article column grows taller, and the suite, which does not split a floating table, cut its last line (`gateway to North and South America`) at the foot of page 2, 6 words more than before. Every floating table above 718 px in the corpus is 720 to 765 px wide, so none is narrowed now.
  - **The 450 saved pages of the real-world sample (govdocs1), converted to PDF by the office suite on the VM before and after** (the same two pages, `govdocs1-002000` and `-002047`, are refused before and after for `&` in a style sheet, entry 51; `govdocs1-001429` is left out of the counts, see the trade-offs). Two counts are read, both against the letters-and-digits words of the staged page as parse5 reads it, each word counted once: the words the PDF text (`pdftotext`) lacks, and the words that are not inside the page box (`pdftotext -bbox`: missing from the PDF, or drawn past an edge). Of the 447 pages compared, the staged HTML of 144 changes and the PDF text of 130 (26 of them have another page count). The words the PDF lacks fall from 70,578 to 48,212, and the words not inside the page from 61,629 to 38,642. The pages that lack more than 100 words fall from 50 to 34, and those that lack more than 1,000 from 14 to 7. The pages with a word past the page edge fall from 55 to 37 (the words from 2,259 to 2,165). 54 pages have more of their words inside the page (23,009 words), and 4 have fewer (19 + 3 words, see the trade-offs). Of the 165 pages that were complete before, 23 have another layout and all 23 are still complete. Pages that were cut at a page break come back whole (`govdocs1-000209`: 4,553 words were on no page, 3 pages before, 15 after; `-001413`: 6,544, 3 pages before, 19 after).
  - **What each rule adds, measured by leaving it out of the final staging over the same 447 pages** (words inside the page box): the width rule keeps 971 more words on 32 pages and costs 3 on one page (`govdocs1-002120`); the section rule keeps 22,044 more on 26 pages and costs 19 on three (`govdocs1-000458` 16, `-001412` 2, `-001419` 1). Leaving the nowrap rule out loses `govdocs1-001742` (160 tokens).
  - **The loop of `govdocs1-001120` is another cause.** The page (145 nested tables) converts to 18 pages with every word in 45.6 s before and after; its staged HTML does not change (none of the three layouts occurs in it) and the PDF is the same, so the 45 s is the suite's layout of deeply nested tables and stays a row of issue #783.
  - **The parity run** (family `document`, quality rows, the VM, without epubcheck, which the VM lacks): 268 rows evaluated, 0 failing, and the 268 result lines are the same before and after, with the 3 HTML to PDF rows (`doc-gov-000429` word recall 0.99508 against the reference 0.99836, `-000824` 1 against 0.97846, `-001709` 0.9906 against 0.9906).
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **A layout the three rules cannot see still loses words.** 37 pages still have words outside the page and 34 pages still miss more than 100 words: floated tables (`align="right"`), cells of fixed widths that add up beyond the page, and sections that a style sheet names. They keep the behaviour they had; the next row of issue #783 names them.
  - **Four pages have a few fewer words inside the page, and the layout the rules change is not what loses them first.**
    - `govdocs1-000458` (16 words, the section rule): a 760 px row of fixed cells (176, 397 and 187 px) in a centered table spills past both edges before and after. With the sections gone from the 187 px sidebar, its runs of no-break spaces widen that column by about 11 px, and the table moves 4 pt left. 16 words of the left column then cross the left edge (`January` at x = 3.25 pt before, -0.85 pt after). With the no-break spaces written as spaces, the move is 0.75 pt.
    - `govdocs1-001412` (2 words) and `-001419` (1 word), the section rule: a sidebar of divisions with ids changes width by a few points. On `-001419` one word ends at 598.4 pt instead of 586.4 pt, and on `-001412` two menu words of the left column are cut.
    - `govdocs1-002120` (3 words, the width rule): 13 words were past the right edge and are on the page now, and the end of the article (`lead successful lives`, the print link, 17 words) is cut at the foot of the page, as the rest of the article already was.
    - Keeping the `id` of a section in a cell of fixed pixel width restores `-000458`. It also gives back 44 words on `-000199`, `-001527` and `-002024` and loses one more on `-000413`, so the section rule is not narrowed. The three pages share no other structure in their markup.
  - **`govdocs1-000452` has 396 words past the page edge, against 1 before, and no fewer words on the page (985 words not inside the page before, 984 after).** Its article sits in a row of a 750 px table whose other rows hold 730 px tables. The suite places that column past the right edge whatever the widths are. Before, the column was not drawn (pages 2 to 8 are blank). After, with the inner tables at 718 px, it is drawn one letter per line at x = 595.3 pt, so each letter counts as a word past the edge. Dropping the 750 px and 730 px widths, or the inner tables themselves, still leaves the article on no page (LibreOffice run on the staged HTML, 24.2.7). The width rule is not what loses this article.
  - **23 pages that were complete change layout.** A `nowrap` cell before a table is wrapped, a section is split across a page, a table wider than 718 px is 718 px: rows move to another page. Of the 130 PDFs whose text changes, 26 have another page count, by one to sixteen pages (the extremes are the pages that were cut off). Text, links and pictures are the same.
  - **`govdocs1-001429` is not measured.** The page (211,000 lines of HTML) converts to 625 pages with every word in some runs and to 284 pages with 127,000 letters missing in others, from the same staged HTML (the staged HTML is byte-identical in all of them; repeated runs one after the other): the suite's layout of a table that size is not deterministic, so it is left out of the counts above; before the change it was 3 pages with 579,000 letters missing.
  - **Ids named by a style sheet keep the cut-off.** `#menu { ... }` is matched by the suite by `id` only, so the `id` stays; a page whose division with a named id holds the rows still loses what is past the page break. The alternative (a class and a rewritten rule) is not done.
  - **The width is a constant of the page the suite sets** (A4, 2 cm left margin); a page size or margin other than the HTML import's would move it. The conversion has no page-size option for HTML.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Clamping a table to the text area or to `100%`**: 9 pages fewer with a word outside the page (19 against 28 among the 168 pages whose staged HTML changes) but 58 pages that were complete changed instead of 37; it squeezed `govdocs1-000451` to a narrow column (6 to 13 pages) and `govdocs1-002120` lost 447 more words.
  - **Clamping a table in a cell to `100%` and the others to 718**: 23 pages with a word outside the page, but 5 pages worse (`govdocs1-002120` with 447 more missing words, `-001705` and `-001726` with 28 more words outside the page, and two of the three pages listed above).
  - **Removing every `nowrap`**: `nowrap` in a cell with text, a date or a number is what keeps it on one line in the source; only the cell before a table lets a long line widen the table.
  - **Dropping every `id`**: the suite gives the start of a division no link target, so the anchor of entry 55 would be the only one, but a style sheet that names the `id` loses its rule; the ids outside tables are harmless and stay.
  - **Writing `overflow-wrap` or `word-break`**: the HTML import does not read them.
  - **Breaking long strings with soft hyphens or zero-width spaces**: it changes the text of the page (copy and search), and the loss is in the layout of the table, not in a long word (the longest word of `govdocs1-001742` is 40 characters).
- **Verified by**: `tests/html-pdf-table-overflow.test.ts` (red before the change, 12 of 13 tests; the 13th pins that a division's `id` is still a link target): six synthetic pages (a `nowrap` cell before a table, two `nowrap` cells with percentage widths, a table of 800 px, a table of 750 px in a cell, and a `div` and a `center` with an `id` in a row above 120 rows), each converted by the office suite with parse5 reading the page and `pdftotext -bbox` reading the PDF (no word outside the page box, no letter or digit of the page missing); the staged HTML for each rule (which cells keep `nowrap`, which widths change, which ids stay, which anchors stay); and three real pages of the corpus (`govdocs1-001742`, `-000209`, `-001413`) with the same oracles. For the floating tables, red before the change: the staged widths of floating tables (765 px kept, 794 px and above written at 794 px, a centered one at 718 px), and `govdocs1-001401`, whose PDF must hold the last line of its article and no word outside the page. A floating table of 900 px keeps every word on the page. The corpus run and the parity run above were made on the VM (Ubuntu 24.04, LibreOffice 24.2.7, Poppler 24.02).
- **Revisit when**: a page of the corpus still loses words to a floated table or to fixed cell widths that add up beyond the page (`govdocs1-000458`, whose centered row of fixed cells spills past both edges), a table of 719 px or more is judged better left as written, the suite's HTML import limits a pixel width itself, or the conversion takes a page size for HTML.

## 70. PDF text of composite fonts without a ToUnicode map: the predefined CJK CMaps, the cmap of the embedded TrueType program and the glyph order of Arial and Times New Roman

- **Status**: Implemented (PDF text row of issue #783, "composite fonts without a ToUnicode map"; epic #760), decided 2026-10-23 (QA request); the glyph order of Arial and Times New Roman added 2026-10-23 (QA decision). It extends the reading order of entry 49.
- **Decision**: the in-process text reader (Markdown, HTML, the Office targets, hOCR and ALTO, and text when Poppler is not used) reads the text of a glyph of a composite (Type0) font in the order of ISO 32000-1 section 9.10.2, and then from the font program:
  - **First the font's ToUnicode map.** Unchanged.
  - **Then the predefined CMaps.** A font whose encoding is a predefined CMap (`UniJIS-UCS2-H`, `GBK-EUC-H` and the others of Table 118), or whose descendant uses the Adobe-GB1, -CNS1, -Japan1 or -Korea1 collection, is read through that CMap and the collection's `-UCS2` map. The reader now loads these maps (the `cmaps` folder of pdfjs-dist, as packed binary CMaps); before, it could not load any, and such text was not read at all.
  - **Then, for an Identity-H or Identity-V font of TrueType outlines (CIDFontType2) with neither, the embedded program's own Unicode `cmap`.** The CID leads to a glyph through the font's `/CIDToGIDMap` (Identity when absent), and the glyph's text is the lowest code point the program's cmap maps to it, from a Unicode subtable (platform 0, or platform 3 with encoding 1 or 10) in format 12, 4 or 6 (OpenType `cmap`). A control code, a private-use character or a noncharacter is no text. A CID that already is the code point the cmap maps to its glyph keeps that reading, so text that read right before reads the same.
  - **Then, for an Identity font whose program names no glyph, the glyph order of the Arial or Times New Roman font its name and widths show it to be.** Print-to-PDF distillers wrote Arial and Times New Roman as composite fonts whose CIDs are the glyph ids of the original font and whose program keeps no character names: a TrueType program without `cmap` and `post` (CIDFontType2, Identity `/CIDToGIDMap`), or a CID-keyed CFF program (CIDFontType0, `/FontFile3` of subtype `CIDFontType0C`). For such a font, with no ToUnicode map and no registered collection:
    - **The name.** The descendant's `/BaseFont`, without its subset tag (six capital letters and `+`, ISO 32000-1 section 9.6.4), with `,` and `_` read as `-` and white space dropped (pdfjs `normalizeFontName`), must be one of the Arial or Times New Roman names that pdfjs reads as Helvetica or Times (`getStdFontMap`): `Arial`, `Arial,Bold`, `ArialMT`, `Arial-BoldMT`, `TimesNewRoman,Italic`, `TimesNewRomanPSMT` and the others of that table, 26 in all. Arial Narrow, Arial Black and Arial Unicode MS (other widths or another glyph order) and Courier New (every glyph is one width, so widths prove nothing) are not. A name longer than 127 bytes (Annex C) names no font.
    - **The order.** Glyph id *n* is the *n*-th of the 258 standard Macintosh glyph names (TrueType `post` format 1; pdfjs `MacStandardGlyphOrdering`), read through the Adobe Glyph List as pdfjs's `getGlyphMapForMacOrderedFonts` reads it, for glyph ids 3 to 171 without 155: space to ellipsis, the Latin-1 letters of the Macintosh character set and its symbols (µ, ±, ≤, ∑, Ω and the others). Ids 0 to 2 carry no text; Arial and Times New Roman leave `nonbreakingspace` out of their order at 172 and order the rest their own way from there, and keep an unmapped `pi1` at 155.
    - **The widths decide.** The font is read this way only when /W gives every CID from 3 to 171 that it lists the width of that glyph in the reference font, to within 1 thousandth of an em, and lists at least one; one width off, or one CID without a reference width, and the font reads as before. The reference for Arial is the Liberation Sans font that pdfjs-dist ships and draws Arial with (regular, bold, italic, bold italic; metric-compatible with Arial), read from the package at run time; for Times New Roman it is the Times widths pdfjs keeps (`getMetrics`), which have no width for ∞, ∏, ∫, Ω and ≈. Only the CIDs /W lists get text; a CID it does not list (pdfjs gives it `/DW`) or one past 171 keeps the reading it had.
    - **Bounds**, against pdfjs's: the table is 169 of the 258 names, so a font gets at most 168 entries; /W is read by pdfjs's rules (a start that is not an integer ends it, a width that is not a number is skipped, a later entry wins) but only for CIDs 3 to 171, so a range is filled in one step where pdfjs walks every CID it spans (`0 4294967295 556` costs nothing more than `3 171 556`); a nested array of widths is read only for those CIDs, where pdfjs walks the whole array for every pair that names it (one 100,000-entry array named 1,000 times costs pdfjs 100 million steps, here a pair costs at most 170); an array is read once however many fonts share the /W; the /W arrays of one document spend at most 1,048,576 steps in all (a pair of /W and a width of a nested array in the span; the largest document of the real-world corpus spends 1,655 on all its fonts and 148 on the fonts the pass reads, and a /W listing each of the 65,536 CIDs as a pair of its own about 65,700), and once they are spent no later font gets a width, so its fonts read as before; a CFF program is not decoded at all; the reference fonts are read once per process; and the 64 fonts, the 64 MiB of programs and the 262,144 mapped CIDs below hold as before, each TrueType program read once with its `cmap`.
  - **Then the glyph names of entry 49, and what no step maps is left out**, as before.
  - **The map reaches the reader as the standard defines it.** The copy of the file that the text thread reads gets an incremental update (section 7.5.6): a ToUnicode stream for each such font, the font dictionary again under its own object and generation number with that stream named, a cross-reference section for those objects and a trailer whose `/Prev` is the file's last section. A file with no such font, or whose fonts give nothing, is passed on as the same bytes; the file a PDF output returns is never this copy.
  - **Bounds.** At most 64 such fonts per document are read, 64 MiB of decoded font programs and `/CIDToGIDMap` streams, and 262,144 mapped CIDs. Every table offset, count and length of the program is checked against the bytes before it is followed; encoding records that do not fit the `cmap` table, format 4 segments or format 12 groups out of order or overlapping (the specification forbids both) make the subtable unreadable. A subtable is checked whole (arrays inside the table, segments or groups in order, every glyph array a format 4 segment names inside the table) before a mapping is allocated, so an unreadable subtable costs one pass over its headers and no 384 KB mapping; and at most one subtable of each format is tried (the first the records name, formats 12, 4, 6 in that order), so a `cmap` of 65,535 records that all name one unreadable subtable costs one pass over the records and three over subtable headers (8.5 to 9.6 s per font before, 4 ms after). Each `/FontFile2` and `/CIDToGIDMap` stream is decoded and its `cmap` read once per document however many fonts name it (64 fonts sharing one program: 16 s for two fonts before, 3 ms for 64 after), and its bytes are charged to the 64 MiB once. A stream that inflates past the per-stream cap or what is left of the 64 MiB ends the font pass for the document and the file is passed on as it is (64 fonts sharing a program that inflates to 70 MiB: 1,191 ms and 491 MB resident before, 21 ms after): the inflate stops at the cap, so what it cost is the cap, and counting that against the budget instead would leave some fonts read and some not by the order the page names them; a limit met is met once, for the document, as the stream guard does. A font that does not read in any way keeps the reading it had. A font with two distinct subtables of one format of which only the second reads loses that reading (one subtable per format); in the 1,261 real-world PDFs and 60 of the regression corpus (the 16 above and 44 others with composite fonts) the update appended to the file is the same bytes as before the bound, so no file there does.
  - **Not read**: `post` glyph names and CFF charset names (no glyph in either corpus below needed them), symbol cmaps (platform 3, encoding 0), composite fonts with an embedded CMap stream as encoding, CFF-based composite fonts (CIDFontType0) other than the Arial and Times New Roman ones above, and fonts not embedded (pdfjs reads Arial and Times New Roman written as CIDFontType2 without a program through its own table for them).
- **Why**:
  - **Where the 11% of entry 49 comes from, and why the font program does not read it.** The real-world corpus (1,261 PDFs) has 265 composite fonts without a ToUnicode map in 63 files, drawing 19,509 glyphs (counted from the content streams by an independent reader). All of them were written by a print-to-PDF distiller of 2002 to 2008: its 181 TrueType programs keep only the outlines (no `cmap`, no `post`), its 80 CFF programs are CID-keyed with the Adobe-Identity collection (no glyph names), and 4 fonts are not embedded. The program therefore gives none of these glyphs a text: 5,764 of them are the glyphs entry 49 leaves out (control codes and private-use characters are what the CID itself gives), and the other 13,745 come out as the wrong letters (a CID of such a font is the glyph index of the original font, so "Report" reads "5HSRUW"; Poppler prints the same). This change leaves them as they were.
  - **What the font program does read.** In a sample of the 9,500 PDFs of the regression corpus (1,500 drawn at random, and the 88 written by CAD plot drivers or one PDF library), 24 fonts in 16 files keep a `cmap`: 6,869 glyphs that read as the CID read as text now (1,397 of them were left out, 5,472 were wrong letters). They are CAD plots (`pdfplot` 10 to 14, collection `PDFAUTOCAD-Indentity0`) and documents of one PDF library (collection `PDFXC-Indentity0`): "SCHEMAT ZAGOSPODAROWANIA DZIAŁKI" read "6&+(0$7=$*2632'$52:$1,$'=,$à.,". Poppler leaves every glyph of such an unknown collection out.
  - **The predefined CMaps were a gap of their own.** Without its CMap files pdfjs cannot read a font with a predefined CMap at all: a Japanese filing set in `UniJIS-UCS2-H` fonts (`cc-6800345.pdf`) gave no text, the text route fell back to OCR (16 s, Latin letters for Japanese), and other documents lost the body set in such a font and kept only their Latin headers. The 1,500-file sample has 143 fonts with a registered collection (210,583 glyphs).
  - **What the glyph order reads, and why it stops at 171.** Of the 19,509 glyphs above, 16,026 are in Arial and Times New Roman fonts. Their /W widths equal the advance widths of the installed Arial and Times New Roman at glyph id = CID for all 16,026, so the CIDs are glyph ids of those fonts. Read through the installed font's own `cmap` at glyph id = CID (the oracle), and compared glyph id by glyph id from 3 to 257: the installed fonts follow the Macintosh order up to 171 (155 aside) and differ from it from 172 on, where they leave out `nonbreakingspace` and every later glyph moves (`quotedblleft` at 179, where the Macintosh order has `emdash`). pdfjs's own map for standard fonts written as CID fonts without a mapping (`getGlyphMapForStandardFonts`) agrees: the Macintosh characters up to 171, nothing at 155, the installed fonts' characters at 179 to 182 and later. Up to 171 two independent sources and the oracle agree; past it they do not, and widths cannot tell some of those glyphs apart (‘ and ’ are both 222 wide in Arial), so the order stops there.
  - **Why the widths are checked against the font pdfjs draws Arial with.** The Helvetica widths pdfjs keeps differ from Arial at five glyphs of the table (±, µ, ∂, ∑, √): checked against them, the 15 Arial fonts of `govdocs1-003205.pdf` that show "µg" (6,706 glyphs) would read as before. Liberation Sans has Arial's widths: at most 0.18 thousandth of an em from /W at the 4,411 drawn CIDs of the table in both corpora, and equal to the installed Arial's advance widths for glyph ids 3 to 171. No serif font with Times New Roman's widths ships with pdfjs-dist, so the Times widths pdfjs keeps are the reference; they equal /W at the 25 drawn CIDs of the table in both corpora and differ from Times New Roman at ±, µ, ∂, ∑ and √, so a font that uses one of those reads as before. The tolerance is that of two roundings of one design width to whole thousandths (0.5 each).
  - **Readers that go past the standard do it this way.** ISO 32000-1 section 9.10.2 ends with the predefined CMaps ("there is no way to determine what the character code represents"). One open-source Java PDF library reads the embedded TrueType `cmap` of a CIDFontType2 font after the ToUnicode map and the `-UCS2` map, in this order; Poppler and MuPDF give the CID itself (Adobe-Identity), nothing or U+FFFD; pdfjs gives the CID. Reading the program's own map is the one source left that the file itself holds.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **A font whose cmap names the wrong character for a glyph gives that wrong character**, as a wrong ToUnicode map would. The CID-is-the-code-point rule keeps the old reading where the two agree.
  - **The glyph order is a guess from a name, checked by widths only.** A font named Arial whose CIDs are not Arial glyph ids but whose /W happens to give every listed CID of the table its Arial width would read as wrong letters. In both corpora no font named Arial or Times New Roman lists a table CID with another width, and every glyph the order reads matches the oracle (below), so the check has not had to refuse a font there; it is the hostile tests that show it refuses one.
  - **Glyphs past 171 keep their wrong letters**: in the real-world corpus 341 glyphs in 8 Arial and Times New Roman fonts that use only such ids (and 229 in 10 fonts of the regression sample). Smart quotes, dashes and accented capitals of these fonts are there; reading them needs the order of those fonts past 171, which no source above gives whole.
  - **Fonts whose dictionary sits in an object stream behind white space are not reached** by the font pass, by any of its steps: `cc-6400269.pdf` has two Times New Roman fonts whose widths pass (2,669 glyphs of the table) and reads as before. This is a limit of the pass as entry 70 first built it, recorded as a follow-up of #783.
  - **Reading the glyph order costs the time of the pass and of a longer copy**: on the Linux test VM, 0.1 to 1.9 ms a document for the font pass before and after, 1.0 to 2.0 ms on `govdocs1-003205.pdf` (64 fonts, a 67 KB update); the whole read of that file takes 298 ms from the file and 305 ms from the updated copy before the change and 308 ms after (median of 21 interleaved runs, ranges 251 to 390 ms overlapping), so the cost is pdfjs reading the update and the text it now has; 15 other documents read in 0.975 to 1.034 times the time (median of 7 interleaved runs each, no consistent direction).
  - **The text thread reads a longer copy**: 1.1 to 9.5 KB more on the 16 files above. Reading the fonts costs 0.3 to 1.7 ms a document there (0.1 ms on others), against 10 ms to 1.3 s for the whole read; on the Linux test VM the 16 documents read in 1.007 times the time (median of the ratios, 7 interleaved runs each, ranges overlapping) and 20 other documents in 1.006 times. Reading a document with a predefined CMap now costs what reading its text costs (250 ms for the Japanese filing, which read nothing in 30 ms before).
  - **Text through Poppler (`pdftotext`) is unchanged**: it still leaves out the glyphs of an unknown collection and prints the CID of an Adobe-Identity font.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Mapping the text pdfjs returns back to CIDs**: pdfjs inserts spaces, folds compatibility characters and reorders right-to-left runs, so CID 32 and an inserted space, or a CID in the Hebrew range, cannot be told apart afterwards.
  - **Writing a whole new file** (qpdf or pdf-lib): a full rewrite of every such document where an update of a few kilobytes does, and a second program between the upload and the reader.
  - **Reading `post` and CFF charset names**: no composite font without a ToUnicode map in either corpus has them. Its TrueType programs carry a `post` table of format 3 (no names) or none, and its CFF programs are all CID-keyed.
  - **The Macintosh order through glyph id 257**: from 172 on it gives Arial and Times New Roman glyphs the wrong characters, and where the two glyphs are the same width the widths cannot catch it (‘ for ’ at 182 and “ for ” at 180 in Arial).
  - **pdfjs's whole map for standard fonts (`getGlyphMapForStandardFonts`, 496 glyph ids)**: past 257 it follows one version of the fonts (Cyrillic at 570 to 633, where the installed Arial has other glyphs at 493 and 494), its entries from 172 on leave gaps (172 to 178, 183 to 199), and the references have no widths for most of them to check.
  - **The Helvetica widths pdfjs keeps as the reference for Arial**: they refuse the Arial fonts that use µ, ±, ∂, ∑ or √ (15 fonts, 6,706 glyphs of `govdocs1-003205.pdf`).
  - **The fonts installed on the host, or a table derived from Arial or Liberation Sans in the repository**: the worker would depend on fonts the image does not hold, and Arial's licence and Liberation's (GPL with a font exception) do not allow copying their data here; the Liberation file is read where pdfjs-dist ships it.
  - **The Foxit Serif font pdfjs-dist ships for Times**: a bare CFF program that would need a CFF reader for its widths, which equal the Times widths pdfjs keeps at every glyph of the table where both have one.
  - **The name alone, or a minimum number of glyphs**: a name is a guess, and a count is a threshold no source gives; every listed width must agree instead.
  - **Taking the highest code point, or the first subtable of the font**: the lowest code point is the one the Java library takes (it sorts the codes of a glyph and takes the first: U+0020 over U+00A0, U+002D over U+00AD), and the format 12 subtable is a superset of format 4 by the specification.
- **Verified by**: `tests/pdf-text-font-program-tounicode.test.ts` (PDFs written by hand around the DejaVu Sans subset that fontTools wrote, with the glyph indexes fontTools reads; 4 of the cases red before the change: Identity, a `/CIDToGIDMap` stream, Markdown, and Adobe-Japan1 over a program that says otherwise; also the same file rewritten by qpdf into object streams and a cross-reference stream, and linearized, a `startxref` that points nowhere, a program without `cmap`, a `cmap` that claims 65,535 records, and the bytes handed to the reader: the same buffer when nothing is added; and 64 fonts that share one program: a `cmap` of 65,535 records that name one unreadable subtable, the same with a readable one behind them, a program of 40 MiB that the 64 MiB budget allows once, and a program that inflates to 70 MiB, which must cost no more for 64 fonts than for one; red before the bounds: 16 s for two fonts, and 1.2 s against 22 ms); `tests/truetype-cmap.test.ts` (formats 4, 6 and 12, the lowest code point, controls and private use, and hostile tables: cut bytes, segments out of order, 65,535 records, a subtable offset out of the table, tables past the end, overlapping tables, a format 12 group over every code point, overlapping groups, and 65,535 records that name one unreadable format 4 subtable, with and without a readable one behind them, which read in 4 ms where they took 8.5 s; a glyph array inside the table reads and one past its end does not); `tests/pdf-text-real-samples.test.ts` on `cc-6200417.pdf` (strings read off the drawn page, since Poppler leaves them out) and `cc-6800345.pdf` (character F1 0.979 against `pdftotext`, 0.95 required), both red before the change. Mutations (the CID-is-the-code-point rule, the registered collection rule, the segment order check, the record bound) each fail a test. Markdown of every PDF before and after the change: the 1,261 PDFs of the real-world corpus read the same (pictures aside: whether a picture is carried varies from run to run before the change too, 21 files); of 625 files of the regression corpus (every file of the sample with a CID font, and 148 more), 44 changed: the 16 files whose fonts the program reads, 27 whose fonts need a predefined CMap or a registered collection's `-UCS2` map (body text that was missing, or the OCR of Japanese pages, replaced by the file's text), and one that hit the 60 s text limit once while the machine was loaded and reads the same before and after on three runs. The document parity rows `pdf-text->txt` (CER 0, word F1 1) and `pdf-structure->docx` give the same values before and after.
  - **The glyph order of Arial and Times New Roman** (2026-10-23): `tests/pdf-text-standard-glyph-order.test.ts` writes PDFs by hand whose /W widths are those the distillers wrote for the same glyphs in `govdocs1-003205.pdf` and `cc-6400269.pdf`: an Arial TrueType font without `cmap` and `post`, an Arial Bold font of CID-keyed CFF outlines with a subset tag, a Times New Roman font, and a /W that lists every CID past the table; all four red before the change, as are the real files `govdocs1-003205.pdf` (strings of page 20 as the drawn page shows them; Poppler prints ")LJXUH" for "Figure") and `cc-4000041.pdf` (strings of page 14 as drawn). It also reads the tables of the pdfjs-dist build the reader runs: every glyph id from 3 to 171 but 155 gets the character of `MacStandardGlyphOrdering` through the glyph list at the Times widths of `getMetrics`, the order agrees with `getGlyphMapForStandardFonts` up to 171 (canonically) and not at 179, and every Arial and Times New Roman name of `getStdFontMap` (Narrow, Black and Unicode MS aside) names the same standard font. Fonts read as before: a font named Arial with one /W width off (µ at 556), with `0 65535 556`, with no CID of the table in /W, a `/CIDToGIDMap` stream, a program with glyph names, a registered collection, and the names Wingdings-Regular, ArialNarrow-Bold, Arial-Black, CourierNewPSMT, a subset tag of small letters or of five letters. Cost: a /W range over 2^32 CIDs (0 ms), 65,536 CIDs listed one by one (12 ms), and 200,000 ranges over every CID (2.4 MB of /W, 77 ms against 62 ms before the change, which reads the same array). Hostile /W (review finding, `tests/pdf-text-cid-widths-bounds.test.ts` and `tests/pdf-cid-widths.test.ts`): one 100,000-entry array named 1,000 times from CID -1,000,000,000 by the 64 fonts of a document took 15.1 s before the bound and 19 ms after (the font still reads, since its listed widths come first); a 1,000-entry array named 100,000 times from CID 3 took 3.0 s and read the fonts, and now leaves them as they were within 29 ms. Mutations (no width check, a looser subset tag, `post` names ignored, the `/CIDToGIDMap` ignored) each fail a test. `tests/truetype-cmap.test.ts` adds the advance widths fontTools reads, a glyph past the last long metric, an em of no units, an `hmtx` shorter than `hhea` names, and a table directory that claims 65,535 records. Corpus, against the oracle (the installed font's `cmap` at glyph id = CID) and the bytes the reader is handed before and after: the 1,261 real-world PDFs are handed the same bytes except `govdocs1-003205.pdf` once qpdf has decrypted it (its open password is empty), whose 64 Arial fonts now read 14,514 glyphs, every one the oracle's character; of the 19,509 glyphs without text before, the rest are 3,483 in fonts of other names (Symbol, Wingdings, Monotype Sorts and others), 1,171 in four Times New Roman fonts without a program that pdfjs already reads, and 341 past glyph id 171. Of the 625 regression files, 623 are handed the same bytes; `cc-4000041.pdf` (three Arial fonts) and `cc-0200785.pdf` (two Times New Roman fonts) read 7,463 more glyphs, every one the oracle's character. No font named Arial or Times New Roman in either corpus lists a table CID with another width. The document parity family gives the same 27 rows before and after.
- **Revisit when**: a source gives the order of Arial and Times New Roman past glyph id 171; a font is found that the widths let through with wrong letters; pdfjs-dist ships a serif font with Times New Roman's widths; the font pass reaches dictionaries behind white space in object streams; a corpus shows composite fonts with glyph names but no `cmap`, or CFF-based ones with a name-keyed charset; the text route through Poppler is to read these fonts too; or pdfjs exposes the font program.

## 71. Legacy Excel files: both stream names, workbooks without cell values, string formula results and BIFF2 cell records, and the damaged files that stay refused

- **Status**: Implemented (this change). The reader changes in four places (below). The three real files whose compound file is destroyed stay refused and are listed as explained refusals (`bench/realworld/known-refusals.json`, issue #783).
- **Decision**: the XLS reader finds the workbook stream under either name, converts a workbook whose sheets hold no cell value to the empty output, reads two kinds of cell it dropped, and still refuses a stream that is not a whole workbook:
  - **The stream is found under either name**, case-insensitively: `Workbook` (BIFF8) or `Book` (BIFF5/7, Excel 5.0 and 95). When a file holds both, the `Workbook` stream wins, as in the readers below. BIFF5 text is read as 8-bit text without a flag byte. This is the behaviour since issue 668 (`selectWorkbookStream`, `tests/xls-biff8-corpus-regressions.test.ts`, "BIFF5 workbooks and files that hold both streams"); nothing here replaces it.
  - **A compound file with neither stream is refused** with `Corrupt XLS: Workbook stream not found in CFBF container`. `govdocs1-007750.xls`, `-007751.xls` and `-008063.xls` are such files: their compound file structure is destroyed, so no directory entry can be read. They are listed as explained refusals, and the nightly gate still counts them in the refusal rate.
  - **No repair is attempted.** The converter does not undo a text-mode transfer, rebuild a FAT, or guess the stream by scanning for a BIFF record.
  - **A workbook whose sheets hold no cell value converts to the empty output of each text target**: CSV and TSV are empty, JSON is `[]` (the text of `JSON.stringify([], null, 2)`), HTML is a page whose table has no row. This is what the XLSX source writes for a workbook without cells, and what xlrd's reading of it (every sheet 0 rows by 0 columns) gives. The reader refuses a BIFF stream with no cell only when it is not a whole workbook: it does not start with a BOF record, or a BOF is never closed by its EOF, or a record is cut short (`Corrupt XLS: The BIFF stream holds no cell records and is not a complete workbook ...`). Blank cells and cells whose text is empty are no value, as in xlrd without formatting.
  - **A formula whose cached result is a string is a cell.** The FORMULA record whose result bytes end in `0xFFFF` and start with `0x00` is followed by a STRING record with the text; the text is stored through the same path as every other cell, so it counts for the rows and the columns of the sheet. Only such a formula expects a STRING record. (Before, the text was stored without the cell being counted, so a sheet whose only cell was a string formula had no cell and was refused, and a string formula in the last column did not widen the sheet.)
  - **A BOF whose substream type no record names is a worksheet, and BIFF2 NUMBER (`0x0003`) and LABEL (`0x0004`) records are cells of a BIFF5-and-older sheet.** The types that are not worksheets stay as they were: `0x0006` (Visual Basic module), `0x0020` (chart), `0x0040` (macro sheet), `0x0100` (BIFF4 workspace), and the workbook globals `0x0005`. The BIFF2 NUMBER has three attribute bytes after the cell address and a double; the BIFF2 LABEL has three attribute bytes and a text with a one-byte length (8-bit text, read as Latin-1 like the BIFF5 text).
- **Why**:
  - **The reported refusal was not a missing `Book` stream.** The hypothesis (BIFF5 keeps its workbook in `Book`, which the reader did not look for) was tested first and is false: the reader has looked for both names since issue 668, and a synthetic BIFF5 compound file (written by the test helper `buildCompoundFile`) converts. The three files hold no stream under any name:
    - `govdocs1-007750.xls` (2,157,906 bytes) and `-007751.xls` (1,537,803 bytes): every LF byte of the file has a CR in front of it (68,946 and 1,803 pairs, no lone LF), which is what a text-mode transfer does to a binary file. Each inserted byte shifts the rest of the file, so the header's FAT sector numbers point into BIFF record data (sector 4046 of `-007750` holds BIFF record data) and the directory chain ends in garbage; the file is 338 bytes past a sector multiple. Taking the CRs out of the CR LF pairs gives 2,088,960 and 1,536,000 bytes, a multiple of 512 plus the header, a valid compound file that xlrd 2.0.2 opens (BIFF8 and BIFF7, 22,021 and 16,384 rows of 4 columns) and that this converter converts to the same cells: the CSV of both is byte for byte the CSV that xlrd's cells give (SHA-256 pinned in the test).
    - `govdocs1-008063.xls` (1,572,864 bytes, exactly 1.5 MiB): the header lists 63 FAT sectors from sector 7953 on, and the file holds 3,071 sectors, so none of them is there. xlrd stops with "File is truncated, or OLE2 MSAT is corrupt".
  - **The readers that exist refuse these bytes too, and the one that opens them reads garbage.** xlrd 2.0.2 raises on all three (`IndexError`, `AssertionError`, `IndexError`). The office suite (LibreOffice 26.8, run on the three files on the development Mac) does not read them as spreadsheets: it falls back to its text import and writes the compound file's own bytes as CSV (`\xd0\xcf\x11\xe0...` in the first cell; 24 MB, 3 MB and 5.5 MB), which is not a conversion of the workbook. Handing the files to it when the reader refuses would turn a clear refusal into an output that looks successful and is wrong.
  - **What the established readers do about stream names** (read for this change; no code copied):

    | Source | What it does | Use here |
    |---|---|---|
    | OpenOffice "Excel File Format" 1.42, section 2.3.2 (https://www.openoffice.org/sc/excelfileformat.pdf) | Table of the streams of a compound document: `Book` is the BIFF5 workbook stream, `Workbook` the BIFF8 one; a BIFF5-BIFF8 workbook stored as a bare stream file is the single `Book` or `Workbook` stream (2.3.1). BOF version `0500H` is BIFF5, `0600H` BIFF8 | The two names and the version words; the reader already takes both |
    | [MS-XLS] 2.1.7.20 Workbook Stream (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/openspecs/office_file_formats/ms-xls/f682f4b0-8c6b-444e-83f8-52d156f1e8ba) | The BIFF8 file has exactly one stream named `Workbook`; the specification covers BIFF8 only | `Book` is outside the specification, so it comes from the older documentation and the readers |
    | xlrd 2.0.2 (BSD), `xlrd/book.py` `biff2_8_load`, line 639 (https://github.com/python-excel/xlrd/blob/master/xlrd/book.py) | `for qname in ['Workbook', 'Book']`; no match: "Can't find workbook in OLE2 compound document". A file without the compound file signature is read as a bare BIFF stream (BIFF2 to BIFF4 files are not compound files). `getbof` (line 1272) takes the BIFF version from the BOF id and version word (`0x0600` is 80, `0x0500` is 50 or 70, by build year), and its text decoding uses the CODEPAGE record (`handle_codepage`, line 837) | Same lookup and the same order; same refusal for a missing stream |
    | SheetJS `xlsx.flow.js` (Apache-2.0), `parse_xlscfb`, line 19817 (https://github.com/SheetJS/sheetjs/blob/master/xlsx.flow.js) | `WB = CFB.find(cfb, '/Workbook') \|\| CFB.find(cfb, '/Book')` | Same lookup and order |
    | Apache POI HSSF, `HSSFWorkbook.getWorkbookDirEntryName`, line 334 (https://github.com/apache/poi/blob/trunk/poi/src/main/java/org/apache/poi/hssf/usermodel/HSSFWorkbook.java) | Refuses a `Book` directory entry with `OldExcelFormatException` ("POI only supports BIFF8"); `OldExcelExtractor` reads BIFF2 to BIFF5 text and also takes a `Workbook` entry | HSSF's refusal is a scope choice; the extractor shows both names occur |
    | The office suite's Excel import, `sc/source/filter/inc/xlconst.hxx` lines 82 and 83 (https://github.com/LibreOffice/core/blob/master/sc/source/filter/inc/xlconst.hxx) | Constants `EXC_STREAM_BOOK = "Book"` and `EXC_STREAM_WORKBOOK = "Workbook"` for the two streams | Same two names (read only, GPL-family source: behaviour, not code) |

  - **The same message covers the cases above and nothing legitimate.** A real workbook of any BIFF version has a stream named `Book` or `Workbook`, or is a bare BIFF stream (read by the second branch of `convertXlsSource`). The refusal is reached only by a compound file that holds neither, which is not a workbook as far as any reader can tell.
  - **The other 50 jobs of the nightly (`Corrupt XLS: No spreadsheet cell records found in BIFF stream`) are 33 files of the Apache POI test corpus** (none is a fuzzer case; they are real files of its bug reports), all read as BIFF with a `Workbook` or `Book` stream. They are three different gaps of the reader, each checked against the reference readers (xlrd 2.0.2 and LibreOffice 26.8 on the development Mac):
    - **31 are valid workbooks without a cell value.** xlrd reads every sheet of 29 of them as 0 rows by 0 columns; `office-spreadsheet-55668.xls` has one formula whose result is the empty string (xlrd: one text cell with no text) and `-57456.xls` has 1,046 MULBLANK records (xlrd 2.0.2 fails in its shared string table, `book.py` 1404; LibreOffice reads it as empty). LibreOffice writes a CSV of one line feed for every one of them. The records of all 31 end with an EOF for every substream; none is cut short.
    - **`office-spreadsheet-stringformulas.xls` holds one formula whose result is the string `XYZ`**: FORMULA (`0006`, result bytes `00 00 0c 00 9e 02 ff ff`) then STRING (`0207`, `XYZ`). xlrd and LibreOffice both read the cell. Followed: xlrd `sheet.py` 929-996 (a result with `FF FF` in bytes 6 and 7 is read by its first byte: `0` is a string held by the next record, `1` a boolean, `2` an error, `3` an empty string), and `string_record_contents` (`sheet.py` 1510-1535). The reader had stored the text of the STRING record without counting the cell in the sheet (`maxRow` and `maxCol`), so no sheet had a cell.
    - **`office-spreadsheet-64130.xls` is a `Book` stream written by an old Mac writer**: BOFs of version `0580H`, a worksheet BOF of type `8000H`, and BIFF2 NUMBER and LABEL records (`,Dawn,Daylight,Dusk,Dark` and `Percent,0.5,80.5,2.3,16.4`). xlrd refuses it (`getbof`, `book.py` 1272-1345: version `0580H` matches no BIFF version, and a BOF type that is not `0010H` is "BOF not workbook/worksheet"). LibreOffice reads it as BIFF5 (`xistream.cxx` `DetectBiffVersion`, version word masked with `FF00H`), takes a BOF sub type it does not know for a worksheet (`impop.cxx` `Bof5`, "tdf#144732 Excel interprets invalid indexes as worksheet") and reads the BIFF2 ids in the BIFF5 worksheet (`read.cxx` state `Z_Biff5T`; `impop.cxx` `ReadNumber`, `ReadLabel`, `ReadXFIndex` with three attribute bytes for the BIFF2 ids). The code follows the office suite's behaviour (its source is GPL-family: behaviour only, no code), and the output is its output.
  - **What the reference readers do about a stream that is not a whole workbook** (the refusals that stay): xlrd reads each sheet up to its EOF and fails when the stream ends first (`sheet.py` 1502 "missing EOF record"; `book.py` 676-683 `get_record_parts`) or when the first record is not a BOF (`book.py` 1280-1284 `getbof`). Bytes after the last EOF are not read, so the check treats them the same way.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - Two real government files that Excel's repair, or a person who undoes the transfer, could recover are refused. A guess at the repair (CR LF to LF) is right only when the file went through exactly one text-mode transfer and held no CR LF originally; `govdocs1-008063` shows that a file can also be cut, and then there is nothing to recover. Refusing costs the person the file; converting a wrong repair costs them cells they cannot tell are wrong.
  - The message names a missing stream, not a damaged compound file, so it does not say what is wrong with the file. A better message needs the compound file parser to tell a file with no stream apart from one whose sector chains it cannot follow; it is a separate change.
  - **BIFF5 8-bit text is decoded as Latin-1; the CODEPAGE record is not read.** xlrd decodes it with the code page of the record (`handle_codepage`, `xlrd/book.py` line 837), so a BIFF5 file in another code page than 1252 (Cyrillic, Greek, Mac Roman) would show different characters for bytes above 0x7F. Of the 9 BIFF5 `Book` files that convert among the 282 XLS files in the local corpus cache, 7 are code page 1252 and 2 are Mac Roman (10000, `govdocs1-003455` and `-006072`); `-003455` has no non-ASCII cell, and in `-006072` every non-ASCII cell xlrd reads is also in this converter's output (the only such character is `±`, which Mac Roman and Latin-1 place at the same byte), so no corpus file shows the gap. It is left as it is and named under "Revisit when". The office suite on the development Mac (26.8) cannot write a BIFF5 file (its "MS Excel 5.0/95" export fails to store), so no independent fixture can be made for a test either.
  - **An empty workbook is a zero-byte CSV and TSV.** The office suite writes one line feed for the same workbook (it keeps the formatted blank cells of its used area: `office-spreadsheet-37684-1.xls` gives 61 of them). The reader drops blank rows after the last one with text for every workbook (`sheetOfCells`), so an empty workbook is the end of that rule, and it is what the XLSX source and xlrd's cell count give. A client that treats an empty body as a failure sees one for a workbook that has no data.
  - **The string-formula fix changes one converting corpus file.** `govdocs1-003460.xls` has string formulas (a space) in columns 12 to 14 of one row of the sheet `compare`; xlrd reads that sheet 14 columns wide. The text outputs are laid over all sheets, so every row is now 14 cells wide (before: 11, and 14 on that one row). The values are the same (every row equals the old row once trailing empty cells are removed).
  - **BIFF2 INTEGER (`0x0002`) and BOOLERR (`0x0005`) are not read in a BIFF5 sheet**, although the office suite and xlrd (in a BIFF2 stream) read them. No file of the corpus holds them, and a record without a real or independent fixture is not added.
  - The three refusals stay in the refusal rate of their pair, so the gate does not hide them; they only stop being listed among the unexplained ones.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Reading the `Book` stream** (the hypothesis of this change): already done; there is nothing to add.
  - **Routing BIFF5 files to the office suite**: it reads BIFF5, but the in-process reader already reads BIFF5 records (the 8-bit text is decoded as Latin-1, see the trade-offs), at no process cost; and for the three files the suite writes the container's bytes as text.
  - **Repairing the files** (undo the CR LF pairs, scan the bytes for BOF records, rebuild a FAT from the sector contents): no established reader does it (xlrd, SheetJS, POI and the office suite all refuse or misread), the repair is a guess the output cannot show, and the converter would own every future damage pattern.
  - **Falling back to the office suite on this refusal**: the output is the file's bytes as CSV, which looks like success.
  - **Handing an empty workbook to the office suite**: it answers a line feed, and starts a process for a workbook with nothing in it; the in-process reader already knows the workbook is empty.
  - **A placeholder row for an empty workbook** (the ODS source writes `Data` and `Empty ODS content`): text that the file does not hold. Neither reference reader writes it.
  - **Treating every unfinished stream without a cell as empty**: a truncated file would be answered with nothing and look converted. xlrd refuses it, and so does the reader.
- **Verified by**: `tests/xls-damaged-real-files.test.ts`, red before the entries in `known-refusals.json` (the explained-refusal case failed; the other cases pin the evidence above and pass before and after, since the reader does not change what it does with these files): per file, the line-feed signature of a text-mode transfer (007750, 007751) or the FAT sector past the end of the file (008063); the refusal for csv; and for the two transferred files the CSV of the file with the inserted CRs removed against the SHA-256 of the cells xlrd 2.0.2 reads (`Workbook` stream and BIFF8 for 007750, `Book` stream and BIFF7 for 007751). The synthetic BIFF5 `Book` conversion is `tests/xls-biff8-corpus-regressions.test.ts`. The three files are the only ones the nightly refused for the missing stream. `tests/xls-biff-reader-gaps.test.ts` (red before the change, 99 of 102 tests; the 3 that passed are refusals): the 31 empty workbooks (CSV and TSV empty, JSON `[]`, an HTML table without a row, per file), the string formula of `office-spreadsheet-stringformulas.xls` (CSV `XYZ` and JSON, the cell xlrd reads), `office-spreadsheet-64130.xls` (the CSV and JSON of the cells LibreOffice writes), synthetic BIFF5 sheets with BIFF2 records in a worksheet and in a substream of type `8000H` (expected values from the record layouts), and the refusals that stay (no BOF at the start, a worksheet without its EOF, a record cut short; the contrast case is a whole workbook without cells). The corpus run converts the 282 cached XLS files to CSV, JSON and HTML before and after: 33 files change from refused to converted (99 jobs), `govdocs1-003460.xls` changes as described above, and the other 744 outputs are byte for byte the same (21 of them are the refusals that stay). The parity family `document` (which holds `doc-poi-12561-2.xls`, `doc-poi-19599-1.xls` and `doc-poi-29942.xls` to PDF) was run on the development Mac before and after (`--parity --quality-only --quick`, 92 rows evaluated and 0 failing, the 170 rows measured identical in ours; the `data` and `ebook` families need tools that were not installed there and are measured by CI).
- **Revisit when**: a corpus or user file with a `Book` stream and a CODEPAGE other than 1252 shows wrong characters (then the CODEPAGE record decodes the 8-bit text, as xlrd does, with a LibreOffice CSV of a BIFF5 file in that code page as the oracle); the compound file parser can tell a destroyed container from a missing stream (then the message should say which); or a reader appears that opens a text-mode-damaged workbook (an upstream repair mode), in which case the three files are re-measured. A real or independent BIFF5 file with a BIFF2 INTEGER or BOOLERR record, or a BIFF8 sheet that stores BIFF2 ids, shows up (then those records are read as the office suite reads them); a client needs the line feed the office suite writes for an empty workbook.

## 72. Text that is not UTF-8, and delimited records of another width than the header

- **Status**: Implemented (this change; #783). Replaces the strict UTF-8 rule of plain text and Markdown that entry 19 left in place, and the refusal of CSV and TSV records whose field count differs from the header's.
- **Decision**:
  - **Plain text and Markdown** (`decodeTextInput`, used by text and Markdown to PDF and by the route planner): a byte order mark names UTF-8 or UTF-16 and the bytes must then be valid in it (a file that breaks its own declaration is still refused). Without one, valid UTF-8 is UTF-8, as before. Anything else is legacy text: bytes that libmagic's `text_chars` table says never occur in text (C0 controls other than BEL to SI, SUB and ESC, and DEL) refuse the file as "not text"; otherwise the ICU character set detector, as the `chardet` package implements it, names the encoding over at most 64 KiB starting two bytes before the first non-ASCII byte; Windows-1252 is read when the sample holds fewer than four non-ASCII bytes (the rule `MIN_LEGACY_EVIDENCE_CHARS` already applies to CSV), when the best match is at ICU's no-evidence confidence of 10 or lower, when the label is one the Encoding Standard does not decode, or when the file is not valid in the detected encoding. Windows-1252 is decoded with the standard's table, so the five undefined bytes become their C1 code points. The conversion's warnings say `Text input is not valid UTF-8; it was read as <encoding>.`
  - **CSV and TSV**: a table is as wide as its widest record. A record with fewer fields is filled out with empty cells; one with more widens the table, the header gaining columns named `Unnamed: <index>` (the index of the column from 0; a name the header already has gets the usual `_1` suffix). Two warnings at most, each with a count and the first row (rows are numbered over every line, as in quote errors): `CSV: 2 rows have more fields than the header (the first is row 10); 1 column was added, named Unnamed: 8.` and `CSV: 32 rows have fewer fields than the widest row (the first is row 2); their missing cells are empty.` An unterminated quote or a stray quote is still refused, with its row. A table whose records all have the header's width is read and written exactly as before, with no warning, and without the limits below.
  - **Limits of a table that is padded or widened** (`assertRaggedTableFits`; they bound what this reading newly accepts, and each refusal is a `DataParseError` that names the row): at most 16,384 columns, at most 1,048,576 records (the lines that are not blank, the header included), no field longer than 131,072 characters, and a padded table of more than 1,000,000 cells must not be more than four times the cells the file holds. The refusal names the first widest record, the first record past the row limit or the first field past the length limit. The streamed conversion of the browser applies the column limit to a header it fills short records out to.
- **Practical basis** (sources read, not described from memory):
  - The HTML Standard, "determining the character encoding": when nothing declares the encoding, UTF-8 for bytes that match the UTF-8 pattern ("very likely to be UTF-8, while documents with byte sequences that do not match it are very likely not"), otherwise an implementation default that its locale table sets to windows-1252 for every locale not listed. Entry 19 applies this to HTML; plain text has no way to declare anything, so the same default is the right last step.
  - Firefox's detector, `chardetng` (`src/lib.rs`, Apache-2.0 or MIT): `feed` (lines 2938 to 2964) skips the leading ASCII and starts two bytes before the first non-ASCII byte, which is the sample window used here; for a generic top-level domain `encoding_for_tld` (line 2126) is the Western candidate, windows-1252, and the detector only moves off it on evidence.
  - The ICU detector (`icu4c/source/i18n/csrmbcs.cpp`, lines 181 to 195): a multi-byte candidate with ten or fewer multi-byte characters gets 10, "not incompatible with our encoding, so don't give it a zero", and 0 when there is no multi-byte sequence in a short text. A confidence of 10 is therefore the absence of evidence, not a weak match. The `chardet` npm package (MIT, no dependencies, 179 KB unpacked) is a TypeScript port of these recognisers (`lib/index.js` lists them).
  - libmagic's `file` (`src/encoding.c`): `file_encoding` tries ASCII, UTF-8, UCS-32/16, then "looks latin1" and "looks extended" (lines 107 to 150), each judged by the `text_chars` table (lines 246 to 265); a byte the table marks `F` is never text. It reads `FILE_ENCODING_MAX`, 64 KiB (`src/file.h` line 533).
  - LibreOffice's text import reads the bytes in the encoding it was told, a byte order mark only when the stream asks (`SvStream::StartReadingUnicodeText`, `tools/source/stream/stream.cxx` line 687; `sw/source/filter/ascii/parasc.cxx` lines 280 to 308), and refuses no byte.
  - RFC 4180 section 2 says each line "should contain the same number of fields", a recommendation. Python's `csv.reader` returns each record as it has fields, and `csv.DictReader` (`Lib/csv.py` lines 158 to 206) fills a short record with `restval` and keeps the surplus of a long one under `restkey`. pandas' tokenizer (`pandas/_libs/src/parser/tokenizer.cpp`, `end_line`) pads a short record with empty fields (lines 513 to 541) and, for a long one, raises unless `on_bad_lines` says to skip or warn (lines 485 to 509); a header cell without a name becomes `Unnamed: <index>` (`pandas/io/parsers/python_parser.py` line 649). LibreOffice Calc's text import (`sc/source/ui/docshell/impex.cxx`, `ScImportExport::ExtText2Doc`, lines 1711 to 1764) puts each line's fields in cells from the first column and widens the range to the widest line (`nEndCol`).
  - The limits of a table. Columns: Microsoft's "Excel specifications and limits" gives 1,048,576 rows by 16,384 columns for a worksheet, and LibreOffice Calc's `sc/inc/address.hxx` has `MAXROWCOUNT = 1024 * 1024` (line 60) and `MAXCOLCOUNT = 16 * 1024` (line 61). Its text import drops the records and fields past those bounds with a warning (`bOverflowRow`, `bOverflowCol`, `sc/source/ui/docshell/impex.cxx` lines 996 to 998 and 1629 to 1631), so a larger table is not one that application holds either; this reading refuses it and names the row instead of dropping data silently. Field length: Python's `csv` raises "field larger than field limit (131072)" (`Modules/_csv.c`, `parse_add_char` at line 710, default `128 * 1024` at line 1765); LibreOffice cuts a cell at `nArbitraryCellLengthLimit = SAL_MAX_UINT16` and warns (`impex.cxx` lines 86 and 673 to 681), and Excel holds 32,767 characters in a cell. The limit taken is Python's, the one that refuses and the larger of the two, so a field that Python reads is read. Padding: every record is padded to the widest one, so the cells made are width times records. A measured table of 20 million padded cells takes 1.4 to 1.7 GB of resident memory whatever its shape (about 75 bytes a cell, on 600 records of 16,384 fields, 5,000 of 4,096, 20,000 of 1,000 and 200,000 of 100), against the 1 GiB heap of a worker (`THREAD_HEAP_LIMIT_MB` in `src/lib/workers/cpu-pool.ts`), so the 20 million cells of `PARQUET_MAX_CELLS` and of the XLSX reader's `XLSX_MAX_MATERIALISED_CELLS` (whose padded rows share one frozen array) are too many for records that are objects. The free allowance is 1,000,000 cells, `PDF_TABLE_MAX_CELLS` (the most the table writer draws, about 75 MB here), and beyond it the table may be four times its cells; the YAML reader bounds its expansion the same way, as a multiple of the values written with a floor of its own (`YAML_EXPANSION_FACTOR` and `YAML_MIN_EXPANDED_VALUES` in `src/lib/conversions/data.ts`).
  - The text sample is bounded as it was: the detector reads 64 KiB (`FILE_ENCODING_MAX`) from two bytes before the first non-ASCII byte whatever the size of the file, and a text file of any size is scanned in chunks, so no limit on the size of a text is added (LibreOffice limits a line to `nArbitraryLineLengthLimit`, 2 x 16,384 x 65,535 characters, `impex.cxx` lines 80 to 87; Python's `bytes.decode` has no limit).
- **Why**: the real-file run of 2026-10-23 refused 57 jobs for these two causes (and 3 more for a third, below): six government text files (Windows-1252 curly quotes, a section sign, a registered mark) with "Text input is not valid UTF-8", and 22 CSV and TSV files (21 of the corpus' CSV files and one TSV) with "Too many fields" or "Too few fields". Every reader in use takes both: a file saved by an old word processor or exported from a spreadsheet with a ragged tail is a normal input, and the user wants the table and the text, with a note on what was assumed.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - The guess can be wrong, and a wrong guess now converts where it used to refuse. A five-syllable EUC-KR file (`한국어 문서`) is detected as ISO-8859-6 (confidence 75), whose table rejects its bytes, so it is read as Windows-1252 and shows `ÇÑ±¹¾î ¹®¼­`; a DOS text in code page 437 (`govdocs1-000226.txt`, `caf\x82 owner`) is read as Windows-1252 and shows `‚` where `é` belongs, because neither the detector nor libmagic knows code page 437. The warning names the encoding that was read, so the wrong guess is visible; a short sample (fewer than four non-ASCII bytes) is never guessed.
  - The sample is 64 KiB from the first non-ASCII byte. A file that changes encoding after that is read whole in the detected encoding, or in Windows-1252 when it is not valid there. Control bytes are looked for in the bytes up to the end of the sample, as libmagic does; a NUL after it passes.
  - A text that is valid UTF-8 is never inspected for control bytes, so a binary file made only of valid UTF-8 sequences converts as before.
  - The CSV reader's own encoding rule (BOM, `encoding` option, UTF-8, then its scored CJK candidates and Windows-1252, refusing a guess backed by fewer than four non-ASCII characters) is unchanged: it decides the output of files that already convert, and three corpus jobs (`govdocs1-006783.csv`) still stop at that rule. Two detectors now exist in the code base; folding the CSV one into `chardet` is a separate change with its own byte-for-byte check.
  - The browser's streamed CSV and TSV conversion (files over 100 MB) fills a short record with empty cells (under a header of at most 16,384 columns) but still refuses a longer one, because the header is already written; the pure in-browser path widens the table like the server and has the limits of the server. Neither reports a warning (they have no channel for one).
  - The delimiter is still detected from records of equal width, so a ragged file in which no candidate gives equal widths keeps the format's own delimiter (a semicolon file that is also ragged reads as one column).
  - A file whose records have the header's width has no limit on columns, rows, field length or cells, as before: those files convert today and a new limit would refuse some of them. Only a table that is padded or widened is bounded, so a ragged file with a field of more than 131,072 characters is refused where a uniform one is read.
  - A record longer than the header and shorter than the widest counts in both warnings; the counts say what each record is measured against.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Keeping the refusal and telling the user to re-save**: refuses what Python, pandas, LibreOffice and every browser open, and the real-file run proves the files exist.
  - **Windows-1252 for every non-UTF-8 text, with no detector** (entry 19's rule for HTML): Shift_JIS, GBK and EUC-KR text would turn into Latin mojibake where it used to be refused. The detector costs one small dependency and runs only for text that is not UTF-8.
  - **Reading the detector's top match whatever its confidence, or a guess from a few bytes**: a lone `é` is read as KOI8-R (confidence 60), a stray accent in a short note as Cyrillic.
  - **Rewriting the detector** (a port of chardetng's scoring, or the scored candidates of the CSV reader extended with more encodings): new statistics code to test and maintain where a permissively licensed package already implements the recognisers.
  - **Dropping long records (pandas `on_bad_lines="skip"`) or truncating them**: loses data silently. **Collecting the surplus under one key (`DictReader`'s `restkey`)**: a column whose cells are lists fits neither CSV nor a spreadsheet.
  - **Padding without a bound, or with the 20 million cells of Parquet**: an 80 KB file (20,000 records of two fields and one of 20,000) is 400 million cells and took a worker to its heap limit; 20 million cells are 1.5 GB of records.
  - **Dropping the records and fields past the limits, as the spreadsheet application does**: the table would be wrong without the user knowing where.
  - **Failing a conversion that has warnings**: the warning exists so the table can be used.
- **Verified by**: `tests/text-input-legacy-encodings.test.ts` and `tests/delimited-ragged-rows.test.ts`, both red before the change on the real files by name (`govdocs1-000081.txt`, `-000219`, `-000226`, `-000502`, `-001171`, `-001559`, the 21 CSV files and `tika-migration-overlay.tsv`). The texts equal Python 3.13 `bytes.decode('cp1252')` and `iconv -f CP1252 -t UTF-8` (digests in the test); each table equals Python's `csv.reader` padded to the widest record (digest of `json.dumps` of the records, `python3 -I`). Synthetic cases: Windows-1252 smart quotes and the euro sign, Latin-1 accents, the five undefined bytes, Shift_JIS, EUC-KR and GBK text (detected and read in their encoding), a PNG header and control bytes in Latin-1 text (refused), tab, form feed and escape (kept), too few non-ASCII bytes, short and long records, a TSV file, quoted fields with line breaks, a header that already has the generated name, and a table with no ragged record (no warning). `tests/delimited-ragged-limits.test.ts` (red before the bounds: the 80 KB file in a child with a 128 MiB heap ran out of memory, and is refused in 5 ms at 174 MiB of resident memory after; 16,384 columns read and 16,385 refused with the row, the first of several widest records, 1,048,576 records read and the next refused with its row, blank lines not counted, a field of 131,072 characters read and one more refused, in a quoted field with line breaks and in the header, 1,000,000 padded cells allowed whatever the width and a table of 1.8 million cells that grows by less than four times padded, the pure data engine, and a uniform table with 20,000 columns or a 200,000 character field left alone); `tests/text-input-legacy-encodings.test.ts` (the characters and the warning of a file scanned in chunks of 3 bytes to 1 MiB equal those of the whole read for Shift_JIS, EUC-KR, GBK, Windows-1252, Latin-1, UTF-8 with an invalid last byte, 85 KB of ASCII before the first non-ASCII byte and a control byte past the sample; a control byte before and inside the sample is refused with the offset of the whole read; a byte order mark that the bytes break is refused without trying another encoding; the detector is given 64 KiB of a file of 3 MB; a file of 64 MiB that is valid UTF-8 up to its last byte is scanned in a 64 MiB heap at 206 MiB of resident memory, where the whole read ran out of memory); `tests/edge-streaming-delimited.test.ts` (padding at every cut point against the server, a header of 16,384 and of 16,385 columns), `tests/pure-data-parity.test.ts`, `tests/data-csv-encoding.test.ts` and `tests/pdf-unicode-text-writers.test.ts` were updated where they pinned the old refusals. Real-file run, 1,008 jobs of the text, Markdown, CSV and TSV sources of the corpus, base and head with a fixed clock: 57 jobs went from refused to converted (28 files: 6 text to PDF, 9 CSV to PDF, 10 to YAML, 9 to JSON, 8 to TSV, 7 to HTML, 6 to Parquet, 1 TSV to CSV, 1 TSV to JSON), every one of them a valid output by the run's own checker; the 948 jobs that already converted give the same bytes (SHA-256); no job went from converted to refused; 3 jobs stay refused (the CSV encoding rule above). After the bounds and the chunked scan, the same 1,008 jobs were run again: the 948 that converted before give the same bytes and warnings as the base, the 57 still convert and give the same bytes as the run before the bounds, and the 3 stay refused, so no real file reaches a limit. The reference-parity quality rows of the `data` family (16 rows) and of the `document` family (92 rows evaluated, 104 measured), run on the base and on this change, have the same values.
- **Revisit when**: a user reports a file the detector misreads, which would justify an `encoding` option for text; the CSV reader's detector is to be replaced by the same package; the streaming path gains a way to report warnings or to widen a table already started; or a corpus holds a binary format whose first 64 KiB are text.

## 73. Imperfect PDFs the PDF readers open: bytes in front of the header, a /Length written after its stream, a wrong Flate checksum and an empty Flate stream

- **Status**: Implemented (this change). Four places change: the upload spoof check for `.pdf` (`isFormatCompatibleWithMagicBytes`, with `hasPdfHeader` and `pdfFromHeader` in `src/lib/format-signatures.ts`), what Ghostscript and LibreOffice are handed (`compress.ts`, `pdfa.ts`), the stream extent of the in-process PDF reader (`PdfDocument.applyLaterLengths`), and its Flate decoding (`inflatePdfFlate` in `src/lib/conversions/bounded-inflate.ts`, and an empty stream in `PdfDocument.decodeStream`). Real-world issue #783.
- **Decision**:
